# Description of the Front Cover

The front cover of the manuscript features a **marbled paper** design and a **red-brown leather spine**. A small, partially obscured library label is visible on the lower right edge of the spine, likely indicating its historical shelf placement.

### Physical Characteristics

*   **Binding**: <term>binding</term> <note>The material and construction used to hold the manuscript's pages together.</note>
*   **Marbled Paper**: <term>marbled paper</term> <note>A decorative technique where paper is dipped into a tray of water containing floating pigments to create stone-like or swirling patterns.</note>
*   **Leather Spine**: <term>leather spine</term> <note>The back portion of the book's binding, here made of leather to provide durability and a surface for titles.</note>
*   **Shelfmark**: <term>shelfmark</term> <note>The specific classification code (such as "A 7 35" seen on the previous page) used by a library to locate the item.</note>

# Prologue

## The musical discipline of Boethius.

<note>original: "Boetii musices disciplina." Boethius (c. 480–524 AD) was a Roman statesman and philosopher whose writings on music theory were the standard university textbooks for over a thousand years.</note>

Boethius's discipline of music, comprised of five volumes, rests upon the deepest foundations of arithmetic and philosophy; because of this, it cannot be understood by everyone everywhere. It is usually called sterile by the half-learned singers of our time—the more obscure it is, the less useful they find it. Yet, to the learned and those who look more deeply, the more subtle and probable it is, the more firm and better it appears. Thus, just as it has always been neglected by the unlearned, so it has always been and remains held in great prize among the more skillful. 

Therefore, we, who strive to be of use to all and to contribute something to the common good, have created this brief compendium divided into three small books. We have reduced its lengthiness into a narrow space, its harshness into a smooth path, and its obscurity into light. Omitting nothing that pertains to the art and its practice, we have constructed a very useful work for both singers, whom we call "practitioners," and observers, whom we call "theorists" <note>original: "practicos" and "theoricos" (from the Greek).</note>. From this work, once they have read and understood it, they will see that they shall gain much honor and pleasure. They will confess that, being wonderfully delighted by this new form of our most beautiful art, we have provided a great deal of help for the general education of everyone. 

From this source—as if from a kind of overflowing and public fountain—anyone may now draw, with very fast and easy study, whatever I have been able to collect over a long time through many sleepless nights and constant midnight labors from the reading of the most approved authors and the discipline of the most famous teachers. By this path, one may reach the highest peak of music with a most peaceful step. 

Let no one fear the majesty of philosophy, nor the complexity of arithmetic, nor the twists and turns of proportions. For here, anyone—even if they are unrefined in every way—provided they are willing to lend their hearing to the discipline and are not entirely devoid of reason, can emerge as an outstanding and most skillful musician. Indeed, we have striven so much to serve the understanding that we have maintained a balance of speech and a moderation of style so that the more skillful may be abundantly refreshed by reading these things, the half-learned may profit greatly, and the entirely unlearned may be educated in the gentlest manner. 

We do not undertake here to instruct only philosophers or mathematicians; anyone who has been taught only the first rudiments of grammar will understand these things of ours. Here the mouse and the elephant may swim together, and Daedalus and Icarus can fly together. <note>This is a classical metaphor: a truly great book is like a body of water where the "mouse" (the beginner) can wade safely, but the "elephant" (the expert) finds enough depth to swim. Similarly, it provides enough "air" for both the master (Daedalus) and the student (Icarus).</note> Furthermore, it will be the duty of the prudent and grateful reader to grant pardon if he does not find in this work of ours that haughtiness of speech found in Cicero or Sallust; if with fewer ornaments...

<insert>
<margin>NATIONAL LIBRARY FLORENCE</margin>
**Ramos de Pareja's Practical Music, and Minor Poetic Works Manuscript.**
**Designated as number 106 among the Codices.**
<note>This later handwritten note identifies the author as Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja (c. 1440–1522), a Spanish music theorist who famously challenged the traditional Boethian system in favor of more practical tuning and composition methods.</note>
</insert>

<summary>In this prologue, the author (Ramos de Pareja) introduces a three-volume compendium designed to make the complex, mathematical music theory of Boethius accessible to both practical singers and theorists. He emphasizes that while the traditional study of music is often considered "sterile" or too difficult, his work provides a clear and "gentle" path for students of all levels.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, Ramos de Pareja, music theory, arithmetic, philosophy, practical music, theorists, music education, Renaissance humanism, Badia Fiorentina</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the prologue. The text picks up from the previous page's final unfinished word "pertains," completing the thought that the author is using a simpler, perhaps less "adorned" Latin style to ensure clarity, following the example of the Roman poet Manilius.</meta>

<note>The top of the page shows signs of water damage and minor paper loss, but the Gothic/Blackletter print remains legible. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the author's introductory thought on his prose style.</note>

...pertains to the matter, even if our discourse proceeds with a less familiar style. For it is permitted for me to say about music what we read that Marcus Manilius said about astronomy: <note>original: "Ornari res ipsa negat contenta doceri." From Manilius's "Astronomica," meaning the subject is technical and requires clear explanation rather than poetic flowery language.</note> "The subject itself refuses to be adorned, content simply to be taught." 

Truly, toward this excellent part of philosophy—namely music—if anyone considers its force, its nature, its beauty, and its nobility, they will not be drawn by my embellishments, but will fly and follow, of their own accord and will, the adorable footsteps of Orpheus of Thrace, Amphion of Thebes, Arion of Lesbos, Mercury, Linus, Solomon, Pythagoras, Aristoxenus, Ptolemy, Chorebus, Lycaon, Theophrastus, Timothy, and others who achieved an immortal name through this discipline. 

<note>The author lists a "who's who" of ancient musical authority, blending Greek mythology (Orpheus, who could charm stones), biblical figures (Solomon), and historical theorists (Pythagoras and Ptolemy) to establish the prestige of music.</note>

These were the men whom venerable antiquity so admired that it said they moved wild beasts by the sweetness of song, possessed the hearts of men, recalled souls into bodies, bent the spirits of the dead to mercy, and drew hardy mountain-ashes from the mountains. Though these things may seem like fables exceeding belief, there is no doubt that music is the producer of marvelous works. It is well known that Saul, King of the Jerusalemites, when vexed by an evil spirit, was accustomed to be cured by the playing of the kithara <note>original: "cythare," a lyre-like stringed instrument.</note>. David used the song of the psaltery <note>original: "psalterij," a medieval string instrument often associated with the Psalms.</note> to prophesy, as if lifting his mind upon a certain vehicle. Elisha, the great disciple of the father of the Carmelites <note>The author identifies the prophet Elijah as the "father of the Carmelites," a common medieval tradition.</note>, called for a psalmist when he wished to prophesy. 

Who does not know that crying infants are calmed by the songs of nurses? And that the inflamed heat of the blood is extinguished by measures? Horses, at the sound of the trumpet, prick up their ears, their limbs tremble, they neigh loudly and leap, unable to stand still, thirsting for battle and war. Priscian <note>A famous 6th-century Latin grammarian.</note> is the authority that there is a fountain among the Sicilians that appears to dance at the sound of the lyre. 

Music undoubtedly possesses the greatest energy and immense authority over human souls, whether it wishes to soothe or to uplift. If in our own time so many miracles are not performed through music, it is not to be blamed on the art—which is perfectly written above nature—but on those who use the art poorly. For if those most proven musicians whom we mentioned above were brought back to life, they would deny that the music of our time was invented by them; to such an extent has it been rendered inept, unharmonious, and scattered by the depravity of certain singers. 

Moved by these reasons, we have published the present work, hoping that even if there are perhaps some now living who are goaded by envy to detract rather than to improve, there will be many among posterity who, with the poison of envy buried, will praise our labor and, driven by the love of virtue, will favor our work. 

Therefore, let this be the division of the work: 
In the first book, we shall set forth subtle **practice**. 
In the second, we shall discuss **theory** accurately. 
In the third, we shall discuss **semi-mathematical and semi-physical music**, and the reasoning behind it. 

First, therefore, let us discuss what music is and what harmony is.

<summary>The author justifies his plain writing style and invokes the legendary power of music to affect souls, animals, and the divine, while lamenting the decline of musical quality in his own era. He concludes by outlining the three-book structure of the treatise covering practice, theory, and the mathematical/physical nature of sound.</summary>

<keywords>musical miracles, Orpheus, King Saul, David, kithara, psaltery, musical practice, musical theory, decay of music, ancient authorities</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general introductory praise of music to a systematic definition of terms. It marks the formal beginning of the treatise following the prologue.</meta>

### The prologue being finished, the treatise begins, where it is first discussed what music is and what harmony is

<note>A decorative initial letter A at the beginning of the text block features stylized floral patterns characteristic of early modern printing.</note>

**H**armony and music are believed by many to be the same thing, but we feel quite differently. From the opinions of certain musicians, collected through long investigation, we have concluded that **harmony** is the mixing of concordant voices; **Music**, however, is the very reasoning of that concord, or a weighed and subtle investigation conducted with reason. 

Music is moreover threefold: for one is **worldly** <note>original: *mundana*; referring to the "Music of the Spheres," the mathematical harmony believed to exist between celestial bodies</note>, another is **human** <note>original: *humana*; the internal harmony between the human soul and body</note>, and the third is called **instrumental**. Since worldly and human music are speculative and theoretical, we will treat them in the second and third books. The third kind, which is entirely concerned with instruments, will claim a smaller place for its consideration. 

An instrument is twofold: for one exists by nature, the other by art. The **natural instrument** is the human voice, by which we can naturally raise and lower our tone. The **artistic instrument** is said to be that which is made by art, not nature, such as the **monochord** <note>a single-stringed instrument used to teach musical intervals and ratios</note>, the **cithara** <note>an ancient stringed instrument, similar to a lyre</note>, and others that serve the performance of songs. Concerning the most accurate practical consideration of these, three things occur to us to be examined: namely, the **voice** (that is, sound) and **number** (or measure). 

"Voice" in this context is used loosely for the sound of both humans and instruments; as **Aristotle's** opinion is in his book *On the Soul* <note>original: *de anima*</note>: voice is the sound of a living thing only, yet "voice" is also used for inanimate things like musical instruments because they possess a similarity in that they produce discrete sounds. "Sound," however, is not taken simply here, but rather for the sound of two or more strings struck together, or of two or more people singing in each other's presence. **Number**, similarly, is not considered simply, but in relation to the "passions" <note>technical term for the mathematical ratios or the emotional qualities affected by music</note>. 

The first consideration is called **firm song** <note>original: *cantus firmus*; the foundational, often slow-moving melody of a polyphonic composition</note> by the moderns, while by some it is called **plainchant** <note>original: *cantus planus*</note>. The second is **counterpoint** <note>original: *contrapunctus*</note>, which is known to have been called "organization" by the ancients. And the third is **figured song** <note>original: *cantus figuratus*; music with complex rhythms or ornamentation</note>, which is called "organ song" by many. According to this threefold consideration, therefore, we shall divide this compendium of ours. 

In the first consideration, we will primarily provide three things. First, we will demonstrate to the senses the correct divisions of the instrument composed by art for those being instructed, and we will advise them to commit to memory the sound of the strings according to the struck divisions. Second, we will treat the natural organ through **arsis and thesis** <note>Greek terms for the "lifting" and "setting down" of the voice or a beat</note>, that is, through the raising and lowering, or through the tightening and loosening...

<summary>This page defines the distinction between harmony (the sound) and music (the reasoning behind the sound), and introduces Boethius's classic division of music into worldly, human, and instrumental categories. It outlines the structure of the upcoming treatise, focusing on practical instrumental music, plainchant, counterpoint, and figured song.</summary>
<keywords>harmony, music theory, musica mundana, musica humana, musica instrumentalis, monochord, Aristotle, cantus firmus, counterpoint, arsis and thesis</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: The author has just defined the three types of music (worldly, human, and instrumental) and is now beginning Chapter Two, which moves from definitions to the practical construction of the monochord.</meta>

# Chapter Two

By joining our voices with the instrument of the art, we shall accustom ourselves to singing harmoniously until we have learned to sing correctly without it. Thirdly, we will clearly describe the odes or musical notes <note>original: *odas vel notulas*; referring to the system of musical notation used to represent pitches</note> by which every chant can be recognized, sung, and composed. However, because there are various types of instruments established by art, we will provide a single method and rule for dividing a string, lest the instruction become obscure through too much variety. From this, the name "monochord" <note>from the Greek *monos* (single) and *chorde* (string)</note> was adopted by the Greeks; afterward, by passing through other steps, we shall arrive at our intended goal.

->The elementary division or composition of the monochord<-

<note>A decorative initial letter R, likely a woodcut, marks the beginning of the primary instructional text, a common feature in 16th-century printing to guide the reader's eye to the start of a new section.</note>

**T**he regular monochord is subtly divided by Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), a Roman senator and philosopher whose works on music theory were the standard textbooks throughout the Middle Ages and Renaissance</note> using numbers and measurement. But just as his method is useful and pleasing to theorists, it is equally laborious and difficult for singers to understand. However, because we have promised to satisfy both, we will provide a very easy division of the regular monochord. No one should think we found this with little effort; indeed, we discovered it with much sweat, reading the precepts of the ancients through many sleepless nights and avoiding the errors of the moderns. Any man, even one who is only moderately educated, will be able to understand it easily.

Therefore, let a string or cord of any length be taken and stretched over a piece of wood that has some hollow space <note>the sound box or resonator of the instrument</note>. Let the outermost place where the string is tied be marked with point **a**. Let the other location, placed in the region from which the string is pulled and twisted <note>the tuning peg</note>, be marked with point **q**. The total length of the string, **q** to **a**, should be divided into two equal parts, and at the point of equal distance, let it be marked with the letter **b**. Next, we divide the length of the string from **b** to **a** through the middle, and in the center of that division, we shall place **d**. The length **b** to **d** will be cut again, and **f** will be placed in the middle of that section. Understand that the same must be done for the other half of the string, namely **b** to **q**: for in the first division, the letter **p** will be shaped in the middle, and in the division of **b** to **p**, the letter **l** is placed equally distant from both. Between **l** and **p**, keeping the same rule of intervals, let **n** be inserted. If we divide **f** to **n** through the middle, we shall mark the letter **i**. 

By this middle division, we will not proceed further into smaller parts until we have made other divisions; instead, we shall divide the whole length **a** to **q** into three parts. Measuring from letter **a**, **m** shall be placed at the end of the first third, and **e** at the two-thirds mark <note>original: *besse*; a Latin term for two-thirds of a unit</note>. Then, let **e** to **q** be divided again into three parts, and coming from letter **q** toward **e**, the sign of a square **b** <note>original: *b. quadruz*; referring to B-natural, as opposed to the rounded "soft" B-flat</note> shall be fixed at the two-thirds mark.

<summary>This page introduces the practical construction of the monochord, moving from Boethius's complex theories to a simplified geometric method for dividing a single string into musical intervals. The author provides step-by-step instructions for marking specific points on the instrument to represent different pitches.</summary>
<keywords>monochord, music theory, Boethius, string division, musical intervals, instrument making, geometry in music</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuity with previous page: Having introduced the general concept of the monochord, the author now begins Chapter Three, providing specific instructions for its mathematical division and the musical intervals that result from these measurements.</meta>

# Chapter Three

If the quantity of point $\pi$ <note>original: $\pi$; used here as a geometric marker on the string</note> is doubled at B-square <note>original: *b. quadr.*; the square-shaped 'b' used in medieval notation to indicate B-natural, the source of our modern natural $\natural$ and sharp # symbols</note> and Q, let it be marked as B. But again, we shall divide the space between M and B in the middle, and we will mark the middle point of the division with the letter K. If we double the quantity of Z and K, we place C at the end of the doubling. But then G should be situated at equal distances between E and B-square. If, however, we divide G into two equal parts, it will be marked with the letter O. Thus, the entire monochord is divided by a proper partition, as you may recognize in the following figure.

->The correct understanding of the given divisions<-

<note>A vertical diagram of a monochord string, labeled with alphabetic pitch notation (a through p) to illustrate mathematical divisions and musical intervals. This is a precise technical diagram of a monochord, a fundamental tool in medieval and Renaissance music theory for demonstrating the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note>

**Alternatively**, now that the monochord has been divided, it remains to consider those quantities more deeply and precisely. It must be known, therefore, that the entire distance between A and B is called a **tone**, and by the Greeks a *phtongos* <note>original: *phtongon*; a Greek term for a musical sound or a specific pitch</note>, which among us is interpreted as a "sound." But this will be more easily understood by the following example: let the string be struck while extended to its full length, and let the sound be noted. Then let a finger or some other thinner object, not spread over a great width, be placed under the string; and let the string be struck again. It will happen that it emits a somewhat higher sound. When you wish to compare the higher sound with the lower sound (or the lower with the higher), the distance will be that of a tone. However, what you compare will become a **semitone**, not because it is half of a tone <note>In the Pythagorean tuning system used in the Middle Ages, a semitone is mathematically slightly less than half of a whole tone</note>, but because the voice does not ascend to the full integrity of a tone; it is called an "imperfect tone." Nevertheless, the practical musician should not be overly concerned with this imperfection of the semitone, since it is a highly theoretical <note>original: *speculativa*; referring to "musica speculativa," the study of music as a mathematical science</note> matter and removed from practical singers. But it is necessary that the practitioner knows it is not a perfect tone. Indeed, we shall defer subtle disputes of this kind to the second book.

Furthermore, that quantity or distance extended between C and D is a tone, and between D and E is likewise a tone. But between E and F there is a **semitone**; between F and G a tone; between G and H a tone; between H and I a semitone; between I and B-square a semitone; between B-square and K a semitone; between K and L a tone; between L and M a tone; between M and N a semitone; then N to O a tone; and O to P a tone. And thus the sounds which arise from the striking of related and neighboring divisions, when compared to each other, emit either a tone or a semitone. But the comparison of distant tones produces several types of species. Thus, because the space between C and E consists of two tones, it is called a **ditone** or **bitone** <note>equivalent to a major third</note>. However, the distance from A to C is called a **semiditonus** <note>equivalent to a minor third</note>, because it is formed as an imperfect ditone with half a tone removed. But B to E is a **diatessaron** <note>a perfect fourth</note> because it is capable of four voices, or it is called a **tetrachord** <note>from the Greek *tetra* (four) and *chorde* (string)</note> which is the division and distance of four strings. For one string or voice is B, another C, the third...

<summary>This page explains the geometric and mathematical division of the monochord string to produce specific musical pitches and intervals. It defines basic musical distances such as tones, semitones, ditones (major thirds), and the diatessaron (perfect fourth).</summary>
<keywords>monochord, interval, tone, semitone, phtongos, ditone, semiditone, diatessaron, tetrachord, B-natural, music theory, Boethius</keywords>

<header>Chapter Three</header>

## Chapter Three

...the fourth <note>original: *quarta*, referring to the interval of a perfect fourth or Diatessaron</note>, within which three intervals are enclosed: namely, two tones and one semitone. Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard textbook for music theory in the Middle Ages.</note> divides the monochord by tetrachords <note>A group of four notes spanning the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> in this manner: **b.c.** is the first tetrachord; the second is **e.h.** In such a way that **e** is the end of the first and the beginning of the second division; and in a similar way **b.l.** But when the end of the preceding is taken as the beginning for the following tetrachord, it is called a **conjoint** tetrachord, which the Greeks call **synemmenon** <note>original: *synemeno*; meaning "connected" or "linked."</note>. 

However, when **h**—that is, the end of the first or preceding—is not taken as the beginning for the second or following, but the subsequent tetrachord **a. b-square** <note>original: *b. qdr.*; the square-shaped 'b' (B-natural) which starts the next grouping</note> takes its start, proceeding through **k. l.** and stopping at **m.**, it is called **disjoint** in Latin, and in Greek is named **diazeugmenon** <note>meaning "separated" or "disjoined."</note>, because those boundary tetrachords are not coupled together, but the beginning of the second is separated from the end of the first by the distance of a tone. 

There is also a fifth tetrachord, **m. p.**, which is called **hyperboleon**, that is, the "exceeding" or "excellent"; for no other string among the high tetrachords surpasses its strings in height and sublimity of pitch. The first tetrachord is called **hypaton** in Greek, which is "the principal" or "the lower" in Latin. The second tetrachord is **messon** in Greek, or "of the middle" in the Roman tongue. 

The harmony is divided in this way because, by the teaching of nature, it is so governed that after two tones it is tempered by a semitone. The strings, of which the names are diverse, are eight in number. These are: **Hypate**, **Parypate**, **Lichanos**, **Mese**, **Paramese**, **Trite**, **Paranete**, and **Nete**. 

<margin>
<note>A faint vertical diagram on the left margin represents the division of the monochord or the ladder of the musical scale (tetrachords). This technical diagram illustrates the Boethian division of the tetrachords, though quite faded in this copy.</note>
</margin>

**Hypate** is called the "principal." From this, the Greeks call a consul **Hypaten**. **Parypate** means "next to the principal." **Lichanos** is so named because, according to the opinion of Boethius, it was struck or touched by the index finger, which is called **Lichanos** in Greek, while the first finger struck the **hypate**. **Mese** means "middle," because it is placed in the middle position in the seven-stringed scale <note>original: *eptra chordo*; referring to the ancient heptachord</note>. **Paramese** means "next to the middle." **Trite** is so called because it is the "third" before the **Nete**. **Paranete** means "near the nete," and **Nete** is the "lowest"—not indeed in its sound, but in the terminology of its position <note>In ancient Greek theory, the highest pitch was often called "lowest" (nete) because it was physically lowest on the instrument or furthest from the player.</note>. 

Furthermore, because these strings are utilized in different tetrachords, they receive various names with a certain addition. For they are called: **Hypate hypaton** <note>Principal of the principals</note>, **Parypate hypaton**, **Lichanos hypaton**, and there follows **Hypate messon** <note>Principal of the middle notes</note>, **Parypate messon**, **Lichanos messon**, and the **Mese**. Then the conjoint: **Trite synemmenon**, **Paranete synemmenon**, and **Nete synemmenon**. But the **Paramese** is disjoined from the **Mese** by a tone. And there follows: **Trite diezeugmenon**, **Paranete diezeugmenon**, **Nete diezeugmenon**, **Trite hyperboleon**, **Paranete hyperboleon**, and **Nete hyperboleon**. 

But because the **Mese** was not positioned in the exact middle since there were fourteen strings <note>original: *nervi*; literally "nerves," used here for the gut strings of an instrument</note>, a string was added which is called **proslambanomenos** <note>original: *proslabanomenos*; meaning "the added note," the lowest note of the system (A)</note> or *prosodos* by the Greeks, but by our people is named the "acquired," "assumed," or "added" string. For if a tone...

<summary>This page explains the structure of the Greek Greater Perfect System as used in medieval music theory, detailing how notes are grouped into five 'tetrachords' (groups of four). It provides the etymology for the names of individual strings, such as the Lichanos (index finger) and the Mese (middle), based on the teachings of Boethius.</summary>
<keywords>tetrachord, Boethius, Synemmenon, Diezeugmenon, Hypate, Mese, Nete, Proslambanomenos, monochord division, medieval music theory</keywords>

# Chapter Three

If a species is added, a **perfect fifth** <note>original: *diapente*; from the Greek for "through five."</note> is created, so named because it consists of five voices or notes. It is also called a **pentachord** because it consists of five strings or four intervals: three whole tones and one semitone, such as the span from **a** to **e**. But if a semitone is subtracted from a perfect fifth, a **tritone** <note>An interval of three whole tones, notorious in medieval music as the "devil in music" for its dissonance.</note> is produced, as seen between **f** and **b-natural** <note>original: *b ♮*, represented here by the "square b" or *b quadratum*.</note>. After the perfect fifth, the previous intervals are added back to the fourth in this manner: a perfect fifth with a semitone added, as from **a** to **f** <note>A minor sixth</note>; a perfect fifth with a whole tone, as from **e** to **h** <note>A major sixth; 'h' was often used in Germanic notation for B-natural</note>; a perfect fifth with a minor third <note>original: *semitonio*, likely a scribal error for *semiditono* (minor third)</note>, as from **a** to **g** <note>A minor seventh</note>; and a perfect fifth with a major third <note>original: *ditono*</note>, as from **e** to **b-natural** <note>original: *b. qdr.*</note>.

But when a perfect fifth is joined with a **perfect fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>, it composes the species called the **octave** <note>original: *dyapason*; from the Greek *dia pason*, meaning "through all [the notes]."</note>. This name signifies "through the whole" or "concerning the total" in Latin, because every melody—which the Greeks call a **symphony**—and every harmonic variation is contained within it. For this reason, it was rightly said by the ancients that after the octave, there is always a repetition. The seven intermediate notes each have their own distinction; hence the poet **Virgil** wisely wrote in the sixth book of the *Aeneid*:

->“The Thracian priest <note>Referring to Orpheus, the legendary musician.</note> also, in his long robe, matches the seven distinctions of voices to the rhythms.”<-

The eighth note is indeed similar to the first. Therefore, **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory I, traditionally credited with organizing the plainchant of the church.</note> did not establish more than seven different letters, but repeats them and places them again, because between the first and the eighth, the greatest affinity and likeness is found. They are so similar that they are not perceived to differ except in their **sharpness** <note>original: *acuminis*; high pitch</note> or **heaviness** <note>original: *grauitatis*; low pitch</note>. Thus, the lower octave was labeled **a. b.**, while the higher was labeled **b. p.** 

The same letters used for the low notes were also used to mark the high notes of the octave. However, it should be noted that while the **musical tones** <note>original: *phthongi*</note> are equal to each other in sound, they vary in the size of their physical representation in the diagram. Depending on whether they have more or less heaviness, they occupy larger or smaller spaces. Consequently, the whole tone **a. b.** has double the physical interval when compared to the tone **b. b-natural**. Similarly, **c. d.** is compared to **k. k.** The same must be said for the semitones and other larger species, as demonstrated in the figure. But hereafter, they shall not be placed in this way, lest the understanding of beginners be obscured; instead, just as whole tones are equal to each other in sound, we shall place the gaps between them as equal. We will, however, diminish the intervals of the semitones so that it is clearly evident that a semitone is less than a whole tone. All these aforementioned points will be made clear by the figure placed below.

The **disjoined** tetrachord: that is, the **diezeugmenon**. <note>So called because it is separated from the middle tetrachord by a whole tone.</note>

# Chapter Three

**T**herefore, so that the arrangement of the figure where we have imprinted the Greek names of the strings and the Latin letters is not forgotten: although the whole range of music is contained within a single **octave** <term>diapason; the interval of an eighth</term>, we have nevertheless set down two, which are called the **double octave** <term>bis diapason</term>, so that we might imitate the teaching of Boethius and the Greeks. The **handbook** <term>enchiridion; a common title for medieval music manuals</term>, however, calls this the *disdiapason*. From this, the error of those who begin poorly is manifest, for they say there are twenty letters: Γ, A, B, C, D, E, F, G, a, b, c, d, e, f, g, aa, bb, cc, dd, ee. Since there are not twenty, but only seven diverse ones, after which no others but the same ones are reborn as if anew. But these people begin from the letter **Gamma** <note>original: Γ</note> and end at the third 'c', because they invent the story that **Gregory** <note>Pope Gregory the Great, to whom Gregorian chant is traditionally attributed</note> wished to place the first letter of his name there, and named it with the Greek name Gamma because the teachings of music were handed down by the Greeks.

But I respond that even if this were permitted to them, they wander greatly from the truth in their division when they say there are eight "grave" (low) notes, seven "acute" (high) notes, and five "super-acute" (very high) notes; for it has been shown that the eighth letter and the first differ only in pitch—that is, in height and depth. But further, they try to fortify themselves with the authority of Boethius, as they say; for they say the first high note is the *Mese* <note>The "middle" note in the Greek system</note> which is the letter 'h' [a], and 'a' is the second. It is gathered, therefore, among the low notes that Γ is placed by them. Even if it is said by Boethius, the placement of Γ among the low notes by Gregory was not well-said because of the addition of the letter Γ. Just as the first 'a' relates to 'b', or to the second 'a', so it relates to Γ. But the second 'a' differs from the first 'a' only in pitch; therefore Γ will also differ from 'a' only in pitch. Given twenty letters, the division ought to be made such that there are seven low, seven high, and six super-high notes. But the addition of the letter did not please Gregory, since he used only fifteen. **Tinctoris** <note>Johannes Tinctoris (c. 1435–1511), a famous music theorist</note>, however, being foreign to this reasoning, says the first Γ is the lowest, the second is low, and the third is high. Truly, we shall show a little later from where this error has invaded the singers. Now let us proceed to declare the teaching of others.

### A subtle application of the aforementioned figure for the use of singers

**A**ll these twenty letters **Guido the Monk** <note>Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the inventor of modern musical notation</note>—perhaps a better monk than a musician—embraced while using the **four-string scale** <term>tetrachord</term> as he composed the **six-string scale** <term>hexachord</term>. And he was compelled to this kind of hexachord for this reason: because the number six is called **perfect** by mathematicians, because its divisor parts [1, 2, 3] when taken together constitute that same six; namely, 1, 2, and 3, which together make six. And each string of this hexachord is named from the first six syllables of six words...

<note>A faint woodcut illustration depicts a Guidonian hand, a mnemonic device used in medieval music to teach the notes and hexachords by mapping them to the joints of the human hand.</note>

<summary>This page critiques the traditional twenty-note musical scale, arguing that it incorrectly repeats seven basic notes. It introduces the historical figures of Boethius, Pope Gregory, and Guido d'Arezzo, specifically discussing the mathematical perfection of the six-note hexachord system.</summary>
<keywords>octave, diapason, hexachord, tetrachord, Guido d'Arezzo, Boethius, Johannes Tinctoris, music theory, Guidonian hand, Gamma ut, Greek note names</keywords>

# Chapter Four

The hymn to Saint John the Baptist received its name from its opening words: "So that with relaxed strings." <note>original: *Ut queant laxis*. This hymn, traditionally attributed to Paul the Deacon (c. 720–799), was used by Guido d'Arezzo to teach singers how to find pitches.</note> "So that your servants may be able to sing the wonders of your deeds with relaxed vocal cords: Cleanse the guilt of the stained lip: O Saint John." <note>original: *Ut queant laxis resonare fibris: Mira gestorum: famuli tuorum: Solve polluti labij reatum: Sancte Ioannes.*</note> From this, if we correctly inspect the first syllables after each punctuation mark, we extract these six voices: **ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la**. Each of these is separated from its neighbor in the series by a whole tone, except for **fa**, which departs from **mi** by the space of a semitone <note>A semitone is the smallest interval in this musical system, roughly half of a whole tone.</note>. It will therefore have two tones below it and two above. 

When the first letter **G** is placed with the syllable **ut**, the whole is called **Gamma ut** <note>This is the origin of the word "gamut," meaning the full range of notes.</note>. Similarly, from the letter **A** and the syllable **re**, **Are** is made; from the letter **B** and the syllable **mi**, **Bmi**; from the letter **C** and the syllable **fa**, **Cfa**; from **D** and **sol**, **Dsol**; and from **E** and **la**, **Ela**.

In order to imitate the teaching of Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works on music were the standard textbooks of the Middle Ages.</note>, which divides the whole harmony by tetrachords <note>A group of four notes.</note>, Guido emits another hexachord <note>A group of six notes.</note> like an offshoot when he reaches the fourth place, which is **C faut**. If the syllable **ut** is placed with the letter **C** (which already has **fa**), the whole compound is called **Cfaut**. This continues with **d sol re** and **e la mi**, where the first hexachord ends. 

But since the letter **F** follows, the syllable **ut** is placed again with the **fa** of the second tetrachord, which is the fourth voice of that second tetrachord. Therefore, since it is **F fa**, once **ut** is joined to it, it receives that name (**Ffaut**), followed by **G sol re** and **A la mi**. And lest he seem to have ignored the similarity of the outer notes of the octave <note>original: *diapason*</note>, he begins to place the hexachord again. Since we have two voices located there from the two previous tetrachords (namely the second and third), the letter **G** with **sol re** and the added **ut** is called by that name: **G sol re ut**. This continues with **A la mi re**, where the third hexachord is completed. 

At this point, two notes are coupled together in one place: **fa** and **mi**. The first is **B fa** <note>B-flat</note> and the second is **B square mi** <note>B-natural</note>. As the letters show, we recognize these voices to be unequal, one being higher than the other, and thus that tone is divided into two semitones. This is followed by **C sol fa ut**, because just as the second hexachord is joined to the first in that place, the whole compound is so named. 

It continues with **D la sol re**, and so on: **E la mi**, **F fa ut**, **G sol re ut**, **A la mi re**, **B fa**, and **B square mi**, just as before. These hexachords could be multiplied to infinity, according to the capacity of the instrument. But since in every science one must eventually reach an end, the repetition of hexachords now ceases. Therefore, in **C sol fa**, **ut** is no longer placed. Instead, we proceed to **D la sol**, where the sixth hexachord remains, and the seventh completes it in **E la**. Thus, he placed seven hexachords separated by seven voices, as he saw fit, which the subject figure will make clear. Do you not see the correct figure of Guido? But in Ripa <note>Likely referring to a different theorist or a specific school of thought.</note> it is not so, but rather through measures <unclear>weight of</unclear>...

<margin>
The high **octave** <note>original: *Diapasō acuta*</note> with seven intermediate notes, which is called the whole song.
</margin>

<margin>
The low **octave** <note>original: *Diapason grauis*</note> which is called the whole song, including the seven intermediate notes.

# Chapter Four

The hymn to Saint John the Baptist received its name from its opening words: "So that with relaxed strings." <note>original: *Ut queant laxis*. This hymn, traditionally attributed to Paul the Deacon (c. 720–799), was used by Guido d'Arezzo to teach singers how to find pitches.</note> "So that your servants may be able to sing the wonders of your deeds with relaxed vocal cords: Cleanse the guilt of the stained lip: O Saint John." <note>original: *Ut queant laxis resonare fibris: Mira gestorum: famuli tuorum: Solve polluti labij reatum: Sancte Ioannes.*</note> From this, if we correctly inspect the first syllables after each punctuation mark, we extract these six voices: **ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la**. Each of these is separated from its neighbor in the series by a whole tone, except for **fa**, which departs from **mi** by the space of a semitone <note>A semitone is the smallest interval in this musical system, roughly half of a whole tone.</note>. It will therefore have two tones below it and two above. 

When the first letter **G** is placed with the syllable **ut**, the whole is called **Gamma ut** <note>This is the origin of the word "gamut," meaning the full range of notes.</note>. Similarly, from the letter **A** and the syllable **re**, **A-re** is made; from the letter **B** and the syllable **mi**, **B-mi**; from the letter **C** and the syllable **fa**, **C-fa**; from **D** and **sol**, **D-sol**; and from **E** and **la**, **E-la**.

In order to imitate the teaching of Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works on music were the standard textbooks of the Middle Ages.</note>, which divides the whole harmony by tetrachords <note>A group of four notes.</note>, ...

<note>A diagram depicts the Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic device used in medieval and Renaissance music pedagogy to teach the hexachord system and solmization. Syllables are assigned to the various joints and tips of the fingers to help students memorize the overlapping scales. This is an excellent and clear example of a seminal musical pedagogical tool, essential for understanding the history of music theory and education.</note>

# Chapter Five

A clear demonstration of the errors of certain people regarding the aforementioned matters.

Although not everything set forth by him <note>Likely referring to a specific theorist or the previous chapter's subject</note> concerning these matters has been published by us, I do not think the reader should judge that we have done so recklessly. Indeed, if God favors our prayers and efforts, every necessary point—even those which are placed in a disorganized and confused manner by him and others—will be addressed in its proper place, and not a single iota shall be left untouched. Furthermore, we shall soon point out other matters which they understood poorly and in which they were deceived by error.

It was stated above and demonstrated that the <term>synemenon tetrachord</term> <note>original: "tetracordum synemenon." In the Greek system, this is the "conjunct" group of four notes that introduces the B-flat.</note> is joined to the <term>diazeugmenon</term> <note>The "disjunct" tetrachord, which uses B-natural.</note> or is separated from it. However, some who are ignorant of these things—as we investigated in a long dispute with our very close friend <term>Tristan de Silva</term>, a Spaniard and a man of the sharpest intellect—place the disjunct tetrachord when they reach the <term>mese</term> <note>The "middle note" of the Greek system, equivalent to A.</note>. After this, they place the *synemenon*, then the <term>hypoboleon</term> <note>The "extra" notes added to the top of the scale.</note>, and thus they cause the <term>nete hyperboleon</term> <note>The highest note of the standard Greek system.</note> to be distant from the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>The lowest note of the system.</note> by three strings beyond two <term>octaves</term> <note>original: "diapason."</note>. It is manifest that this is against the truth and the teaching of <term>Boethius</term> <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard textbook for medieval music theory.</note>.

Boethius himself declares this most accurately in the first book, in the chapter beginning "But since he places the middle note again," and it is further declared evidently with numbers and a measured diagram in the fourth book, in the chapter beginning "The two tetrachords which are joined to each other, but disjoined from the middle note." But regarding these matters, we have written in our *Theoretica* <note>The author's own theoretical work.</note>; nor would I have cited anything here except to show the error of many regarding this, as many of these people easily follow these teachings in their own learning.

<term>Marchetto</term> <note>Marchetto da Padova (fl. 1305–1319), a hugely influential Italian theorist who updated the system of notation and measurement.</note>, however, does not do it this way; rather, he places the disjunction from the *mese*. After that, he places the *hyperboleon*, then the *synemenon*, and thus he arranges eighteen strings. To these, he adds one below (namely <term>gamma</term>) and another above (namely *e*), increasing the number to twenty strings. But if anyone wishes to understand him in a healthy way, they could, in my judgment, say that once that *synemenon* tetrachord is removed, the remaining notes are arranged in that manner. Then, after the *nete hyperboleon*, since it is similar to the *mese*, the *synemenon* tetrachord is placed in the way it ought to follow after the *mese*; and so as not to hinder the *diazeugmenon*, he arranged it thus. It could be saved <note>In a logical or theological sense: to "save" an argument is to find a way to make it consistent with the truth.</note> in many other ways as well. I, for my part, do not doubt that he is "saved," since Christ on the cross prayed for those who know not what they do.

A certain brother, <term>Johannes of the Carthusian Order</term>, a Frenchman, tries to "save" him by calling him both unlearned and in need of the schoolmaster's rod. For my part, I value this Marchetto as much as four Marchettos. <note>This is likely a pun or a bit of academic sarcasm regarding Marchetto's status and the Carthusian's defense of him.</note>

<summary>The author critiques various music theorists, including Marchetto da Padova, for their misunderstandings of how tetrachords are structured in relation to the Guidonian hand and the Greek system. He appeals to the authority of Boethius to correct these errors while offering a witty, slightly backhanded defense of Marchetto's mistakes.</summary>

<meta>The author continues his critique of other music theorists, specifically targeting Roger Caperon. # Chapter Six

I would not doubt that Roger Caperon, the Frenchman, could be swallowed in a single gulp <note>original: "vno potu deglutiri." This is a sharp rhetorical flourish common in medieval and Renaissance academic polemics, suggesting Caperon’s theories are so flimsy they can be dismissed instantly.</note>. For this same Roger Caperon says thus: There are four figures which are added in song: namely, <term>Coruph</term> <note>From the Greek *koryphe*, meaning "summit" or "head."</note>, <term>synemenon</term>, <term>apotome</term>, and <term>crisis</term>. He calls the note Gamma "Coruph"; because B-flat <note>original: "b"</note> is added, he calls it *synemenon*; and for the B-natural <note>original: "♮"</note>, he uses *apotome*. But he calls the high "e" *crisis*, in which the followers of Guido place the note *la*. He is correct to say the *synemenon* is B-flat, if he understands it as the distance of a <term>semitone</term> from A to B-flat—though he does not state this himself. He also speaks well of the *apotome* if he understands the distance between B-flat and B-natural; for it is so named by <term>Boethius</term> and <term>Philolaus</term> <note>Philolaus (c. 470–385 BCE) was a Pythagorean philosopher; Boethius (c. 477–524 CE) was the primary authority on music theory in the Middle Ages.</note> as that which we call the "major semitone." And thus he posits twenty strings, falling into the same error as others.

### A brief notice on various instruments.

Having set out a moderately regular division of the <term>monochord</term> <note>A one-stringed instrument used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note>, it remains for us to teach how to reduce the human voice to this rule, raising and lowering it alternately. We shall achieve this better if a knowledge of various instruments is first declared to us, while we follow their main tracks, so that we may know how to temper the natural organ—the voice—with other instruments. 

Of these instruments, some thin out the sounds through excessive tension, or dull them and return them to a low pitch through loosening. There are also diverse strings, both in length and thickness, as in the <term>cithara</term>, the <term>lyre</term>, the <term>polychord</term>, the <term>clavichord</term>, the <term>harpsichord</term> <note>original: "clavicibalo"</note>, the <term>psaltery</term>, and many other instruments to which new names have been given by posterity, and of which we shall make clear mention in the second book. 

All these, however, cannot escape our mathematical division; for the strings of the monochord—which are of the same thickness, length, and tension—if struck at the same distance, will necessarily emit the same sound, just as we find in ancient monochords. But according to whether each is struck closer to or further from the place where they are twisted <note>Referring to the bridge or the tuning pins.</note>, it emits a lower or higher sound according to the proportions of the division given above. Now, however, not all strings are of the same thickness, nor are they tempered with the same tension. Therefore, if the frequent practice of music were to fall from memory, we could by no means find the truth of <term>consonance</term> <note>The sounding together of notes that results in a pleasing or stable interval.</note> through these modern instruments. But returning to the prior division, we would mark the sounds. For if anyone wishes to fit this in harmony, let him turn to the sound of our instrument—the voice—and having weighed that, he will know this. There are, however, some of the new monochords having one...

<summary>The author critiques the terminology of Roger Caperon and explains that while musical instruments vary in construction, they all must adhere to the mathematical principles of the monochord to maintain correct pitch.</summary>

# Chapter Six

...the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> divided toward the higher parts in this manner: since at least six strings are tempered in that way and are of the same thickness. In such a case, the sharpness or depth of the strings’ sound is produced by the small or large distance between them, according to the measurement of the proportion of tones or other species. Those constructed in this way are very easily tuned, because any sound is most easily brought into harmony with its own octave.

There are also other instruments whose strings are arranged in the opposite way; for the closer the finger is placed to the point where the string is twisted <note>The tuning pegs.</note>, the deeper the sounds they return, and vice versa—such as the <term>lyre</term>. But this does not hinder our system of division, because the striking of the string does not happen from the middle of the string—that is, from the place where it is twisted to point *b*—but from the place of the binding <note>The bridge or nut of the instrument.</note> to point *b*. Thus, the closer the finger is placed to the binding of the string, the sharper the sound will be, because the string is shorter. Conversely, the closer it approaches the place where it is twisted, the deeper it sounds, because the string is longer.

If, therefore, we wish to divide this instrument, the same result will occur by swapping and transposing the letters. That is, while the letter *b* remains in the middle as before, let *q* be moved to the place of *a*, and *a* to the place of *q*, and let each of the remaining letters be transferred into the place of the other.

Furthermore, in any modern <note>original: "nouozuz," likely "novorum" (new/modern).</note> perfected instrument, the tone is divided into two semitones. This is just like our <term>mesẽ</term> and <term>paramesẽ</term> <note>The "middle" note and the note "next to the middle" in the Greek Greater Perfect System.</note> through the <term>trite synemmenon</term> <note>The "third string of the conjunct tetrachord," essentially a B-flat.</note>, a division of which we shall speak shortly. When we wish to produce a whole tone in such instruments, it will be necessary for us to cross two divisions of the string.

In this instrument, thus divided down to semitones, many strings are placed—some thicker and others thinner. Nowadays, they use five strings arranged so that the thickest, in its total length, sounds a tone below the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>The lowest note of the Greek scale.</note>, which we call "Gamma-ut." The second string is the <term>hypapate hypaton</term>, which is a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> distant from the first. The third is the <term>lichanos meson</term>, higher than the second by a <term>major third</term> <note>original: "ditono," literally "two tones."</note>. Let the fourth string sound the <term>mese</term>. Let the fifth string emit the sound of the <term>paranete diazeugmenon</term> or the <term>nete synemmenon</term>, sounding an octave and a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note> with the first string.

However, this is not done by necessity; they can be harmoniously arranged in other diverse ways. For instance, the first could be the *proslambanomenos*, the second the *lichanos*, the third the *mese*, and the others elsewhere. These can similarly be placed elsewhere at the will of the player. But because the method described above is now more commonly in use, we have set it down by preference.

In other instruments that sound by breath <note>Wind instruments.</note>, the width of the pipes <note>original: "calamorum," literally "reeds" or "stalks," referring here to organ pipes or recorders.</note> according to the proportion established above will determine the sharpness of the pitch, and...

<summary>This page explains the physics of pitch in stringed instruments, noting that shorter strings produce higher sounds, and outlines the standard five-string tuning system used in the author's time.</summary>
<keywords>octave, diapason, pitch, string thickness, lyre, tuning, Gamma-ut, Greek scale, wind instruments, proportion</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion on the mathematical division of stringed instruments to Chapter Seven, which deals with wind instruments (pipes and flutes) and the transition to the study of the human voice as a "natural instrument."</meta>

# Chapter Seven

...depth: therefore, pipes <note>original: "calami", referring to reed pipes or stalks used as flutes</note> that are twice as large sound an <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> lower; and other intermediate pipes, according to their greater or lesser thickness, will produce lower or higher sounds. This is provided that the opening where the sound is generated corresponds to both the length and the thickness.

There are also flutes <note>original: "fistule"</note> and <term>sambucae</term> <note>Historically a stringed instrument, but here likely referring to a type of pipe or flute based on the context of holes.</note> in which the length creates the difference; for these are opened with at least eight holes so that all can be blocked by the fingers. For if there were more holes, they would be useless, as the lower ones could not be closed, or the upper ones would remain uncovered and emit a sound we did not desire. Therefore, the closer the holes approach the bottom opening <note>The foot of the instrument.</note>, the lower the sounds they return; and the closer they approach the mouth of the player, the more sharply they cry out. But if half of any hole is closed with a finger, it creates a <term>semitone</term> compared to the full opening.

There are also others of this kind that are different, however, because they possess only four holes along with the main opening; yet with those four, they encompass any song in its high and low range, which is most wonderful. But this happens because a single hole can produce the sound of a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, an octave, or both, as well as a double octave, above or below—and this occurs if the breath is emitted with double, triple, quadruple, or three-halves <note>original: "trienti"</note> force. 

But for those desiring full knowledge of these instruments and their inventors, and how they gradually reached perfection, let them seek out our "Speculation" or "Theory," in which they will find wondrous things and most pleasant to know. If we had enclosed these in the small volume of this first book, they would have made the teaching more difficult. Therefore, leaving these behind, we shall come to the remaining natural instrument, as we promised:

## A subtle method of joining the voice with an instrument:

Some, being unwilling to instruct us in this teaching, put first what should be last and vice versa. Thus, when they want to prove something that will follow from their words, and they realize they have made no mention of it in its proper place, they insert it elsewhere where it fits less well. Therefore, that teaching is called <term>hyle</term> <note>From the Greek word for "matter" or "substance," often used to describe unformed or chaotic matter.</note>, or unformed matter, or a "chaos" of confusion. We, however, do not think we should proceed in this way; instead, just as one leads more easily from one thing to another, we have arranged these matters in the most suitable disciplinary order, serving the understanding. Hence, having looked with a moderate degree of knowledge at the instrument made by art, we wish to instruct more precisely regarding the natural instrument through an orderly and refined presentation. Therefore, we advise that the learner hold...

<meta>This page continues the practical instruction on musical pedagogy from the previous page, moving from the physical dimensions of instruments to the vocalization and naming of notes on the monochord. It introduces the author's unique solmization system "psallituros uoces istas" as an alternative to the systems of Odo and Guido of Arezzo.</meta>

# Chapter Seven

After we have made the division of the **monochord** <note>A one-stringed scientific instrument used to measure musical intervals.</note> as described, the student should strike the string and emit a voice in unison with it. Then, placing a finger on the second letter—namely **b**—comparing the string against the wood and striking the **b** upon the string, let him note the quality of the sound. Then let him emit a voice that is in unison and equal to the sound of the string. And thus, ascending through the other letters in order up to the **mese** <note>The "middle" note in the ancient Greek system, corresponding to our modern 'a'.</note>, let him emit voices and then descend in the same manner. For in this way, he will be able to do this from every letter to its octave.

But since the student must trust his teacher, we wish to constrain those to be instructed by certain laws: we command that they do not begin from just any letter, but from the letter **c** up to the next **c**. Nor shall they touch the first **b** <note>the "soft" or flat B</note>, but the second <term>♮</term> <note>The "square" or natural B.</note>, which is a whole tone distant from **a**, both in ascending and in descending. 

But someone might ask: "What should I utter with my mouth? A word, or only a sound?" The answer is that it does not matter on the first or second try which of the two you do; it is only necessary to understand the sounds and the distances between sounds, which are included within the eight tones containing the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note>. But afterward, so that the memory of the sounds may be retained, each one should be uttered with a different name. 

This was the custom among the ancients: for instance, <term>Odo</term> <note>Odo of Cluny, a 10th-century theorist.</note> of Euchariadis used to say *noe noë anne cane agis* <note>original: "noe noë anne cane agis"; these are traditional Byzantine-influenced nonsense syllables used to teach melodic formulas.</note>, which have no meaning. Others used *tri pro de nos trite*, which signified the series of the <term>modes</term> <note>Scales or melodic patterns.</note>, about which we shall speak in the proper place. Others, however, used only the letters of the alphabet, namely **a, b, c, d, e, f, g**, such as <term>Gregory, Augustine, Ambrose, and Bernard</term> <note>The author attributes the letter-name system to these famous Church Fathers and saints.</note>. 

<term>Guido</term> <note>Guido of Arezzo, the inventor of the "ut-re-mi" system.</note>, however, used <term>Γ (Gamma), mi, fa, sol, la</term> as we said before; although he did so by chance, since he also demonstrates all his examples with letters. However, his followers have since clung so strongly to these syllables that they think they are absolutely necessary for music, which is laughable. 

Therefore, we, who have labored long in studying and watching to seek the truth of this art, assign new words to each string, denoting the effects of the entire harmony. Thus, on the lower note, one says **psal**; on the next, **li**; on the third, **tur**; on the fourth, **per**; on the fifth, **vo**; on the sixth, **ces**; on the seventh, **is**; on the eighth, **tas**. And thus the conclusion of the syllables will be: <term>psallituros</term> <note>A Latin word meaning "those about to sing." The author is proposing his own mnemonic syllables.</note> these voices, since the whole harmony is made of eight tones. 

We place these from the low letter **c** to the high letter **c** because they teach one to sing perfectly. Therefore, they must begin from the letter **c**, because the song starts from that same letter and the first of the two semitones <note>The half-steps in a scale.</note> is enclosed in the interval, and the second sounds between two. The first, therefore, is **e-f**, [assigned the syllables] **tur-per**. Through the flat sign <term>♭</term>, a semitone is sometimes made from **a** to **b** <note>B-flat</note>, and sometimes from **a** to <term>♮</term> into the high **c**, on account of the <term>tetrachord synemmenon</term> <note>The "connected" group of four notes used to introduce B-flat.</note> and the <term>tetrachord diazeugmenon</term> <note>The "disjointed" group of four notes used for B-natural.</note>.

<summary>The author explains how to practice vocal intervals using a monochord and critiques existing solmization systems (like Guido's 'ut-re-mi'), proposing a new eight-syllable mnemonic system: psal-li-tur-per-vo-ces-is-tas.</summary>
<keywords>monochord, solmization, Odo of Cluny, Guido of Arezzo, psallituros, octave, semitone, tetrachord, music theory history</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter Seven of Ramos de Pareja’s "Musica Practica," transitioning from the physical measurement of the monochord to the vocal practice of solmization. It introduces the author’s revolutionary eight-syllable system as a replacement for the traditional six-syllable Guidonian hexachord.</meta>

# Chapter Seven

And if your division is located there, those three places are denoted by the letter **f**. 
This is: **ces is tas** <note>original: "ces is tas"; these are the final three syllables of the author's solmization system: psal-li-tur-per-vo-ces-is-tas.</note>. By using these voices in unison with the strings of the instrument, we will easily be able to harmonize the natural instrument <note>The human voice.</note> with that which is made by art <note>The monochord.</note>. But if we wish to climb above the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an eighth.</note>, we will place the syllable **sing** <note>original: "psal"</note> on that same sound as before. The two-syllable sounds **ces is** and **tas** will follow, and then **sing** <note>original: "psal"</note>, followed by **li** and **tur**, and the rest just as before. The same must be done in the lower register, because as we have often said, after the octave, the voice ascends again; and as many times as we transcend or descend beyond the octave, we renew the voices accordingly. 

Regarding these eight notes, therefore, the teaching is correct. When the song has reached the octave by such degrees, let the singer gradually lower the voice through the same steps and reversed syllables, and let him do this for a long time until he has thoroughly learned to do the same without striking the **monochord** <note>A scientific instrument with a single string used to teach musical intervals.</note>. Once this is accomplished, let him again ascend from the first note to the second; then let him sing from the first to the third, first with the middle note included and then skipping directly to it. Then let him descend from the third to the first, both by steps and by a leap. He should do the same from the first to the fourth, both ascending and descending, by degrees and by leaps. In the same manner, let him practice from the first to the fifth, both in raising and lowering the voice. 

Furthermore, just as he practiced from the first to the fifth in raising and lowering, let him take care to <term>rejoice</term> <note>original: "iubilare"; refers to singing melismatic or joyful passages.</note> from the second to the sixth, from the third to the seventh, and from the fourth to the eighth. In this exercise, however, we always command that the "foot" be fixed on the fifth note; this is for many reasons which will be discussed in the section on <term>modes</term> <note>original: "tropis"; the melodic frameworks or scales of medieval music.</note>. Similarly, when he first ascends to the octave, let him rest on the fifth note, and beginning again on that same sound, let him reach the octave so that he says: **It is sung through voi-** <note>original: "psallitur per uo"</note>, and after a period of rest is made, let him say: **-ces are these** <note>original: "uo ces is tas"</note>. He should repeat this often, descending: **these are ces voi-** <note>original: "tas is ces uo"</note>. 

Afterward, having dropped the voice with an interval of rest, let him say: **voi- through -tur -li sing** <note>original: "uo per tur li psal"</note>. Then, let him stretch the voice from the first note to its octave, namely: **sing these** <note>original: "psal tas"</note>, and conversely, let him descend: **these sing** <note>original: "tas psal"</note>. In these and other exercises, each person should take up as much practice as he sees is necessary to sing promptly and expeditiously. 

But lest the pronunciation of the sound be hindered by a multitude of letters in a single syllable, we wish for singers to remove the letters that follow the vowel if they cause any harm, such as the **s** and the **p** that precedes the **s**. These should always be removed, since here we are teaching music, not <term>orthography</term> <note>The correct way of spelling; Ramos prioritizes vocal clarity over literal spelling.</note>. 

Someone might doubt, however—and not without merit—why we place eight distances when there are only seven intervals, as is also handed down by us; he might think a mistake has been made. To this, one should say that there is the greatest conformity and similarity between...

<sig>g</sig>
<meta>Catchword: the first and the eighth</meta>

He also offers a pedagogical tip on simplifying pronunciation for singers, emphasizing musicality over correct spelling.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Chapter Seven of Ramos de Pareja’s "Musica Practica." It transitions from the physical mechanics of vocalization (phonetics) to a cosmological defense of his eight-syllable solmization system, contrasting it with the traditional systems of Gregory the Great (7 notes) and Guido of Arezzo (6 notes).</meta>

# Chapter Seven

We have asserted that they differ in sharpness <note>original: *acumine*; refers to high pitch</note> and gravity <note>original: *gravitate*; refers to low pitch</note>, and we have never denied it. Therefore, we have demonstrated both the difference and the similarity between them, since we have placed the same vowels of the letters—namely, **a**—showing their similarity and conformity. But by other letters—that is, **s** at the beginning or **c**—we have revealed the difference between sharpness and gravity. 

Once their difference is understood—since a sound is grave <note>low-pitched</note> when the breath is drawn from the depths of the chest, while a sharp <note>high-pitched</note> sound is emitted from the surface of the mouth—the more profound the pronunciation is near the lungs, the graver it sounds; and the closer it comes to the location of the teeth, the sharper it sounds. Thus, we recognize that the letter **r** joined with **a** is made by the contact of the tongue and the closure of the teeth. But we do not doubt, as we have declared, that **s** joined with **a** comes from the application of the tongue to the palate. 

It is clear, therefore, from what has been said, that the syllable **sal** is graver than **sas**, especially if we weigh the difference between gravity and sharpness in the pronunciation of **l** and **s** placed at the end of a syllable. It will be established that such a placement was most suitably made by us. For the liquid letter **l** naturally emits a grave sound, while the density of the letter **s**, being almost like a hiss, rises into sharpness. For no voice is sharper than a hiss.

If, however, there is an argument regarding the number—because our grouping of eight <note>original: *octonarius*; the author's eight-syllable system</note> does not seem to have as much value as the grouping of seven <note>original: *septenarius*</note> by which the world and the heavens revolve—for which reason Gregory <note>Pope Gregory the Great, traditionally associated with the seven-letter names of notes (A-G).</note> set down only seven letters; and since the eight is not of such perfection as the grouping of six <note>original: *senarius*; the hexachord system</note>, for the sake of which Guido <note>Guido of Arezzo, the 11th-century monk who developed the six-syllable solmization system Ut-Re-Mi-Fa-Sol-La.</note> reduced the voices to six—since, therefore, the six and the seven are more perfect than the eight, and since the same thing we do with eight can be done through them, those who followed the seven or the six will seem to have perceived things more correctly than we who follow the eight. 

It must be said that the number eight possesses great perfection and dignity in music, and it was not placed there in vain, but by necessity. First, its perfection is proven through comparison: for just as some prove the perfection of the seven because there are seven planets, we, by the same reasoning, prove the perfection of the eight. For when the firmament <note>The eighth sphere of the fixed stars in the medieval geocentric model.</note> is added to the seven planets, the number eight results. In their comparison, a graver error is committed because that which is more excellent—namely, the eighth sphere—is left out. For, if we grant silence <note>Referring to the Earth at the center of the universe, which was thought to be stationary and silent.</note>, just as the Earth with its elements is first, then the first and gravest sphere is the Moon, the second of Mercury, the third of Venus, the fourth of the Sun, the fifth of Mars, the sixth of Jupiter, the seventh of Saturn, and the eighth is the starry heaven...

<summary>Ramos de Pareja defends his eight-syllable solmization system by explaining the phonetic qualities of the syllables and justifying the number eight through a cosmological comparison to the eight celestial spheres.</summary>
<keywords>phonetics, pitch, octonarius, septenarius, senarius, Guido of Arezzo, Pope Gregory, celestial spheres, firmament, cosmology</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the phonetic and physiological arguments of the previous page to a mathematical and cosmological defense of the number eight. Ramos de Pareja invokes classical authorities like Cicero and Macrobius to prove that the "octonary" (eight) represents physical and musical perfection, justifying his eight-syllable solmization system against traditional six-syllable systems.</meta>

# Second Treatise

Marcus Tullius <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher.</note> does the same in the sixth book which he composed on the State <note>original: *De Re Publica*; specifically referring to the "Dream of Scipio" found in this book.</note>. He uses different words but conveys this same meaning. And this much pertains to the number seven; but as for the number six—since these are mathematical ratios—we shall say that mathematical bodies must be submitted to the mind and not to the senses. In these, the principle is the point, which has neither length, nor breadth, nor depth, nor height. This point, when drawn out, produces a line, which is of one dimension: namely, length without breadth or depth, and it is contained by two points bounding the length on either side. 

If you double this line, a second mathematical body is created which extends in two dimensions—namely in length and breadth—while lacking height; this is called a surface. And this is contained by four points, given that each of the two lines is assigned two points. If, however, these two lines are duplicated—as if two others were placed above the two beneath—depth is added, and thus a solid body is created, which is undoubtedly contained by eight angles. We can see this in a die or tile, which is called a *cubus* <note>Greek: *kubos*, meaning cube.</note> in the Greek name.

To these geometric principles let the natures of numbers be joined; for the <term>monad</term> <note>The number one, seen in Neopythagorean thought as the indivisible source of all numbers.</note> is thought of as the point, because just as the point is not a body but creates a body from itself, so the monad is not called a number but the origin of numbers. Therefore, the first number is the binary <note>two</note>, which is similar to a line drawn from a point under the double termination of points. This number—the binary—when doubled by itself makes four. The quaternary <note>four</note> likewise, when doubled, returns the octonary <note>eight</note>, which number imitates a solid body. 

For we have said that two lines placed above two lines create the complete solidity of a body through the dimension of eight angles. And this is what is said among geometers: "twice two, twice" <note>original: *bis bina bis*; the formula for a cube (2x2x2=8).</note> is a solid body. <term>Macrobius</term> <note>A 5th-century Neoplatonist whose commentary on Cicero’s *Dream of Scipio* was a primary source for medieval music theory and cosmology.</note> also mentions this in his book on the *Dream of Scipio*, using other words but containing the same meaning. And he concludes that, starting from an even number, the progression up to eight is the solidity of a body; and he continues, saying that among the first principles, they assign fullness to this number. 

We conclude, therefore, that whoever cuts off or reduces the eight voices from our music takes away perfection and fullness from it. Thus, the eighth number is not imperfect, but in music it is full and perfect, since it is the "container of the whole," and the whole harmony—as has often been said—is enclosed within eight voices. We do not, therefore, use the number eight in vain, but of necessity. So much for these things; now we hasten toward representing the voice with figures in plainchant.

<summary>Ramos uses Pythagorean geometry and the writings of Cicero and Macrobius to argue that the number eight represents the first "solid" perfection (a cube). He concludes that a musical system must therefore be based on eight voices to be complete and perfect.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, octonary, monad, binary, quaternary, Macrobius, Cicero, cube, musical perfection, solid body</keywords>
