<meta>This page transitions from a mathematical and geometric discussion of the number eight and the nature of the cube to a musical-theoretical investigation into the nature of the voice and sound. The author moves from abstract geometry to the physical properties of sound (acoustics), citing Boethius and Albinus to define how musical sound differs from speech.</meta>

# Second Treatise

->Second treatise in which it is shown how and in what manner the voice ought to be represented on a plane surface<-

**N**ow let us distinguish the musical voices <note>original: *uoces*; in this context, "voices" refers to musical sounds or pitches.</note>. Voice is the unbroken percussion of air reaching the sense of hearing. The human voice is twofold: one kind is continuous, while the other is truly discrete <note>Discrete here means "distinct" or "separate," referring to individual pitches rather than a sliding scale.</note>. Continuous voices are those which are joined by a common boundary, as if one were to strike a string and, while striking it, twist it; it would happen that in the beginning it sounds lower, and is continuously sharpened <note>Made higher in pitch.</note> more and more, and thus the sounds of the low and high voice become continuous, just as occurs in the groaning of the sick. The same also happens to certain people when reading, who, by continuing the voice while reading, gradually ascend or descend. Regarding such things—to speak in the words of Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524), the primary authority on music theory in the Middle Ages.</note>—we do not wish to treat them, since they are separate from the science of harmony. 

Discrete voices, however, have their own proper places. Therefore, both the sounds of instruments and harmonic voices are subjected to the art of music. In another way, Boethius also distinguishes the human voice into the continuous and discrete in his first book; for he says the continuous is that by which we strike words while speaking or reading prose. For then the voice hastens not to dwell on high or low sounds, but as swiftly as possible the impulse of the voice works to <unclear>bring out</unclear> the words, to expedite the senses, and to express the speech. But he calls that voice "discrete" which we suspend by singing, in which we serve not the speech but rather the <term>modulations</term> <note>Melodic patterns or intervals.</note>. And this voice is itself slower, making a certain interval through the varieties to be modulated—not an interval of silence or stillness, but rather of a slow and suspended song. 

To these, as Albinus <note>Likely Alcuin of York (c. 735–804), a scholar often called Albinus.</note> asserts, a third difference is added which can include the middle voices: namely, when we read the poems of heroes, which we pronounce neither with a continuous run like prose, nor with a suspended and slower draw of the voice like a song. Similarly, when we read prayers, chapters, lessons, and similar things in church, concerning which it will appear more clearly in the treatise on <term>tropes</term> <note>In this context, tropes likely refer to the different melodic formulas or "tones" used for liturgical recitation.</note>. 

Therefore, since the voice is of the category of successive things, it exists while it is being made, but when it has been made, it no longer exists. For that reason, it must be represented to the imagination by rules and figures. For a figure identical to the voice cannot be made, especially one depicted on a plane surface, since when it is brought forth, it is not produced in the manner of a flowing point constituting a line in length only, or in length and breadth like a line and a surface, but it diffuses in a circle and into space, so that it is heard by the ears through the six differences of position: that is, up, down, before, behind, <note>The text likely intended to include "left and right" to complete the six Aristotelian directions of space, but they are absent from this transcription.</note>

<summary>The author defines the human voice, distinguishing between the continuous sound of speech and the discrete, distinct pitches used in music. He cites Boethius and Albinus to categorize a third "middle" voice used for reciting heroic poetry and church liturgy.</summary>
<keywords>human voice, discrete voice, continuous voice, Boethius, Albinus, acoustics, musical notation, liturgical recitation</keywords>

# Second Treatise

...to the right and to the left. For Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524), the primary authority on music theory in the Middle Ages.</note> attempts to represent the voice by the image of a stone striking still water; once the stone is thrown, waves are stirred up and flee outward in a circle, so that it may be understood that the air is propelled by the voice just as the water is by the stone. Therefore, the voice is not [literally] shaped on a plane, but the elevation or intensity of the voice, and its depression or remission <note>terms: *intensio* (raising/tightening) and *remissio* (lowering/loosening) refer to the physical tension of vocal cords or strings.</note> are represented in a certain way. For just as in a geometric demonstration a drawn line—which is placed in the stead of a [theoretical] geometric line—does not lack the width that a geometric line is understood to lack, so in our music we show that very elevation or depression (which consists in an invisible point) through certain sensible intervals of notes <note>original: *notularum*</note>.

Therefore, when we look at the following notes starting from the low pitch <note>original: *gravitate*</note>, if we see them above the first, we are taught to raise the voice into the high pitch <note>original: *acumen*</note>. But if the second note is lower, we shall descend from the high to the low. And this method is to be observed in all the subsequent notes of the entire chant.

But since the degree to which the elevation or depression of a note should be held in the voice is not easily known, our contemporaries have devised an excellent method for this. For they decreed that four or five lines be extended. And from a line to a space, and from a space to a line, there is a progression just as the voices continuously follow and succeed one another in an instrument made by art <note>Such as a pipe or a monochord.</note>. And so that their distinct places may be known—due to the difference of intervals and the inequality of the gaps—one of those lines is marked with this sign or that one <insert>𝄡</insert> <note>The symbol for a C-clef or F-clef.</note>, the first of which demonstrates low F <note>original: *f. grave*</note> and the second demonstrates high C <note>original: *c. acutum*</note>. And these signs the moderns <note>original: *neotherici*; referring to medieval scholars who updated the older Greek systems.</note> are accustomed to call "keys" <note>term: *claues*; the origin of the modern word "clef."</note>, because they clearly demonstrate the locations.

We shall therefore arrange five lines with the middle of them marked by the first sign, which is F. Then *C*, which we called *sol*, falls in the space contained between the lower line and the following one; and on the first and second line *D*, which we called *la*; and in the space contained under the marked line *E*, which we called *fa*. Therefore, on this line is placed *F*, and in the space contained above it is *G*, which is *ut*; and it follows in the same way for the rest, namely *ut, re, mi, fa* <note>The author is describing the Guidonian hexachord system where pitches are mapped to these syllables.</note>, so that *sol* falls on the line; higher, there is another *C* where another key is located. But if we wish to do the same with this [sign] in a higher position, the same result will occur. But if that key is not placed higher, but in the following [position] below the first line, we shall place *sol*. And thus *ut* and *re* will fall on the marked line, which will also happen with the first, if it is placed on the fourth line from the top. Thus, neither the lines nor the spaces contained between the lines will always hold the same voice <note>pitch</note>, but rather according to whether the key is placed higher or lower.

<summary>This page explains the transition from the physics of sound (using Boethius's ripple metaphor) to the practical invention of the musical staff and clefs. It describes how "modern" theorists use lines, spaces, and "keys" (clefs) to fix the pitch of notes like F and C.</summary>

# Second Treatise

...will be raised; the lower or higher places will hold them. We shall therefore now mark a double octave <note>original: *duplicem diapason*</note>, one from the letter c to the high c, and another from that same high c to the super-high c, so that those beginning through various places may also note the pitches.

It is jumped through these voices: tas is ces uo uo per tur li fal

g. a. ♭ c. c. ♮ a. g. g. f. e. d. c. If, however, the reader cannot so easily run through the notes with the voices <note>The author refers to the vocalization of pitches.</note> in their rising or falling, let him return to the monochord <note>A teaching tool consisting of a single string stretched over a soundboard with a movable bridge, used to demonstrate mathematical musical intervals.</note> and, beginning from the third pitch, let him ascend to its octave, and let him climb to the third pitch, as was said in the chapter "Many Wishing" <note>original: *Multi volentes*</note>. Therefore, all that was said to be necessary with an instrument, he will know how to do in these exercises without it by looking at the notes.

->Explanation of feigned music, Second Chapter<-

**But in order** that a fuller knowledge of these signs and notes may be had, we shall investigate some matters concerning this more subtly. For other signs are usually placed in chants through which the distance of the intervals <note>original: *intercapedinū*</note> is known to be unequal: one is written as a round **♭**; the other is figured as a square **♮**. The first sign is called "soft b" <note>original: *b molle*; our modern flat sign.</note> or "round b"; the second is called "square b" <note>original: *b quadratum*; our modern natural or sharp sign</note> or "hard b" <note>original: *b durum*</note>. They are named "square" or "round" from the quality of their shape, but they are called "soft" or "hard" because when singers—using the letters of Gregory <note>The standard letter names A, B, C, D, E, F, G, attributed to Pope Gregory the Great.</note>—make a semitone between A and B, they call that B "soft." This is because when the jump occurs through a semitone during the rising <note>term: *arsis* — the upward movement or stress in a musical phrase.</note> and falling <note>term: *thesis* — the downward movement or relaxation in a musical phrase.</note> of the voice, that voice becomes softer than when it occurs through a whole tone. Just as A to a soft B is soft, and A to a square B is hard, so also a "semiditone" <note>term: *semiditonus* — a minor third.</note> is softer than a "ditone" <note>term: *ditonus* — a major third.</note>. For example, G to soft B is softer than G to square B. Similarly, a fourth <note>original: *diatessaron*</note> is softer than a tritone <note>An interval of three whole tones, historically called the "devil in music" due to its perceived harshness.</note>; for example, F to soft B is soft, while F to square B is very hard. 

From this, the error of certain singers who speak of "soft b" or "square b" is evident. They err in two ways: first, because they sing by the syllables of Guido <note>Guido of Arezzo (d. 1033), the monk who invented the "ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la" system.</note> and not by the letters of Gregory. Therefore, they should pronounce neither "soft b" nor "hard square b," but rather *fa* or *mi*. Second, they do not make the correct relationship; for when they say "square b," they ought to say "round b" correspondingly.

<summary>This page explains the use of the double octave and introduces the concept of "soft" and "hard" B (flats and naturals), clarifying how these signs affect the intervals and vocal quality of a chant.</summary>
<keywords>diapason, monochord, musica ficta, b molle, b quadratum, semitone, arsis, thesis, Gregory, Guido of Arezzo, intervals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Second Treatise," specifically the second chapter titled "Explanation of feigned music." It expands on the use of accidentals—b-flat and b-natural—comparing medieval practice with ancient Greek theory and citing contemporary theorists like Philipetus and Johannes Tinctoris.</meta>

# First Part

And when they say "soft b" <note>original: *b molle*; the b-flat</note>, they ought to say "hard b" <note>original: *b durum*; the b-natural</note> <insert>♮</insert>, and thus the correct relationship will be made. This was the custom among the ancients when they sang using Gregorian letters, for which there are specific names, just as the Greeks had their own terms like *synemenon* <note>The "conjunct" tetrachord in the Greek system, which introduced a flat to avoid a tritone.</note> or *diazeugmenon* <note>The "disjunct" tetrachord, representing the natural scale.</note>. But for those of us who sing using our own utterances <note>solmization syllables like ut, re, mi, etc.</note>, the specific names will be "b" if it is in a conjunct <note>the soft or flat hexachord</note> and "b" <insert>♮</insert> if it is in a disjunct <note>the hard or natural hexachord</note>. Truly, the terms used to create a tone or a semitone are common to all.

But singers do not only mark tones or semitones with these signs at the *paramese* <note>the note B</note>; for they say that wherever "fa" is found without "mi," a "mi" must be made there, as in the pitch b-fa-<insert>♮</insert>-mi. The same applies where there is "mi" without "fa," which many call "feigned music" <term>musica ficta: the use of notes outside the standard medieval hexachord system, essentially using accidentals</term>. Regarding this, a certain Philipetus, speaking rashly, says: "There is only one feigned music." However, he was ignorant of the fact that it must be done in at least two ways: for making "fa" from "mi" is a different method than making "mi" from "fa," as will be said shortly. This is because the voices should correspond to the way they are naturally placed. 

Therefore, when "fa" must be made from "mi," they write it with this sign, namely the "round b" <note>♭</note>. But when "mi" is made from "fa," they mark it with this sign <insert>♮</insert>, namely the "square b." When this "soft b" <insert>♭</insert> is placed, it will be in five locations according to them: namely in b-mi, in e-la-mi, in the first a-la-mi-re, in high e-la-mi, and in the second a-la-mi-re. In these places, we shall say "fa" by a semitone lowered from its proper place. 

But this <insert>♭</insert> or this <insert>♮</insert> may also be placed in c-fa-ut, in f-fa-ut, in c-sol-fa-ut, in high f-fa-ut, and in c-sol-fa. In these locations, we shall say "mi" by a semitone raised from its proper place. They also call these "conjuncts" <note>Latin: *coniunctas*; a technique of transposed hexachords</note> for this reason: just as when the *trite synemenon* is placed after the *mese* <note>The middle note of the Greek scale</note>, causing the tone between the *mese* and *paramese* to be divided into two semitones, so too must any other tone located elsewhere be divided. 

They further add that any of these conjuncts is a hexachord <note>original: *exachordum*; a six-note scale</note>, just like the others previously established. Therefore, just as after f-fa-ut (in which "ut" is said) g-sol-re-ut follows, where "ut" is placed again because of the established rules, so it is in each of these locations. They define it in this way: a conjunct is to make a tone from a semitone and a semitone from a tone; likewise, a ditone <note>major third</note> from a semiditone <note>minor third</note>, and a semiditone from a ditone, and similarly for other musical intervals. And thus they speak rightly, because these conjunct hexachords behave in the manner of the *diazeugmenon* and *synemenon* tetrachords. 

Johannes Tinctoris <note>A famous Flemish music theorist (c. 1435–1511) known for his strict definitions.</note>, being far from true knowledge, says this: "A conjunct is the positioning of the soft b [♭] or hard b [♮] in an irregular place." For if the sign of the soft b were placed in c-sol-fa-ut, or in another place where "fa" would be irregular, it would be so positioned.

<summary>This page discusses the theory and notation of musica ficta (feigned music), explaining how the signs for b-flat and b-natural are used to create semitones and "conjunct" hexachords. The author critiques the definitions provided by other theorists like Philipetus and Johannes Tinctoris, arguing for a more nuanced understanding of how intervals are raised and lowered.</summary>
<keywords>musica ficta, b-flat, b-natural, conjunct, hexachord, Philipetus, Johannes Tinctoris, semitone, solmization, music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "First Part" of the treatise, moving from the general definition of accidentals and "feigned music" into a technical explanation of how the hexachord system—represented by the Guidonian Hand—is expanded using "conjunctae." It specifically discusses the theoretical pitch "retropolis" and the mathematical perfection of the three-octave system.</meta>

# First Part

...and yet it would not be a *conjuncta* <note>A "conjuncta" refers to the transposition of a hexachord to a non-standard starting pitch, which introduces an accidental like B-flat or F-sharp into the scale.</note>. Thus, if a square-b <note>original: *b quadratum*; the natural sign ♮</note> is placed where "mi" is, and if a round-b <note>original: *b rotundum*; the flat sign ♭</note> is placed in the pitch B-mi, a *conjuncta* is formed. And yet, this is an irregular location for that B, because it is an octave from the round-b. Therefore, when "fa" is placed in B-mi—at the eighth position from the "fa" that is placed in the *trite synemenon* <note>A specific pitch in the Greek Lesser Perfect System, equivalent to the note B-flat.</note>—it resounds in the harmony of an octave <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term>.

It will have the other seven voices responding one-by-one in the harmony of an octave, resonating with the others. Just as the "ut" of the *synemenon* <note>the "conjunct" tetrachord of Greek theory</note> is exceeded by a whole tone by the "ut" of the *diazeugmenon* <note>the "disjunct" tetrachord</note>, so too the "ut" of this system will be a tone below *Gamma-ut* <note>The lowest note of the standard medieval gamut, a low G</note>, which they call *retropolis* <note>Literally "behind the pole"; a theoretical pitch one step below the standard starting point of the scale.</note>.

Because *Gamma-ut* is placed at the tip of the thumb <note>The "Guidonian Hand" was a mnemonic tool where each joint of the thumb and fingers represented a specific pitch.</note>, this *retropolis* is placed behind it, specifically at the first outer joint of the finger. It is followed by "re" in *Gamma-ut*, "mi" in A-re, and "fa" in B-mi; but "sol" is in C-fa-ut, and "la" in D-sol-re. Likewise, for the "mi" placed in C-fa-ut to be raised, "ut" is placed in A-re, and this hexachord <term>hexachord: a six-note scale (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la) that formed the basis of medieval music</term> is completed in F-fa-ut.

Furthermore, those *conjunctae* which lower a voice from its proper place by a semitone are called "soft b" <note>flat</note> *conjunctae* by these authors; but those that are raised by the same semitone are called "square b" <note>natural or sharp</note> *conjunctae*.

For a brief understanding of any *conjuncta*, this is the method: once the location of the *conjuncta* is known, we should count backward from that point through the joints [of the hand], saying "fa, mi, re, ut" if it is a soft-b, or "mi, re, ut" if it is a square-b. Thus, whoever examines everything said here will see the [Guidonian] hand correctly arranged in this way.

Namely: "ut" in *retropolis*; "ut" and "re" in *Gamma-ut*; "ut," "re," and "mi" in A-re; "ut," "re," "mi," and "fa" in B-mi; and in C-fa-ut, "ut," "re," "mi," "fa," and "sol"; and in D-sol-re, "ut," "re," "mi," "fa," "sol," and "la." From there up to the second A-la-mi-re, we will find these six voices repeated in all signs or locations.

After A-la-mi-re, it resolves; for that which began first, finishes first. And so, in the second B-fa-♮-mi, there will be "re," "mi," "fa," "sol," and "la"; in C-sol-fa-mi: "fa," "sol," and "la"; in D-la-sol: "fa," "sol," and "la"; and in E-la: "sol" and "la." Afterward, they place another voice, namely "la," which is a whole tone distant from the previous one because it depends on the last of the square-b joints. And thus, there were twenty-two locations <note>distinct pitches or positions on the Hand</note>.

Therefore, after the addition of these *conjunctae*, the hand is called "perfect," because the whole is correctly divided by semitones. They call it perfect because it encompasses three octaves <note>original: *trium diapason*</note>. Indeed, the greatest perfection is denoted by the number three, because the whole is equal to its parts, both quotient and aliquot, taken together, and no other number claims this dignity for itself <note>The author is applying medieval mathematical philosophy, which viewed certain numbers as "perfect" if they equaled the sum of their divisors.</note>.

But they only speak well in part—namely, that the number three is perfect—yet in this they err: because the [Guidonian] hand is not actually three octaves, as we shall show shortly. From these things which have been said, readers will be able to consider how confusion arose from the teaching of Guido <note>Guido of Arezzo, the famous 11th-century music theorist.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the expansion of the Guidonian Hand through "conjunctae," introducing the theoretical pitch retropolis and defining perfect intervals and scales using medieval numerology.</summary>
<keywords>Guidonian Hand, hexachord, conjuncta, retropolis, diapason, semitone, b-flat, b-natural, musical perfection, Guido of Arezzo</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the First Part into the "Second Treatise," shifting from the theoretical construction of the scale to the practical application of solmization. It continues the discussion of Guido d'Arezzo’s system, specifically focusing on "mutation"—the technique of switching between hexachords to navigate melodies that exceed a six-note range.</meta>

### Second Treatise

...is. For he <note>Referring to Guido d'Arezzo, the 11th-century monk who developed the solmization system.</note> himself considered that wherever a semitone was to be pronounced, singers ought to utter "mi" and "fa" <note>In the medieval system, the semitone (half-step) always occurred between the syllables *mi* and *fa*.</note>. Because of this, he placed the hexachords <term>hexachord: a six-note scale segment (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la)</term> more frequently in turns within the tetrachords, both conjunct and disjunct. For this reason, he necessarily placed two or three syllables in one and the same location, as has been shown above. But we have demonstrated mathematically, having taken his foundation, that those same six syllables are necessarily composed in the aforementioned locations. But enough has been said thus far concerning the confusion of voices in one location arising from Guido's art; next, however, we must discuss more subtly which of them are to be uttered and which are to be withheld—what his followers call "mutations."

### Chapter Four: On the permutation of voices

Having recognized such a variation of voices <note>original: *vocū*; here meaning the solmization syllables</note>, it remains for us to learn precisely why they are placed together in one location: whether we ought to pronounce all of them at once, or whether we should take one while dismissing the others in that same spot. Regarding this, it should be noted that three of these are called "ascending," namely *ut, re, mi*; but three are "descending," namely *fa, sol, la*. Whence Guido's followers say: "*ut, re, mi* climb; *fa, sol, la* descend."

They teach us that when a song ascends to the heights, we should take an ascending syllable in place of a descending one. For example, if we are on *Mese* <note>The central pitch of the Greek system, corresponding to the note A.</note> with the syllable "la," and the song seeks higher places, according to their doctrine we ought to dismiss "la" and take up "re" or "mi." Then, with "re" or with "mi," we will be able to reach the higher places more easily. Likewise, when we are on *Paranete Synemenon* <note>The pitch D in the Greek "conjunct" system.</note> or *Trite Diazeugmenon* <note>The pitch C in the "disjunct" system.</note> with "ut," and the song glides down to the depths, they command us to dismiss "ut" and take "sol" or "fa," and thus we will be able to descend while singing with "fa" or "sol."

And this is what they call mutation, saying: "Mutation is the variation of one voice into another." Others, however, define it thus: "Mutation is the variation of two voices equal to each other in pitch, occurring in one sign <note>The letter-name of the note (e.g., G or A).</note> and one voice, through different properties <note>The three "properties" or types of hexachords: Natural (starting on C), Hard (starting on G), and Soft (starting on F).</note>."

Furthermore, permutation occurs in two ways: either for the sake of the necessity of climbing or falling, or for the sake of placing a semitone before or after. We know that these are always made in syllables that belong to the same "squares" <note>A technical term likely referring to the grouping of syllables that share the same direction—either both ascending or both descending.</note>; that is, both ascending or both descending. The other type, however, is not like that, but is composed of one ascending and one descending syllable. Therefore, mutation will be threefold: one type for ascending, another for descending, and a third that comprises both. Specifically, that which occurs in ascending is composed of ascending syllables, such as in *G-sol-re-ut*: "re" into "ut" or "ut" into "re"; or in *A-la-mi-re*...

<summary>This page explains the theory of "mutation," the method by which medieval singers switched between hexachords to sing melodies covering a wide range. It defines which solmization syllables are used for ascending versus descending and provides the formal definitions of mutation used by music theorists.</summary>
<keywords>solmization, hexachord, mutation, Guido d'Arezzo, ut re mi fa sol la, Mese, music theory, semitone, vocal pedagogy</keywords>

# First Part

...**mi re re mi** and in its octaves. What indeed occurs in descending just as many times: that is certainly [the case] which consists of descending voices: just as in **C sol fa ut**: **sol fa fa sol**. In **D la sol re**: **la sol sol la**: and in its octaves. Thus it always occurs between voices <note>original: *voces*; refers here to the solmization syllables ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la.</note> distant by a tone according to the sequence. I say "according to the sequence" because, as was said above, just as **ut** is distant from **re** by a tone, so from **re** to **mi**; likewise **fa** from **sol** and **sol** from **la**. But when a mutation occurs, they are placed in an equal location <note>Meaning they occupy the same pitch/line on the musical staff.</note>.

The third mutation, however, occurs in two ways: either in voices which, placed in sequence, are distant by a fourth <term>diatessaron: the musical interval of a perfect fourth</term> or by a fifth <term>diapente: the musical interval of a perfect fifth</term>. By a fourth in three ways: namely, **fa ut, ut fa**; **sol re, re sol**; **la mi, mi la**. By a fifth, however, in two ways: namely **la re, re la**; **sol ut, ut sol**. Therefore, when the "hand" <note>The Guidonian Hand, a visual mnemonic used by medieval singers to map the notes of the scale onto the joints of the left hand.</note> is incomplete, such as in **Gamma ut**, or in **B mi**, or in **E la**, a permutation cannot occur; because it does not belong to one voice but to two equal ones. For this reason, it also does not occur in either **B fa** or <term>♮</term> **mi** <note>original: *b fa ♮ mi*; this refers to the "split" note B, which could be sung as either B-flat (fa) or B-natural (mi).</note>.

In the first art inspected, where there are only two equal voices, two mutations will occur: one from the first voice into the second, and the other conversely. But when there are three, they will hold this manner: from the first to the second and vice versa makes two; from the first to the third and vice versa another two; and from the second to the third and vice versa again another two; and thus six will be had. But when we name the second voice as "ascending," they say the mutation is caused "in ascending." But further, they add the property of that voice: namely, **square B** <note>original: *b quadrati*; the symbol for B-natural and the "hard" hexachord.</note>, or **soft B** <note>original: *b mollis*; the symbol for B-flat and the "soft" hexachord.</note>, or **natural**. 

They call "natural" the hexachords <term>hexachord: a six-note scale segment (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la)</term> which begin on either **C**; **square B** those from **G**; and **soft B** those from **F**. So that we may conclude with one example through which the knowledge of others may easily be reached: we say in **G sol re ut**: **sol re, re sol, sol ut, ut sol, re ut, ut re**. **Sol re** in ascending from natural to soft B. **Re sol** in descending from soft B into natural. **Sol ut** in ascending from natural to square B. **Ut sol** by the contrary. **Re ut** in ascending from soft B into hard B. **Ut re** in ascending from square B into soft B <note>original: *b. rotundū*; "round B," another name for the flat or soft B.</note>. In this way, having preserved the method in other places, anyone will be able to investigate these permutations for themselves through what has been said.

But having this foundation in the "complete hand," one can easily make all mutations from all six voices placed in the same location. Whence, for the sake of example, let us take **D sol re**, where six voices were located. Let us join **la** in the aforementioned way, namely with voices which are distant in order by a tone, or a fourth, or a fifth. We shall say **la sol, sol la**, and we will have two. Then with **mi**, namely **la mi, mi la**; and there will be four. Then with **re**, because by joining through a fifth we again make two through...

It details the specific syllable changes required when moving between the natural, hard (square B), and soft (flat B) hexachords.</summary>

## Second Treatise

...mutations, namely: **la re, re la**: <note>original: *mutationes s. la re re la*</note> therefore, we do not doubt that they arise through [the process] where, when [one syllable] is dismissed, **sol** is taken. And in the same way, six [mutations] will occur: namely **sol fa, fa sol**; **sol re, re sol**; **sol ut, ut sol**; **la sol**, provided that **fa** is taken. That [syllable **fa**] can only be joined with the voice **ut**; and there will be fourteen [mutations]. But **mi** combined with **re**, and **re** combined with **ut**, produce four. Wherever, therefore, six voices <note>The term "voices" (voces) refers to the six solmization syllables: ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la.</note> are found, there will be twelve [mutations]; in **B mi** only six; but four in **A re**; in **gamma-ut** you will have only two. In the same way, you will have **B fa ♮ mi** secondarily, just as **C fa ut**; and **♮ ela** <note>The high E at the top of the scale.</note> just as **gamma-ut**; and thus for the other signs or locations throughout these extents.

### Chapter Five: Refuting certain previous theories and demonstrating the correct method of conjunctae.

<note>A vertical diagram or table of the Guidonian gamut showing the relationships between different hexachords—natural, and those shifted or "accidental"—through solmization syllables. This allows a singer to visualize how "accidental" notes (the *conjunctae*) relate to the standard notes of the musical scale.</note>

Now that the "hand" <note>The Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic used to teach the musical scale.</note> has been perfectly arranged, and the form of those things required for its perfection has been prescribed above, some matters remain for us to investigate more subtly. For although it may be said that from **D sol re** all the way to the second **a la mi re**, six voices [syllables] are found in any given place; and from those same six voices, eighteen mutations are caused by the turns—this is indeed true regarding the turns, but not at all regarding the mutations.

For the sake of clarity, let a figure be arranged with the syllables of <term>Guidonis</term> <note>Guido of Arezzo (d. 1033), the monk credited with inventing the solmization system (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la).</note> from <term>gamaut</term> <note>Gamma-ut, the lowest note of the medieval musical system (low G).</note> to **ela**; this is called the **natural order**, because the voices are arranged naturally, just as they arise from the division of the regular <term>monochord</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used to measure the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note>. But let this same figure be arranged on its left side, raised by a tone <note>original: *trono*, likely a scribal error for *tono* (tone).</note>; and likewise to the right, lowered by the same tone.

From this arrangement, we shall surely find any tone of the natural order divided from its accidental counterpart. By this division, every perfect instrument ought to be divided. For from <term>are</term> <note>The note A, one step above Gamma.</note> to <term>b mi</term> <note>The note B.</note> there is naturally a tone, because it is **re mi**. But since, from the accidental order, the voice **mi** lowered by a tone is equal to that natural **re**, it follows that the voice **fa** (which follows that voice **mi**) does not reach the **mi** of the natural order, since the former makes a <term>semitonium</term> <note>A semitone or half-step, the smallest interval in the system.</note> and the latter intends a whole tone.

Furthermore, since from **b mi** to <term>c fa ut</term> of the natural order there is a semitone (because it is **mi fa**), and from the **re** of the left accidental [order] (which is equal to that **mi**) there follows a **mi** raised by a tone, it follows that it will be higher by a semitone than the voice **fa** of the natural [order]. And thus the natural tone which is sung from **c fa ut** to <term>d sol re</term> remains divided into two semitones. Again, since from **d sol re** to <term>elami</term> there is naturally a tone (because it is **re mi** or **sol la**), and the **mi** of the right accidental [order] is equal to that natural **re** or **sol**, the voice **fa** follows. This will make a semitone, dividing that tone which is between **d sol re** and **e la mi**, and it will not reach the voice **mi** of the natural order; just as the subtle reader says in these letters in this <term>semitetrachord</term> <note>A four-note scale segment containing a semitone.</note>. Anyone can do this by inspecting the figure placed above.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical theory, transitioning from the rules of hexachord mutations to the mathematical measurement of the monochord. It specifically addresses how to calculate "conjunctae"—notes outside the standard "natural" scale, similar to our modern flats and sharps—by dividing the string into precise ratios.</meta>

<header>Part One</header>

# Part One

Someone might say, however, that although these conjunctae <note>original: *coniunctae*; these are chromatic "accidental" notes that allowed singers to shift the six-note scale (hexachord) to different pitches.</note> can be created at will, the measurement of them cannot. To this we say briefly: they will be created as clearly and with as little effort as the natural order itself was created in the first figure. To examine this, let the first measured figure be arranged; then we will create the **soft B** <note>original: *b mollis*; the origin of the modern flat symbol (♭), used here to mean the calculated flat notes.</note> conjunctae in this manner. 

By doubling the quantity of **Q** <note>A point of measurement on the monochord string.</note>, we will mark the first **soft B** in the junction; thus the first **B** will be between **a** and **b**. Then, if the length of the string between **I** and the first **B** is divided in half, the second **B** shall be marked, which will be between **d** and **e**. If you divide that second **B** quantity in the middle, we will mark the fourth **B** between **l** and **m**. But if the length between the fourth **B** and the second **B** is divided in the middle, we will mark the third **B**. If that quantity is further divided, the fifth **B** will be marked. We will therefore have five **soft B** conjunctae from this division, calculated by straight division.

However, if we endeavor to have the **square B** <note>original: *b quadrati*; the origin of the natural (♮) and sharp (♯) symbols, used here to mean the calculated natural or sharped notes.</note> conjunctae, we will divide by three from point **Q**. Coming from the letter **Q** toward **b**, at the end of the third part <note>original: *triētis*; the one-third mark.</note> we will place the fourth **♮**, namely between **n** and **o**; and at the two-thirds mark <note>original: *bes*</note> the second **square ♮**, which falls between **f** and **g**. But if the quantity of the second **square ♮** is divided by three, coming from letter **Q** toward the second **square ♮**, we will place the third **♮** at the two-thirds mark; it will be between **k** and **l**. If the quantity of this and **Q** is doubled, the first **♮** will result, marked in **f**. We will have, therefore, from this division, four **square ♮** conjunctae resulting from straight division, as is evident in the figure.

If we wish to have a fifth, we would divide the third **square ♮** quantity in the middle, and it would be above **p** by two tones. Truly, because we do not wish to add anything below the *proslambanomenos* <note>The lowest note of the ancient Greek musical system, corresponding to A.</note> nor above the *nete hyperbolaion* <note>The highest note of the ancient Greek musical system.</note> in the unmeasured figure, we do not place it. This is not because it could not be done, but because the ancients held to this, and we find this doctrine handed down by Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose works on music were the standard textbooks for the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>. Therefore, when we wish to add something below or above, we can do so not on the same string, but on different ones, harmonizing those strings with these divisions correctly made in an octave <note>original: *diapason*; the interval of an eighth.</note> relationship. 

For example, if we wish to add one string below the *proslambanomenos*, we will arrange it so that it sounds in an octave with the *lichanos meson* <note>A specific pitch in the Greek system, roughly equivalent to G.</note>, and it will be **Gamma-ut** <note>The lowest note of the medieval "Gamut" scale.</note>. If we add another below that, it will sound in an octave with the *parhypate meson* <note>Roughly equivalent to F.</note>; a fifth <note>original: *diapente*</note> with the *parhypate hypaton*; and a fourth <note>original: *diathessarōn*</note> with the first conjuncta. This string is what the moderns call the *retropolis* <note>Literally "behind the city," a term for notes added below the traditional bottom of the scale.</note>, as we have already said above. Almost all modern instruments that we find in Italy, which are many-stringed <note>original: *pollichorda*</note>, begin on this, including organs and other complete instruments that are divided by *syntonia* <note>A system of tuning or temperament.</note>. In our Spain, however, we find that old monochords and even organs began on great **C**. But the many-stringed instruments and organs of the moderns place eight voices below great **C** in the natural order, though not...

<note>A vertical diagram illustrates a monochord musical scale. It shows the proportional divisions of a string, labeled with letters from Q through r. These markings indicate the specific points where the string must be divided to produce "b mollis" (flats) and "b quadratum" (naturals or sharps), providing a visual mathematical proof for the tuning system described in the text.</note>

It transitions from the physical measurement of the string to the vocal rules of "mutation"—the shifting between scales during a song.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>Second Treatise</header>

## Second Treatise

...they have conjunct voices <note>original: *voces coniunctas*; notes that fall outside the standard natural scale, essentially flats and sharps.</note> of the **square B** <note>original: *b quadrati*; the "natural" or "sharp" sign (♮).</note> or **soft B** <note>original: *b mollis*; the "flat" sign (♭).</note> below the *proslambanomenos* <note>The lowest note of the ancient Greek musical system, corresponding to the A below the bass clef.</note> no; but there is only a direct fifth <note>original: *diapente*.</note> below *Gamma-ut*: so that *Gamma-ut* is the octave of *G-sol-re-ut*, and an octave—that is, a *diapason*—of *F-fa-ut*, and another *diapason* of *E-la-mi*, and another of *D-sol-re*, and another of *C-fa-ut*. The octave below *D-sol-re*—that is, the *diapason*—we have already found here in Bologna on the polychord <note>original: *pollichordum*; likely a monochord with multiple strings for comparison.</note>, but below *C-fa-ut* [it is found] only in Spain. However, it does not matter where one begins, as long as the measurements of the strings and the divisions of the semitones and tones are observed.

These tetrachords <note>four-note scale segments</note> relate to one another just like the *synemmenon* <note>original: *synemenon*; the "conjunct" tetrachord in the Greek system which allowed for a flat note.</note> and the *diazeugmenon* <note>the "disjunct" tetrachord which used a natural/sharp note.</note>. Therefore, it is because of this that our contemporaries call them "conjunct," but they also improperly call them "disjunct" <note>original: *disiunctas*.</note> when they transfer from one property <note>A "property" refers to which of the three types of hexachords—Natural, Hard, or Soft—is being used.</note> to another without a mutation <note>A "mutation" is the formal pivot where a singer changes the syllable of a note to shift scales (e.g., treating a G as "sol" in the C-scale and then as "re" to move into the F-scale).</note>. For example, if you were at *C-sol-fa-ut* and said *fa*, and were immediately forced to descend to *F-fa-ut* and then to lower notes; then that descent is called "disjunct," because they pronounce *fa* in the higher voice and *fa* in the lower. Likewise, whenever a leap is made across an octave <note>original: *diapason*</note>, wherever it may be, it will always necessarily be disjunct. 

I said "necessarily" regarding the octave because a fifth <note>original: *diapente*</note> is not always necessarily disjunct; but only when the fifth is <unclear>diminished</unclear> <note>original: *mi mītuz*</note>, as from *fa* to *fa* in *C* and *K*. But if it happens from *A-la-mi-re* where you are on *re* and you descend by a leap of a fifth, immediately that *re* is mutated into *la*, and it is called *re-la-re*, because then that *re* follows well from that *la*. Likewise, in *G-sol-re-ut* <note>original: *geristes*</note> with *ut*, when a leap of a fifth is made immediately, it is not disjunct but is mutated into *sol*, and it is called *ut-sol-ut*, because *la-ut* follows well from that *sol*. 

An immediate tritone <note>original: *tritonus*; an interval of three whole tones, considered dissonant and difficult to sing in the Middle Ages.</note> always causes disjunct voices; for if a leap is made from *F* up to *C-sol-fa-ut* passing through a single note, then it is said in *F-fa* and in *B-fa-natural-mi* <note>original: *b fa ♮ mi*</note>, and *C-fa* follows, and then it is called disjunct, because that *mi* neither follows nor depends on that lower *fa*. Other leaps that are larger than a fifth always create disjuncts, both in ascending and in descending, except where *la* can be taken in the hexachord; as if at *A-la-mi-re* *re* or *mi* were moved by a leap to *C-fa-ut*, then *la* must be taken... otherwise, above a fifth, it will always be disjunct.

But we must see what was to be proven: whether eighteen mutations can be made in each of the locations through the conjunct voices. If this is looked at well, it will be very easily recognized in the figure. For only in *D-sol-re*, *G-sol-re-ut*, and their octaves will eighteen mutations occur; because in these places alone are those six voices <note>The syllables Ut, Re, Mi, Fa, Sol, La.</note> placed equally, as appears in the figure. In other places, however, because not all voices meet on the same line—some being higher, others lower—there will not be eighteen in all. In *E-la-mi*, therefore, where *fa* and *ut* are lower, there are not eighteen, but there will only be twelve <note>original: *i 2*, likely a transcription of *xii*.</note> in this manner: from *la*... from *sol* only two... <unclear>re-sol</unclear>: because it can be paired <note>original: *biari*, likely *binari*</note> neither with *fa* nor with *ut*. From *mi* paired with *re*, there are another two, and so on... because between equals <unclear>others</unclear>... we shall have others.

<summary>This page explains the theory of "conjunct" and "disjunct" melodic movement, defining how singers must mutate between hexachords during leaps, particularly across fifths, octaves, and tritones. It also discusses the mathematical possibility of having eighteen different syllable-shifts (mutations) at specific pitches on the musical scale.</summary>

# First Part

...two; and so there will be twelve. Similarly, in *F-fa-ut*, where the syllables *la* and *mi* are equal, we will still have twelve in this manner: *la-mi* and *mi-la*, though there are only two actual notes. But we do not doubt that these arise from the *sol* of the "soft" or flat hexachord <note>original: *sol b*; referring to the hexachord starting on F, which requires a B-flat.</note>. It is certain that four mutations arise from the combination of *fa* with *ut*, and *re* with *ut*. Therefore, there are twelve such mutations in *E-la-mi*; but in *A-la-mi-re*, where only *fa* is unequal, there will be fourteen. This is because four others must be made with it, namely: *sol-fa*, *fa-sol*, and *fa-ut*, *ut-fa*. And similarly in *C-sol-fa-ut*. But in *B-fa-natural-mi* <note>original: *b fa ♮ mi*</note> there are twelve. And so it is in their octaves, as anyone can correctly see for themselves.

It appears, therefore, from these things, that mutations <note>The process of changing from one six-note scale (hexachord) to another to reach higher or lower notes.</note> do not occur in all eighteen places; thus the flaw of the "perfect" Hand <note>The Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic tool used by medieval singers to map the notes of the scale onto the joints of the hand.</note> becomes apparent. It is not perfect across three octaves <note>original: *diapason*</note>, because it exceeds them by a semitone <note>original: *semitonium*</note>. For it would be certain and perfect if that last voice of the accidental order on the left were distant by only a semitone from its neighbor. 

And then it would go against Guido <note>Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the monk credited with inventing the staff and the solmization system.</note> himself and his followers, who say that between *sol* and *la* there is not a whole tone <note>original: *tonus*</note>, but only a semitone distance. It should be noted, therefore, that singing through the "accidental" order is sometimes the same as singing through the "natural" order. The only difference lies in the shapes of the notes and the letters—specifically, that the semitone does not fall in the same place as it does in the natural order. 

For if in the natural order the semitone was from E to F, by placing the sign **b** <note>the flat sign (♭)</note> on that same E, the note is lowered, and the semitone occurs from the letter D to E. Likewise, if the **♮** <note>the square B or natural sign</note> is placed on F, it is raised, and the semitone occurs from F to G. Truly, when we wish to sing through any accidental order, the semitone follows after two whole tones; thus it is in the "conjunct" <note>original: *coniunctum*</note> and the "disjunct" <note>original: *disiunctum*</note> tetrachord <note>A four-note scale segment.</note>, which was called **feigned music** <note>original: *musica ficta*; the practice of using notes outside the standard medieval gamut, essentially sharps and flats.</note>. 

And then one must beware of the natural scale just as much as the accidental, since the followers of Guido who are our contemporaries do not act truly through the natural order. Instead, they frequently transfer themselves from the accidental to the natural and vice versa. Some do this perhaps by chance because they do not understand the underlying structure of the song; others, however, do it intentionally and shrewdly. But those who do this intentionally mark the letter and the space with this sign **b** <note>flat</note>, or this **♮** <note>natural</note>, or this **#** <note>sharp</note>. 

And then they follow that sign while singing. However, they say that if a sign is placed at the beginning of the music, that scale must be observed throughout the entire song. But if it is not at the beginning but placed during the course of the melody, they say that only that note to which the sign is applied is subject to it. From this, they make various considerations in raising notes from their proper place or lowering them. 

For if in *B-mi* the note is marked with this sign **b** <note>flat</note>, then an *a* follows it; if in *C* it is marked with this **#** <note>sharp</note>, even though in the natural order there is a larger gap, because of the lowering of the first note and the raising of the second, it becomes a semitone interval. This is also the case in all places where semitone distances occur. 

Thus, if a melody in *E-la-mi* was sung with this sign **b** <note>flat</note>, and from *A* to *C* with this **#** <note>sharp</note>, the interval of a major third <note>original: *ditonus*; literally "two tones."</note> is converted into the distance of a whole tone <note>original: *tonus*</note>. They teach that the same should be done in whatever places the aforementioned distances occur, and for other larger types of intervals. They say such an order should be kept so that the sign of the **square B** <note>original: *b quadratum*; the ancestor of the natural and sharp signs.</note> is always placed in the location of the sharped "conjuncts," and this **b** <note>the "round B" or flat sign</note> in those which are flatted "conjuncts."

The author critiques the limitations of the traditional Guidonian Hand and discusses how contemporary singers navigate between natural and accidental scales.</summary>

# Treatise on Musical Practice <note>original: "Tra: scas"; likely an abbreviation for *Tractatus secundus* or a similar title regarding musical practice.</note>

...is placed: Johannes de Londoniis and other less practiced scholars say that just as in **B-fa-natural-mi** <note>original: *b fa ♮ mi*; the position in the musical scale where both B-flat (fa) and B-natural (mi) can occur.</note> both signs can be located, so too can they be in other places. Where neither *fa* nor *mi-natural* can be produced, I do not think one should attempt it. Therefore, if for the reason already stated, the two semitones are divided into two parts, the remaining conventions are frustrated. For this is permitted, as has been said, so that we may have a tone and a semitone in any voice, just as they are said to be in **B-fa-natural-mi**. 

For if in the explanation there is a signed syllable with a preceding **b** <note>a "soft" b or B-flat</note>, and in the same place there stands a preceding **natural** <note>original: ♮; a "hard" b or B-natural</note>, although they may seem to be the same pitch, they differ because of the lowering of the semitone and the raising of the semitone, just like *mi* and *fa*. They speak wrongly for two reasons. First, because even if the preceding sign were not there, the second would still be higher. Second, they err because if one is lowered from that location by one semitone and the second is raised from that same location by another semitone, they are different pitches. Therefore, they are separated by the distance of a whole tone, not a semitone. 

They say the same regarding **D-sol-re** and its octaves. These things we have mentioned are to be found in the works of musicians who use three lines, such that they extend four or five lines and mark some of those lines with signs of the two aforementioned types, and arrange the notes with signs in this manner. From this begins the divergence from the teaching of Guido <note>Guido d'Arezzo, the father of modern musical notation</note>. Those who follow it with stubbornness fall into error, and they hold it as certain that no semitone can be made between *fa* and *mi*. They say this because *mi*, by closing the mouth, denotes obscurity, while *fa*, by relaxing the mouth, signifies brightness. But I will show that these reasons are nothing to those who have mastered music and mathematics. I shall take care to show that the difference is not unequal.

---

Musical difference consists in quantity or magnitude or strength of the voice. In the art of Guido, the properties of three voices <note>the three hexachords: natural, hard (with B-natural), and soft (with B-flat)</note> are placed as differing among themselves. It is not necessary to place a difference between equal voices. For in **G-sol-re-ut**, the *sol* of the natural hexachord and the *sol* of the soft hexachord <note>original: *b mollis*</note> or the *ut* of the hard hexachord <note>original: *♮ duri*</note> must necessarily create a difference. Thus, in *ut* and *g*, they are not equal, and therefore mutation <note>the process of switching between hexachords</note> could not occur in them. And yet they themselves perform it according to their teaching already published above, in which they necessarily contradict themselves.

Certain people, however—including myself and a **Spanish scholar** <note>likely Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja, a 15th-century theorist who famously challenged Guidonian tradition</note> who published a certain treatise in this discipline—think they are led to a better discovery. If they did not see *fa* and *sol* in **C-sol-fa-ut**, they would not place them there, for it is pointless to use many things when few will do. They place them there because they differ. So too are the other voices located elsewhere. This discovery is not for nothing, since they place them there to make the semitone either longer or closer, from which musical difference arises. For if all steps were equal, there would be no musical difference in tones or semitones. If I wished to resolve this through many arguments, I could easily do so. Indeed, let them say that the voices of the **natural/hard B** <note>original: ♮</note> differ from the voices of the **soft B** <note>original: *b mollis*</note>; they do not know how to distinguish them from themselves, even as they agree with what we have proven with examples. For in **C-fa-ut**, they clearly place *fa* and *natural-mi* there through a "conjunction" <note><term>conjunction: a technique in late medieval music to extend the hexachord system using accidentals</term></note>.

# Part One

...are placed. They do this because they recognize a great difference between *fa* and *mi* <note>In the hexachord system, *fa* represents a lower pitch and *mi* a higher pitch; the interval between them is a semitone.</note>. And yet, both voices are located in the same place: therefore, the same thing would differ from itself, which was the point to be proven. So it is also in **A-la-mi-re** <note>The pitch A in the medieval gamut</note>, where *mi* is located, they place a "soft B" <note>original: *b mollis*; a flat sign</note> as a conjunct *fa*; therefore, one "soft B" would differ from another "soft B." It is certainly absurd in music to posit a difference between the voices of "hard B," "soft B," or "natural." 

If Johannes de Sancto Vincentio <note>A likely obscure or local theorist</note> attempted to affirm this in theology, because he composed no book, nothing more need be said of him. But if Johannes Hothby <note>original: *ioāēs otbi*; an English Carmelite composer and theorist active in Italy</note>, who posits a "hard" semitone as well as a "soft" and "natural" one, he understood the matter far worse. Although he indeed adopted the numbers for his monochord <note>A one-stringed instrument used to measure musical intervals mathematically</note> from those which Boethius <note>The primary authority on music theory in the Middle Ages</note> posits, he received that distinction of the semitone not from Boethius, but from some unlearned person. 

I must say of him what Johannes Carthusiensis <note>Johannes de Mantua, a Carthusian monk and theorist</note> used to say of Marchetto <note>Marchetto da Padova, an influential 14th-century theorist who proposed dividing the whole tone into five "dieses"</note>: "Since the world began, it has not been heard that one should posit a triple semitone—the Chromatic, the Enharmonic, and the Diatonic." For as he says, who ever heard from any true teacher that there was a triple semitone, except from some unrefined "Marchettist"? I believe Johannes Hothby took some of his foundation from him. But I am not surprised, for he is a follower of Guido of Arezzo <note>The 11th-century monk credited with inventing the staff and the hexachord system, whom the author frequently critiques</note>. 

I, however, intend to crush the head of this theory, so that this body, built upon errors, may now become a corpse and no longer be able to live. Furthermore, that "binder of the world of music" says that these three properties—namely **Hard B** [♮], **Soft B** [♭], and **Natural**—relate to each other just as the three genera of melodies do which are posited by Boethius: the **Diatonic**, **Chromatic**, and **Enharmonic**. 

Of these, the **Diatonic**, because it is somewhat "hard," he says holds the likeness of the **Square B** <note>original: *▢ quadzati*; the "hard" or natural B</note>. The **Enharmonic**, being more closely adapted to the "soft," he compares to the **Soft B** [♭]. The **Chromatic**, which holds a middle ground between the two, preserves the image of the **Natural**. 

<margin> = </margin>

We already argued against this while we were lecturing at the University of Salamanca, in his presence, and in the treatise we composed there in our mother tongue <note>Spanish. This likely refers to a lost earlier work or a draft of the current treatise</note> concerning this faculty, we contradicted him in all things. So much so that, after seeing and examining my treatise, he said: "I am not as familiar with Boethius as that man is." 

For I have indeed proven that in the other two genera there is both "hard" and "soft" just as in this one; there, I do not doubt that the *synemmenon* tetrachord <note>The "tetrachord of the conjuncts," the ancient Greek way of accounting for B-flat</note> is found. Their opinion is one thing; mine is another. Let us divide the tetrachords of the **Chromatic** genus by a semitone, another semitone, and a *triemitone* <note>A minor third, or three semitones</note>; that is, the first interval is a semitone, and the second is a semitone, but the third is a *triemitone* or a semi-ditone. 

The **Enharmonic** genus, however, divides the semitone into two parts, which are called *dieses* <note>Quarter-tones</note>; thus, two spaces are held by individual *dieses*. But the third interval encompasses a [ditone] distance. However, we will not treat these genera further at present, because at the end of this first part, we will put them into practice with signed notes. Now, however, [we discuss] how...

<meta>This page continues the author’s (Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja) critique of traditional music theory, specifically the system established by Guido of Arezzo. ## Second Treatise

Let us declare how the <term>mi-fa</term> <note>The semitone interval, the smallest distance between two notes in this system.</note> semitone is created, and we shall discuss why the mutations of <term>conjuncts</term> <note>Notes that fall outside the standard Guidonian hand or gamut, effectively medieval accidentals.</note> are necessary. We will also explain how "tonal" voices (whole tones) are made into semitonal ones, and "ditonal" intervals (major thirds) become "semiditonal" (minor thirds), and vice-versa. To ensure that what follows is correctly understood, let us examine the matter diligently.

### Chapter Seven: Reproving the followers of Guido and subtly demonstrating the truth of the matter

Having examined and perceived the differences in music, it remains to be proven how tonal voices become semitones and vice-versa. Regarding this, it must be known that a song in ascent—as **Johannes de Villanova** <note>A 14th-century theorist often associated with the *Ars Nova* style.</note> says—wants the voice to be "fortified" (strengthened), and in descent, to be made "soft." 

For this reason, he says that if a song is sung as **A C D** and does not return to **C**, although it should be called <term>re fa sol</term> <note>The standard syllables for the notes A, C, and D in the medieval "natural" hexachord.</note> as the order demonstrates, it ought instead to be called <term>ut mi fa</term> because the distance from **A** to **C** is not a semitone, but a <term>ditone</term> <note>A major third.</note>. However, if it were pronounced with those same voices—namely <term>re fa si</term>—it would be called an "implied" ditone. 

Furthermore, if a melody goes **G F G** and does not touch the **F** again, that **F** is an implied semitone, even though it is called <term>sol fa sol</term> or <term>re ut re</term>. He considers the same thing: that a <term>synemmenon</term> <note>original: *synemenon*; a Greek term referring to the "connected" tetrachord which introduced the B-flat into the scale.</note> must always be used when a note in the **B-fa-♮-mi** position is followed by another in the *mese* <note>The central note of the Greek scale, corresponding to A.</note>; this applies whether the melody reaches it from the low notes or descends to it from the high notes, especially if it strikes the same place multiple times. 

Likewise, if a song makes the progression **D B C D C D D**, the distance from **B** to **C** is an octave <note>The text likely implies a specific melodic leap or relationship here.</note>, and **C** to **D** becomes a semitone played twice. Thus, either the tonal voices will hold their place by implication, or by mutation <note>The process of changing from one hexachord to another to reach different notes.</note>, **mi** will become **re**, which is a voice of the "conjuncts." 

The same Johannes says that a <term>semiditone</term> <note>A minor third.</note> is made from a ditone in this way: if a melody says <term>la fa sol sol</term> without returning to **fa**, it will either be an implied semiditone, or by mutation **la** becomes **sol**, so that it is sung as <term>la sol mi fa fa</term>. In this manner, the diligent reader can judge other similar arrangements. Such notes ought to be marked with this sign: **#** or this: **♮** for greater clarity. 

Therefore, he who wishes to compose a song must pay close attention to these things, which we will discuss more subtly in the second part. For now, let these few words suffice to clarify the confusion resulting from Guido's voices. **Johannes Carthusinus** <note>Johannes de Mantua, a monk who critiqued the complexity of the Guidonian system.</note> spoke well of these mutations—I do not mean the mutation of one voice into another, but the variation from one ambiguity to another—noting only the tones and semitones and singing by the letters of Gregory <note>The letter names A, B, C, D, E, F, G.</note>. 

I say this regarding my own voices: for whoever wishes to sing by *our* voices will be required to make only one single mutation in one <term>diapason</term> <note>The interval of an octave.</note>. When the song ascends beyond high **C**, we will change <term>tas</term> into <term>psal</term>, and thus the mutation <term>sas sal</term> will occur. But when it comes downward, they will change <term>psal</term> into <term>tas</term> in this manner:

<note>The text cuts off as it begins to demonstrate Ramos de Pareja's unique 8-syllable solmization system: *psal-li-tur per vo-ces is-tas*.</note>

<meta>This page continues Ramos de Pareja’s radical re-evaluation of musical theory. Having previously discussed the nature of semitones, he now moves into the practical application of his new octave-based system, contrasting it with the older Guidonian hexachord system and addressing potential critics.</meta>

# Part One

...to be sung in the low register; and also they should not force students to do this; for we sometimes permit one syllable to be used for another, provided they only pay attention to the rules mentioned above—that is, they should be accustomed to observing the <term>species</term> <note>The specific patterns of whole tones and semitones that make up musical intervals.</note> of tones or semitones, so that they do not perform one in place of the other. 

This error often happens to those who <term>solmizate</term> <note>The practice of singing scales using syllables like ut, re, mi.</note>, as they say, using the syllables of **Guido**. Indeed, the "Catholic faith" of the modern theorists regarding the syllables of **Guido** <note>Guido of Arezzo (d. 1033), the monk credited with inventing the staff and the syllables ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la.</note> has grown so great due to the long duration of custom that **Ugolino of Orvieto** <note>original: "ugolinus vrbiveteranus"; a 15th-century theorist who wrote a massive encyclopedia of music.</note>, while secluded in the cathedral church of Ferrara, when he begins to treat these syllables, calls them Greek terms. He says the Greeks had only five—namely *re, mi, fa, sol, la*—lacking *ut*. He attempts to prove this because their art begins from the <term>lowest note</term> <note>original: "proslambanomeno"; the lowest note of the Greek "Greater Perfect System," which corresponds to the note A.</note>, which is the letter **A**; from this, he concludes his argument. Truly, if this theory were to be brought into the light and made public, it should be held as a joke and a mockery.

We, therefore, being wonderfully delighted in this, our most excellent new form of the art, have decided to begin the song with **ut**, because that syllable holds a nobler tone (as will be said later) and also because, as **Guido** himself says, we find it to be the first in the nature of numbers. We shall therefore make one <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval where a note repeats at a higher or lower pitch.</note> for it in the lower part and others in the higher part, so that our music contains three octaves. Thus it may be useful not only for ecclesiastical chant but also for the more inquisitive secular music. 

This shall be the music of **Gregory**, **Ambrose**, **Augustine**, **Bernard**, **Isidore**, and the *Handbook* <note>original: "enchiridion"; likely referring to the *Dialogus de musica* or *Enchiriadis*, early medieval textbooks.</note> of **Odo**, and of **Guido**, who took nearly everything from these men and their followers. Just as the Law of Scripture was not given to all (for some sing without it to this day), our music shall be "catholic," or universal, like the Law of Grace. As the Law of Grace contains within itself the Written Law and the Law of Nature, so also our music will contain everything that those most wise ecclesiastical men and ancient musicians spoke of and discovered. 

Therefore, when we place the eighth note below **C**, let the Italians not marvel; for it is established that this is not new, but very frequently used. Nearly all the <term>polychords</term> <note>Instruments with many strings, such as early keyboards or harps.</note> of the modern theorists have it, though it is found through instruments that the <term>monochords</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used by ancients to measure musical ratios.</note> of the ancients made their beginning on that same low **C**. Therefore, we have established the beginning of the song on that same letter. However, on the monochord, following the Greeks and **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard university text for music.</note>, we begin on **A**. 

Now, we place the <term>art of memory</term>—that is, the letters to be sung with the syllables via the joints of the fingers—into the palm in this manner. Not without reason, as **Guido** did at his whim, but with the greatest resemblance to the reality of the matter, we shall arrange the syllables in this way: Let the hollow of the hand be the "hollow instrument" where silence is placed, for no voice should be made there; it shall be like the earth. Then, the first voice shall be placed in the "restricted" part <note>The base of the thumb or the lower part of the hand where it narrows.</note>, below which no lower voice should be placed because there will be silence; for the hollow of the hand...

<summary>Ramos de Pareja critiques the outdated theories of Ugolino of Orvieto and Guido of Arezzo, proposing a new "universal" octave-based system starting on the note C. He justifies his system by citing both ancient authorities and the practical range of modern multi-stringed instruments.</summary>
<keywords>solmization, Guido of Arezzo, Ugolino of Orvieto, Boethius, monochord, polychord, octave, diapason, musical hand, music theory reform</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Ramos de Pareja's "Musica Practica," moving from a critique of the traditional Guidonian hexachord system to a practical explanation of his own octave-based system. Having dismissed the "Catholic faith" of modern theorists in the previous page, he now describes how to map his new system onto the human hand, a standard pedagogical tool known as the Guidonian Hand, but here modified to suit his eight-note octave theory.</meta>

<note>Partial hand diagram on the upper left with alphabetic letters marking musical divisions of the finger joints. This early example of a musical mnemonic hand diagram is integrated into music theory texts to help students visualize the relationship between notes.</note>

# Second Treatise

A part [of the hand] lacking a joint <note>original: "iunctura"</note> lacks distinct movement. But movement is restricted first by the application of the thumb moved to the fleshy part <note>original: "pinguedinem"; refers to the thenar eminence at the base of the thumb.</note> of the hand. Since, therefore, there is motion there, we will locate a sound; then the second voice <note>In this context, "voice" refers to a specific pitch or note in a scale.</note> at the root of the thumb on the outer part where there is another motion distinct from that one. The third, indeed, in the other outer joint of the thumb where the motion is also distinct. The fourth, for the same reason, in the penultimate outer joint. The fifth, however—because it has greater agreement with the first than the fourth itself does—we will place at the tip of the thumb turned back, that is, looking toward the first. This is for the same reason that the voice is a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; an interval of four notes.</note>, and so we will place the sixth voice in the following inner joint looking downward. And thus also the seventh; and so seven different notes will be placed in a different manner.

But the eighth, which is similar to the first yet higher <note>original: "acutior"; literally "sharper" or more acute, used here for higher pitch.</note>, will be located at the root of the index finger. And since that finger is more slender than the thumb, it clearly demonstrates that more slender <term>strings</term> <note>original: "chordas"; Ramos often uses the physical properties of strings to explain vocal pitch.</note> return higher-pitched voices. We will arrange another <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an eighth.</note> in this way, so that with the eighth of this first octave—namely the triad <note>Likely referring to the completion of a structural unit of three octaves.</note>—we place the first of the following, for example, the first psalm-tone, in the root of the index finger itself, and in the second joint of this finger, then in the third, then at the top. Across the top from the summit [of the index] to the summit of the middle finger, we will draw two lines so that those two fingers appear bound together at the top; and at the summit of the middle finger we will place a voice turned back for the same reason we spoke of regarding the other.

<note>Faint full hand diagram on the right side of the page, showing finger joints and horizontal divisions for mapping musical notes. This illustrates the pedagogical use of the Guidonian hand in Renaissance music theory for teaching intervals and the musical gamut.</note>

In the same way, we will fit the following voices to the following joints, so that in the root of the middle finger itself the eighth voice is placed, and in the root of the index it is placed in unison <note>original: "unisono"</note>. The same should be done for another, third octave with the little finger <note>original: "aviculari"</note>, at the root of which the series reaches its end. Not that we say it is impossible to proceed further to higher notes, nor do we deny that "wings" <note>An allegorical way of describing the extension of the musical scale.</note> can be let down below the lower notes; but we say this because the teaching is sufficiently expanded by these three octaves. In the low strings there is a limit of voice so that depth does not descend into total silence, and in the high notes a limit of sharpness is kept, lest—as **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose "De institutione musica" was the fundamental music theory text of the Middle Ages.</note> says—the thinness of an overly strained voice be broken. But let the whole be harmonious and fitting to itself. All this, therefore, the following figure of the hand will make clear.

We imagine this figure, arranged in this way, so that the thumb is cut through the middle, as if at the hollow of the hand, and the inner part is joined together with the index finger separated from the middle. Thus also the middle finger is raised above the binding which ties both by their tips, so that the voices marked with inverted letters are represented by upright ones; while the <term>ring finger</term> <note>original: "medicus"; literally the "medical finger," a common name for the fourth finger in the Middle Ages.</note> [remains] at the side...

<summary>Ramos de Pareja explains his innovative eight-note octave system by mapping it onto the joints of the human hand, replacing the traditional Guidonian hexachords. He uses the physical anatomy of the hand—joints, finger thickness, and movement—to justify his musical divisions and cites Boethius to explain the natural limits of high and low pitch.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical concepts of sound and silence on the "Guidonian Hand" to the practical structure of musical intervals.
