## Second Treatise

standing upright in the middle of the little fingers: and rising by connecting through the head: so that its root is at the summit: and thus we demonstrate the likeness of the ancients with the first fifteen strings or voices. Therefore, we place them in a single upright arrangement: but those eight which we join for perfection, we join there to be denoted as is clear in the figure. <note>The author is likely referring back to a diagram of the musical scale or "Guidonian Hand" mentioned on the previous page, explaining how the standard 15-note Greek system was expanded to the medieval system.</note> But enough of these matters; let us now turn our discourse to the primary <term>species</term> <note>In medieval music theory, "species" refers to the different arrangements of whole tones and semitones within a specific interval.</note> which were spoken of before, since the entire power of harmony depends upon them.

### Chapter Eight, in which it is subtly declared in how many ways each species is formed

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Take note, reader: that each species is formed in as many ways as the intervals it contains. This is because the <term>semitone</term> <note>The smallest interval in the scale, such as the step between E and F or B and C.</note>—which has an unequal interval—rotates through the other intervals. Thus, it is sometimes placed in the first, sometimes in the second, and sometimes in the third interval. However, a species that does not contain a semitone is formed in only one way, even if it contains several intervals; for example, the <term>ditone</term> <note>A major third, consisting of two whole tones.</note> and the <term>tritone</term> <note>An interval of three whole tones, often called "the devil in music" for its dissonance.</note> are always formed in only one way. But the <term>semiditone</term> <note>A minor third, consisting of one tone and one semitone.</note>, since it has two intervals—namely one tone and one semitone—can be formed in two ways: one when the semitone is in the higher interval, such as A to C, or D to F, or G to B-flat, or B to D in the "disjunct" system, and in their octaves. The other way is when the semitone is in the lower interval, such as B to D, or C to E, or H to B in the "conjunct" system, and in their octaves. <note>The letters H and B were used to distinguish between B-natural and B-flat in Germanic and some Latin musical traditions.</note> The <term>diatessaron</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fourth.</note>, because it contains three intervals—namely two tones and one semitone—can be formed in three ways. Sometimes the semitone is in the middle, such as A to D, D to G, and G to C in the "conjunct" system, and H to L in the "disjunct," and in their octaves. And this is called the first mode, since the art of <term>Boethius</term> <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages.</note> begins there. The second mode is when the semitone is in the lower place, such as B to E and E to A, and so too H to L in the "conjunct" system, and in their octaves. The third mode is when the semitone is in the higher place, such as C to F, F to B, and G to K, and in their octaves. The <term>diapente</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fifth.</note> is formed in four ways because it contains four intervals—namely three tones and one semitone. Therefore, the first mode will have the semitone in the second interval, such as A to E, or D to H, and so too G to I in the "conjunct" and B to M in the "disjunct" systems, and in their octaves. The second mode will have the semitone in the first interval, such as E to B <note>original: "e ♮"; the square/natural sign indicates B-natural.</note> and B to M in the "conjunct" system, and in their octaves. The third mode will have the semitone in the fourth interval, such as F to K or B to N in the "disjunct" system, and in their octaves. The fourth mode will have the semitone in the third interval, such as C to G, or F to K in the "conjunct," or G to K in the "disjunct" system, and in their octaves. This is understood in the natural order; for in the accidental order, by coming from any one string to another, we can make all four different fifths.

<summary>This page explains how musical intervals like the fourth (diatessaron) and fifth (diapente) are categorized into "species" based on the shifting position of the semitone within the scale. It provides specific letter-name examples and references Boethius as the primary authority on these musical structures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals, moving from the internal structure of intervals to a definitive list of thirteen "species" or types of melodic movement. The author shifts from a general introduction to a specific classification system rooted in the authority of Boethius, a 6th-century philosopher whose works were the foundation of medieval music theory.</meta>

# Second Treatise

and so there are other species. But we set these down so that the teaching does not become confused and that ecclesiastical chant—which is regularly ordered—may be understood. Other chants, however, composed more out of licentiousness than beauty, never or rarely follow the rule of which we shall speak shortly. First, however, let us speak of all the species which are thought by many to be thirteen. 

According to <term>Boethius</term> <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the most influential musical authority of the Middle Ages.</note>, a species is a certain position having its own proper form according to each genus of every proportion that creates a consonance, established by fixed terms; namely, departing from equality and moving up to the <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave.</note> through the addition of a <term>semitone</term> <note>The smallest step in the medieval scale.</note>. 

Therefore, the first equality is called the <term>unison</term>. After this, the smallest quantity is the semitone; then the tone, which is worth two semitones. After this comes the <term>semiditone</term> <note>original: "semiditonus"; a minor third, consisting of a tone and a semitone.</note> which contains three semitones, namely a tone with a semitone; and so on: the <term>ditone</term> <note>A major third.</note>, the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>A perfect fourth.</note>, the <term>tritone</term> <note>An augmented fourth.</note>, and the <term>diapente</term> <note>A perfect fifth.</note>. 

The diapente with a semitone is called by the Greek name <term>minor hexas</term> <note>original: "exas minor"; a minor sixth.</note>, since it is capable of six voices and has only a semitone beyond the diapente. The diapente with a tone is called the <term>major hexas</term> or <term>hexachord</term>; it has a tone above the diapente. Likewise, there are the minor and major <term>heptas</term> or <term>heptachord</term> <note>original: "eptas" / "eptacorduz"; a seventh.</note>, because it is capable of seven strings or voices. These are the diapente with a semiditone and the diapente with a ditone. Finally, there is the diapente with a diatessaron, which, as we have said, when joined together make the diapason.

So, to speak summarily: the species by which all chant is governed are thought to be these thirteen:
1. Unison
2. Semitone
3. Tone
4. Semiditone
5. Ditone
6. Diatessaron
7. Tritone
8. Diapente
...along with which the former ones are repeated up to the diatessaron, and in this way the diapason itself is completed. 

Someone might ask, however, why we say that the diapente and diatessaron joined together fulfill the diapason. For would not the ditone with the minor sixth do the same, or the semiditone with the major? Likewise the tone with the minor seventh, and the semitone with the major? It must be said that this is true. But because those are the principal parts—namely the diapente and diatessaron—into which the diapason is first divided, the diapason is said to be regularly formed from them. 

For those who have looked well at the quantity of the string in the first figure [marked] **b. a.**: in the middle, **v** stands at an equal distance from both; let the diatessaron sound with **a** and the diapente sound with **b**. Therefore, the quantity of the string of the diatessaron **v. a** is the same as the diapente **v. b**. Thus, it is no wonder if the diapason is always said to be perfected from that quantity into which it is first divided. Thus also chant, in its progression, proceeds through those voices which form these species more than through others, as will be said shortly. Yet let no one judge from this that there is such...

<summary>This page defines and lists the thirteen species of musical intervals, ranging from the unison to the octave (diapason). It explains why the octave is traditionally divided into a perfect fifth (diapente) and a perfect fourth (diatessaron) using a mathematical string-length demonstration.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals from the previous page, specifically focusing on how certain dissonant intervals like the tritone relate to perfect intervals like the fourth (diatessaron) and fifth (diapente). It transitions from the "Second Treatise" on intervals into the "Third Treatise," which defines the musical modes or tones using the Greek pitch system.</meta>

# Second Treatise

...the truth of the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> in sound is as great as the <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapente"; the interval of a perfect fifth</note> since, as has been said, it is exceeded by a tone. There is, however, another quantity which differs almost nothing in sound, into which the <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave.</note> can be divided: for example, the <term>tritone</term> <note>An interval of three whole tones, famous for its dissonant quality.</note> and the imperfect fifth which is called the <term>semidiapente</term> <note>A diminished fifth.</note>, such as B-flat to F and F to B-natural <note>The text uses the medieval symbols for "soft B" (♭/b) and "hard B" (♮).</note>. For the distance is as great between F and B-flat as it is between F and B-natural. This does not differ in the judgment of practitioners, though it does for theorists who examine the specific difference of a <term>semitone</term> <note>The smallest step in the scale.</note>; yet in sound, it is no less discordant than the tritone considered directly. 

Therefore, we have not mentioned the <term>semidiapente</term> because the <term>tritone</term> is accepted in its place in sound. We specifically said "directly," because if one proceeds through intermediate notes, whether ascending or descending, the sound is sweet and playful, such as F-E-D-C-B and conversely B-C-D-E-F. However, the song should not rest on F when it ascends, but should turn back to E; likewise in descending, it should turn back to C. To create a tritone, as <term>Brother Johannes Carthusinus</term> <note>Johannes Gallicus (c. 1415–1473), a Carthusian monk and influential music theorist.</note> says, is not a mortal sin as many believe; indeed, it is necessary. For otherwise, one could not provide the third species of the <term>diapente</term>, which would be clearly false and against the truth. In its own place, it seems necessary, as if a song ascends in this manner: F-G-A-B-flat-C. This ascent is sweeter than F-G-A-B-natural-C. In descending, however, it is the opposite way. But enough about these things for now; we must now speak of the species of the <term>diapason</term> itself—how many there are, what they are, and how they are arranged. Within this, the whole power of harmony rests, and they are called <term>modes</term>, <term>tropes</term>, or even <term>tones</term>.

## Third Treatise, in which a full knowledge of the tones, modes, or tropes is set forth.

->Let us proceed, therefore, to that species of music which is said<-
->to contain the whole of harmony. Since, therefore, it is made<-
->from eight notes containing seven intervals, five tones, and<-
->two semitones, it is made in seven modes; because if it<-
is correctly composed of a <term>diapente</term> and a <term>diatessaron</term>, and the <term>diapente</term> consists of four modes and the <term>diatessaron</term> of three, it remains that the <term>diapason</term> itself must be varied in seven modes. Therefore, let us begin from the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "proslabanomeno"; the lowest note of the traditional Greek scale, corresponding to A.</note> up to the <term>mese</term> <note>original: "mese"; the middle note of the scale, corresponding to the A an octave higher.</note>, which will be the first species. This is divided into a <term>diatessaron</term> and a <term>diapente</term> by the <term>lichanos hypaton</term> <note>original: "licanon hypaton"; the pitch D.</note> dividing it and cutting the string in the middle. This species of the <term>diapason</term> will therefore have the first <term>diatessaron</term> from A to D, with the first <term>diapente</term> from D to A, since the semitone of each is placed in the second interval. 

The second species of the <term>diapason</term> will be from the <term>hypate hypaton</term> <note>The pitch B.</note> to the <term>paramese</term> <note>The pitch B an octave higher.</note>, which is divided by a string equally divided in the middle by the <term>hypate meson</term> <note>The pitch E.</note>. It will therefore have the second <term>diatessaron</term>, namely B to E, and the second <term>diapente</term>, namely E to B-natural, since the semitone...

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of musical intervals, specifically moving from the general definitions of the fourth and fifth into the specific "species" or patterns that these intervals form within the scale. It connects to the previous page's discussion of the octave (diapason) by showing how different starting notes create different arrangements of whole tones and semitones.</meta>

<header>First part</header>

# First part

...of both is placed in the first interval, that is, in the lower one. Therefore, the third species of the whole harmony <note>original: "concentus"</note> is created from the <term>hypate hypaton</term> <note>The lowest note of the principal Greek scale.</note> to the <term>trite diezeugmenon</term> <note>A note in the upper-middle part of the scale.</note>, which is divided, like the others, at the <term>parhypate meson</term>. It will therefore have the third species of the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> and the third <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapente"; the interval of a perfect fifth.</note>, since the semitone of the fourth is placed in the third interval, and that of the fifth in the [last].

You make the fourth species of the <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval of an octave.</note> from the <term>lichanos hypaton</term> to the <term>paranete diezeugmenon</term>, dividing it by the <term>lichanos meson</term>. This fourth species of the octave will therefore have the first species of the fourth, but the fourth species of the fifth, because the semitone is placed in the third interval; in this, it is easily recognized as differing from the first species. 

However, if a fifth is made from the <term>lichanos meson</term> to the <term>nete synemmenon</term> <note>The "disjunct" or "conjunct" note in the Greek system.</note>, it will be the same as the first species, and there is no difference, because the semitone will be in the second interval just as in the first. Since the fourth is entirely similar, it follows that there is no difference between these species arranged this way—unless someone were to say they differ because the fifth of the first species is made from line to line (such as from B to F) and the fifth of the fourth species from space to space (such as G to D). But such a distinction is of no value, because a **line or space** does not prove a difference in music. 

<term>Brother John of the Carthusians</term> <note>Johannes Carthusiensis, a 15th-century music theorist also known as Johannes Gallicus.</note> attempted to prove that only fifteen strings <note>original: "nervos"</note> were placed by the ancients for this reason: that the notes **A-la-mi-re** and **G-sol-re-ut** <note>Medieval names for specific pitches on the musical staff.</note> are placed in the second space, and so the rest of the strings that come after A-la-mi-re correspond in a similar way. For if this were so, the third species of the fifth would be similar to the first, and the fourth to the second, because they now occupy lines or spaces [similarly]. Therefore, the fourth species of the fifth is not dissimilar to the first, but is the same, if the <term>tetrachord synemmenon</term> <note>A set of four notes "joined" to the scale, used to provide a B-flat.</note> is used. 

In this way, by dividing the string in the middle, we make four different species of the octave, and there cannot be more. However, if we divide the string not through the middle but through the three [proportions], we will make another four different ones—not because they are entirely different, but because the fourth will be above the fifth and not below it. Such is the fifth species, from the <term>lichanos hypaton</term> to the <term>paranete diezeugmenon</term>; for the fifth will be the same as the first held, and the fourth is stretched above it. In this, it will be seen to differ sufficiently from the first.

The sixth species of the octave goes from the <term>meson</term> to the <term>nete diezeugmenon</term>, which will have the same fifth as the second species and a similar fourth, but stretched above the fifth; and in this, it differs from that second species. The seventh species of the octave will be made from the <term>parhypate meson</term> to the <term>trite hyperboleon</term>, taking the fifth which the third species held and stretching the fourth above it. We make the eighth species in this way: from the <term>lichanos meson</term>, we stretch the same fifth as the fourth species held...

<summary>This page defines the various species or patterns of the octave (diapason) by combining different types of fourths and fifths. It also argues that musical differences are defined by intervals and semitone placement, not by whether notes sit on lines or spaces of the staff.</summary>
<keywords>octave species, diatessaron, diapente, Johannes Carthusiensis, tetrachord, Greek musical system, music theory, staff notation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the species of the octave. The previous page ended while describing the fourth species of the octave; this page concludes that description and transitions into Chapter Two, which explains how these mathematical intervals form the basis of the musical modes (Dorian, Phrygian, etc.) used in plainchant and classical music theory.</meta>

# Third Treatise

...on the *paranete diezeugmenon* <note>original: "paranete diazeugmenon"; a specific note in the Higher Disjunct tetrachord of the Greek scale, roughly corresponding to the note D</note> above which we intend the first <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diathessaron"; an interval spanning four notes of a scale</note> to reach the *paranete hyperboleon* <note>The note G in the highest range of the Greek system.</note>. If, however, the <term>conjunct</term> <note>original: "synemmenon"; referring to the tetrachord that overlaps with the one below it, effectively adding a "flat" note</note> is taken, it will be like the fifth species; because of this difference between the conjunct and the disjunct, an eighth species of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> is added. We shall now discuss how the modes or tones arise from these species.

### Chapter Two: On the origin of the tones

From the species of the octave, therefore, as <term>Boethius</term> <note>An influential 6th-century Roman philosopher; his "De institutione musica" was the primary music theory textbook of the Middle Ages.</note> says, there arise what are called modes, which some also name "tropes" or "tones." These tropes are the arrangements <note>original: "constitutiones"</note> of the entire range of voices, differing in their highness or depth. An arrangement is like the "body" of a melody, consisting of a joining together of <term>consonances</term> <note>Musical intervals that sound stable or "sweet" to the ear.</note>.

Boethius calls these modes by distinct proper names; for he says that whatever name a particular nation delighted in, the mode was called by that same name. For example, the **Dorian** was so named because the Dorians <note>An ancient Greek tribe.</note> delighted in it. It consists of the first species of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"; an interval spanning five notes</note> and the first species of the fourth stretched above that fifth: f. c b ᚦ b l. <note>These letters represent specific pitch markers in medieval notation.</note>, which we said is located in the fourth place of the octave. 

However, when this fifth has a fourth lowered below it (that is, d. a), it is called the **Hypodorian**, because it is placed "under" <note>The Greek prefix "hypo-" means "under" or "below."</note> the Dorian. 

The second species of the fifth joined with the second species of the fourth stretched above—as e □ □ m—is called the **Phrygian**, because it was in use among the Phrygians. But if the second species of the fifth should have the second species of the fourth lowered below it, it is called the **Hypophrygian** by that same logic, as e □ e b.

The third species of the fifth with the third species of the fourth stretched above is called the **Lydian**, because it was frequently used in Lydia (from where the Tuscans trace their origin) with great joy, as f k (the fifth) and k n (the fourth stretched above). But if it holds the fourth not above but below, it shall be named the **Hypolydian** for the reason stated before, as f k (the fifth) and f c (the fourth). 

The fourth species of the fifth with the first species of the fourth stretched above lacks a name of its own, but because it is placed next to the Lydian, it has been called the **Mixolydian**, as g l (the fifth stretched up) and l o (the fourth stretched up). But if it does not stretch the fourth above it, but lowers it below, it is called the **Hypomixolydian**, as g l (the fifth) and g c (the fourth lowered). 

Because of this consistency in both the matter and the naming, the Greeks and also our own ancestors say there are only four [main] modes, because each species of the fifth is double <note>Meaning each fifth can be paired with a fourth either above it or below it to form two related modes.</note>. Thus the first species is called **Protus** in Greek, which is interpreted as "First" in Latin; the second is **Deuterus** in Greek, "Second" in Latin; the third is **Tritus** in Greek, "Third" in Latin; and the fourth is **Tetrartus** in Greek, "Fourth" in Latin.

<summary>This page defines the eight musical modes (Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, Mixolydian, and their "Hypo-" counterparts) based on the mathematical arrangements of fourths and fifths. It attributes these definitions to the Roman philosopher Boethius and explains the Greek numbering system for the modes.</summary>
<keywords>Boethius, musical modes, Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, Mixolydian, Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrartus, octave species, diatessaron, diapente</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the eight musical modes are constructed from the species of the octave. It specifically addresses the relationship between "authentic" and "plagal" modes and critiques contemporary theorists like Johannes de Muris and Ugolino of Orvieto regarding the classification of "perfect" and "imperfect" melodies.</meta>

<header>Part One</header>

# Part One

When, therefore, the <term>protus</term> <note>original: "protus"; from the Greek for "first," referring to the first pair of musical modes (Modes 1 and 2), which are based on the note D.</note> has the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> above its <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"; although the text here implies the structure of the octave, the fourth is placed above the fifth in authentic modes.</note>, it is called the **authentic protus**, that is, the "first authoritative" or "master" mode. But if it receives the fourth below it, lacking that higher elevation, it is named the **plagal protus**, that is, the "collateral" or the "disciple," as the <term>moderns</term> <note>The author refers to "modern" music theorists of the 14th and 15th centuries.</note> say. If a mode contains both ranges, it is called "mixed." You should understand the same for the other three categories <note>The Deuterus (E), Tritus (F), and Tetrardus (G) categories.</note>. 

However, we speak of this mixing only superficially for now, because we are dealing only with the species of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> from which those eight <term>tones</term> <note>original: "toni"; used here interchangeably with "modes."</note> arise. When these tones each fulfill their full octave, they are considered **perfect**. If they fall short, they are **imperfect**; if they exceed it, they are **superfluous**. They are not, however, "more than perfect," as <term>Ugolino</term> <note>Ugolino of Orvieto (c. 1380–1452), a famous music theorist.</note> asserts, along with <term>Johannes de Muris</term> <note>An influential 14th-century mathematician and music theorist.</note>—whom Ugolino praises excessively—and <term>Marchetto</term> <note>Marchetto of Padua, a transitional figure between Medieval and Renaissance music theory.</note>, who was rightly criticized by Brother John. 

For I say that a tone which fills its octave, neither more nor less, is **perfect**. That which exceeds it or falls short is **imperfect**, either by superfluity or by diminution. The moderns call these eight modes as follows: 
1. The **authentic protus** is called the "first."
2. Its **plagal** is the "second."
3. The **authentic deuterus** is the "third."
4. Its **plagal** is the "fourth."
5. The **authentic tritus** is the "fifth."
6. Its **plagal** is the "sixth."
7. The **authentic tetrardus** is the "septimus."
8. Its **plagal** is the "octave."

But we must see how a <term>trope</term> <note>Another term for a musical mode.</note> or mode is understood: whether simply by ascending a fifth, by ascending or descending a fourth, or by proceeding from the first note—that is, from the lowest to the highest—through all the intermediate notes, or if some other way of proceeding in song is required. Since we have established four different categories, it is necessary that they maintain their differences in song. 

For the **quality** of one mode is carried along in the manner of a steady and tranquil history; another is composed of twists and leaps; another is "talkative." Yet another is severe, raising the voice to the sublime and lifting the spirits of the listeners, while another is placid, indicating a joyful character. From this it should be noted that musical movement draws its quality and its distinction from the mode itself—whether it be the *protus*, the *deuterus*, or another. We must therefore speak about these modes individually.

<summary>The author explains how the eight musical modes are categorized as "authentic" or "plagal" based on their range and describes the unique emotional "quality" or character of different modes. He also engages in a theoretical debate, rejecting the term "more than perfect" used by other famous scholars of his time.</summary>

<keywords>modes, authentic, plagal, protus, deuterus, tritus, tetrardus, Johannes de Muris, Ugolino of Orvieto, perfection in music, musical ethics</keywords>

# Third Treatise

## Chapter three, in which the conformity of cosmic, human, and 
## instrumental music is shown through the modes:

For this instrumental music <note>Music played on instruments or sung, as opposed to the silent music of the spheres or the internal harmony of the body.</note> has the greatest conformity and similarity with both human and cosmic music. With human music, it works in this way: for those four modes <note>Referring to the four primary categories of the eight church modes.</note> move the four <term>complexions</term> <note>The "temperaments" or humors (phlegm, choler, blood, and melancholy) thought to govern human health and personality.</note> of man. Wherefore the <term>protus</term> <note>original: "protus"; the first mode group (Modes 1 and 2), based on D.</note> rules over phlegm; the <term>deuterus</term> <note>original: "deuterus"; the second mode group (Modes 3 and 4), based on E.</note> over choler; the <term>tritus</term> <note>original: "tritus"; the third mode group (Modes 5 and 6), based on F.</note> over blood; and the <term>tetrardus</term> <note>original: "tetrartus"; the fourth mode group (Modes 7 and 8), based on G.</note>, which is more sluggish and slow, over melancholy.

The protus mode itself moves phlegm by awakening it from sleep, and thus its figure is depicted with a crystalline color, because the crystalline heaven is said to be made of water—the element which creates phlegm. But we assign it a crystalline color rather than that of other waters because not all phlegmatic men are able to perceive a sweet sound; rather, it is for men who are ingenious and as clear as crystal, who, when they are weighed down by phlegm—either from food (without gluttony) or from some deep external occurrence—are relieved of sleepiness, sluggishness, or sadness by the sounding of the protus mode.

But indeed, its <term>plagal</term> <note>The "lower" version of the mode (Mode 2), which has a deeper range.</note> behaves in the opposite way. For the first tone <note>The "authentic" or higher version (Mode 1).</note>, as <term>Ludovicus Sanctus</term> <note>original: "Iodociuicus sanciꝫ"; likely referring to Lodewijk Heyligen (1304–1361), a music theorist and friend of Petrarch.</note> says, is mobile and suited to all emotions: namely, it is desirable, as in songs. But the second tone is grave and mournful, and most fitting for the wretched and the lazy, as in the <term>Threni</term> <note>original: "trenis"; funeral dirges.</note> and the Lamentations of Jeremiah. For we do not doubt that sleep arises from sadness on account of the movement of phlegm, hence the saying: "their eyes were heavy with sadness" <note>A scriptural reference to the disciples in the Garden of Gethsemane.</note>.

It was also a custom among the <term>Pythagoreans</term> <note>original: "perhagoricis"; followers of the Greek philosopher Pythagoras.</note> that when they wished to dissolve the day’s cares in sleep, they would use the <term>Hypodorian</term> <note>Mode 2, the lower version of the D-mode.</note> so that a gentle and quiet sleep might steal upon them; but once awakened, they would purge the stupor and confusion of sleep with the <term>Dorian</term> <note>Mode 1, the higher version of the D-mode.</note>. They did this knowing surely, as <term>Boethius</term> <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the Roman statesman and philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music textbook for a millennium.</note> says, that the whole framework of our soul and body is joined by a musical proportion, and just as the emotions of the body behave, so also...

<summary>This page explains the "doctrine of temperaments," linking the musical modes (Protus, Deuterus, etc.) to the four human humors and the physical heavens. It specifically highlights how the Dorian and Hypodorian modes were used by ancient Pythagoreans to manage sleep and emotional states.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four musical mode groups and their influence on the human "complexions" or temperaments. Having previously discussed the Protus mode and its relation to phlegm, the text now moves through the Deuterus, Tritus, and Tetrartus modes, finally linking the musical scale to the order of the planets (cosmic music).</meta>

## Part One

...the pulse of the heart is stirred by these motions. The **Deuterus** mode <note>original: "Deuterus"; the second mode group (Modes 3 and 4), based on the note E.</note>, however, moves **choler** by inciting it and provoking one to anger; for that reason, it is depicted in a **fiery color**. This is because it is severe and stirred up in its course, having stronger leaps, as we shall say in its proper place. This tone is suited to proud, angry, and haughty men, showing the greatest harshness and severity; therefore they rejoice in it. For **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook for a millennium.</note> says that those who are harsher among nations are delighted by harsher modes, while those who are gentle are delighted by moderate ones.

Boethius said that a **young man of Tauromenium** was incited by this tone, so much so that he prepared to break down the doors of a harlot, but was calmed by the **Hypodorian** <note>A lower, more stable mode.</note> mode. Its **plagal** <note>The "subordinate" or lower-ranged version of the mode.</note> version, the **fourth tone**, is called charming, talkative, and most suitable for flatterers; for in one's presence they soothe men with charming words, but in their absence, they sting. Thus this tone is seen to be somewhat playful, yet without grace, and sometimes inciting according to its mixture.

The **Tritus** <note>original: "tritus"; the third mode group (Modes 5 and 6), based on F.</note> authentic trope <note>The "standard" or higher-ranged version of the mode.</note> has obtained dominion over the **blood**; therefore this tone is called delightful, modest, and cheerful by the blessed **Augustine**, gladdening the sad and anxious, and restoring the fallen and desperate. For this reason, it is depicted in a **blood-red color**. Regarding this, Boethius says that the **Lydians**, who are especially pleasant and joyful, are delighted by this, particularly their women, from whom the **Russians** <note>original: "russi"; likely referring to a specific group or perhaps a folk etymology of the time.</note> are said to have arisen, who take great pleasure in dances and leaps. Its plagal, however, is mournful, suitable for those who are easily moved to tears, because it has voices most gathered together <note>Referring to a narrow melodic range or close intervals.</note>, as will be discussed in the same section.

The **Tetrartus** <note>original: "tetrartus"; the fourth mode group (Modes 7 and 8), based on G.</note> authentic partakes of playfulness and pleasantness, besides having various leaps of incitement and representing the character of youth. Therefore, it holds dominion over **melancholy**, sometimes resisting it and sometimes increasing it, and this according to the mixture it makes with others, as will be said shortly after. On this account, it is depicted in a **yellowish semi-crystalline color**.

Its plagal, however, is sweet, well-mannered, and serious, according to the manner of discerning men, as **Ambrose** reports. Therefore, the seventh and eighth modes move melancholy with their melody, bringing sad and weary men to a middle ground—namely, the authentic by inciting them, and the plagal by gladdening them.

From these things, therefore, the agreement between **instrumental** and **human music** is clear. But that **cosmic music** <note>original: "musica mundana"; the silent, mathematical harmony of the universe and the celestial spheres.</note> has the greatest conformity with instrumental music is clear by taking account of the comparison. For from the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>The lowest note of the traditional Greek musical system.</note> up to the <term>mese</term> <note>The central or "middle" note of the scale.</note>, the order of the planets is arranged with the firmament: so that the **Moon** is the *proslambanomenos*; **Mercury** the *hypate hypaton*; **Venus** the *parhypate hypaton*; the **Sun** the *lichanos hypaton*; **Mars** the *hypate meson*; **Jupiter** the *parhypate meson*...

<summary>This page details how different musical modes (Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrartus) influence specific human temperaments and emotions, concluding with a cosmic mapping of musical notes to the planetary spheres.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of "Musica Mundana" (the music of the spheres), specifically how the celestial bodies correspond to musical pitches, intervals, and the eight church modes. # Third Treatise

Saturn is at the *Lichanos Meson* <note>original: "licanos meso"; the "index-finger" note of the middle tetrachord in the Ancient Greek pitch system.</note>; the starry heaven is at the *Mese* <note>original: "mese"; the central or "middle" note of the scale, often considered the "sun" of the musical system.</note>. If, therefore, the Moon is at the *Proslambanomenos* <note>original: "proslabanomenos"; the "acquired note," the lowest pitch in the Greek scale.</note> and the Sun is at the *Lichanos Hypaton* <note>original: "lycanos hipaton"; the third note from the bottom of the scale.</note>, it is clear that these two planets are to be placed within the species of the **Diatessaron** <note>original: "diathesfaron"; the musical interval of a perfect fourth.</note> song. And thus, the Moon holds the **Hypodorian** mode, while the Sun holds the **Dorian** mode. From this, it is clearly established that the Moon increases the **phlegmatic** and moist humors in man, while the Sun dries those same moist and phlegmatic humors. Therefore, because these two planets are the "principals" and the great luminaries, they rule the first mode along with the second—that is, the **authentic** and **plagal** <note>The "authentic" (higher) and "plagal" (lower) versions of a musical mode.</note> **Protus** <note>The first category of modes (Modes 1 and 2), based on the note D.</note>. For the Dorian, the first of the authentic modes, is rightly compared to the Sun because it holds the principal place among all modes, just as the Sun does among all planets. For all terrestrial exhalations and the greatest vapors are lifted up by the solar rays, from which meteorological impressions are created. The agreement between the Sun and Moon is thus clear: she shines at night, while he puts the night to flight. The Hypodorian brings on sleep, but the Dorian expels it. They agree, therefore, both in their position and in their conformity within the harmony of the **Diatessaron**.

**Mercury**, however, rules the **Hypodorian** <note>The author here transitions to a different system of planetary associations.</note>. For this mode is "adulatory" (flattering); it praises both the wicked and the wise and upright in equal measure, and is easily converted to either side: that is, to lamentation or to joy, to incitement or to sedation. Such is the nature of Mercury, who is good with the good and most wicked with the evil.

**Mars** holds the **Phrygian** mode <note>Mode 3, based on E.</note>, which is entirely **choleric** and wrathful; for by his wrath, he strives to destroy all the good things of the world. When Mercury is joined with him, or in a certain aspect to him, he is as evil in his anger as Mars himself; for the latter wounds with the sword, but the former with the tongue.

The **Hypolydian** <note>Mode 6, based on F.</note> is attributed to **Venus** herself, who is a "fortune" <note>An auspicious planet in astrology.</note>, though a feminine one, because she sometimes provokes one to pious tears.

The **Lydian** <note>Mode 5, based on F.</note> is attributed to the "greater fortune" <note>Jupiter.</note>, who creates **sanguine** <note>The temperament associated with blood: cheerful, social, and optimistic.</note> and benevolent men, mild and joyful. They harmonize in the air, since this mode always signifies joy. Its agreement with Venus is in the **Diatessaron**, and they agree in the fortune of their goodness; they do not differ except in the difference of their pitches. For the lower voice is not as sweet as the high voice, nor is it as pleasant.

The **Mixolydian** <note>Mode 7, based on G.</note> is attributed to **Saturn**, since it is concerned with **melancholy**.

The **Hypermixolydian** <note>original: "hip mixolydius"; the eighth mode.</note>, however, is placed entirely as "Castalian" <note>Associated with the Muses or the divine.</note>, since it is attributed to the starry heaven or the firmament. For this mode, above all others, possesses a certain wonderful sweetness and beauty, and is immune to all [mortal] qualities and suitable for every business. **Guido** and **Odo** <note>Guido of Arezzo and Odo of Cluny, two of the most important music theorists of the Middle Ages.</note> say that this mode represents **Glory**, nor does it descend much. As it is said: running through seven ages we labor, but in the **eighth** we expect rest from all our labors. From these, therefore, thirteen mu... <note>Text cuts off; likely "mutationes" (mutations) or "musicae" (types of music).</note>

<summary>This page maps the seven planets and the starry firmament onto the eight church modes, explaining how the musical intervals between celestial bodies mirror their astrological influences and effects on the human temperaments.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from theoretical "Musica Mundana" (celestial music) to the practical arrangement of musical modes and their origins in classical mythology and planetary movements. It bridges the gap between the divine order of the spheres and the structural "tropes" used in composition.</meta>

# Part One

Thus, the harmony between human and worldly music, alongside instrumental music, has been sufficiently discussed. For in the second and third books, we intend to speak of the measures and proportions. We have placed these matters here so that we might accustom the reader's inner mind to them and remove any ignorance regarding their significance.

It is clear, therefore, from the aforementioned comparisons and authorities, that each **tone** <note>In this context, "tone" refers to a musical mode or scale, not just a single pitch.</note> possesses a quality different from the others. If it pleases the reader to prove this even more certainly, let us look to authority and comparison through that very source from which music drew its origin. As it pleased **Hesiod** <note>A famous Greek poet (c. 700 BCE) who wrote about the origins of the gods.</note>, we shall arrange the nine Muses—the daughters of Jove and Memory—in the following way:

We assign the Muse who narrates wars to Mars and likewise to the **Phrygian** <note>The Phrygian mode was anciently associated with excitement, violence, and the god of war.</note> mode. Similarly, we assign the one who commemorates tragedies or slaughter to Saturn and to the noble **Mixolydian**; while the one who indicates joy is assigned to Venus. Thus, each Muse is situated in her proper place according to the authorities of **Marcianus** and **Macrobius** <note>Marcianus Capella and Macrobius were influential Latin writers of Late Antiquity who preserved much of the Greek musical and planetary lore for the Middle Ages.</note>. We shall impose a voice upon each planet to which they correspond.

We shall arrange the Muses as follows:
*   **Thalia** shall hold silence, just like the **Earth**.
*   Then we attribute **Clio** to the **Moon**.
*   **Calliope** shall be dedicated to **Mercury**.
*   **Terpsichore** shall be joined to **Venus**.
*   **Melpomene** shall be adorned by the **Sun**.
*   **Erato** shall stand with **Mars**.
*   **Euterpe** possesses the far-reaching power of **Jupiter** and his lyre.
*   **Polymnia** is saddened by **Saturn**.
*   Finally, **Urania** shall be given the beauty and governance of the **Starry Heaven**.

When we make the circuit from the first silence [of Earth] to the last [heavenly sphere], returning the harmony of the entire sphere to the court, we produce the **Hypodorian** mode. We believe the same should be done for the rest, continuing to create "spires" <note>This likely refers to the spiral-like winding of the musical scale as it ascends through the planetary spheres.</note> until we reach the final Muse. Any extension beyond her would be superfluous, as it would merely be a folding-back of what came before. 

This is like the crisis mentioned by certain authors regarding the note added above the *Nete Hyperboleon* <note>The highest note in the standard Ancient Greek musical system.</note> and the figure of **Coroebus** <note>A mythological character credited by Boethius with adding extra strings to the lyre, though some say he failed.</note> who added a note below the *Proslambanomenos*. I believe that he proposed the entire art in a single Cretan day, but when he reached his limit, both he and his system collapsed. For it is proven that while one Muse holds silence, another holds a higher voice. Let us be careful, however, not to overstep the bounds set by the ancient authors. Therefore, the first voice shall be the **Proslambanomenos** <note>The lowest note of the "Greater Perfect System" of Greek music.</note>, and the last shall be the **D** [re] of the **Hyperboleon** <note>A high-pitched note in the uppermost range of the medieval scale.</note>. We have placed a figure representing all of these below:

<note>The OCR does not provide an image here, but the text refers to a "subjoined figure" illustrating the mapping of Muses, planets, and musical notes.</note>

From this arrangement, it is clear why they are called **Tropes** <note>From the Greek 'tropos', meaning a 'turn' or 'manner'; these are the musical modes.</note>, and how each is produced from the others. The order proceeds such that if one stretches the first species of the **Diapason** <note>An octave.</note> from the *Proslambanomenos* to the *Mese* <note>The central "middle" note of the scale.</note> into a higher pitch—making the *Hypate Hypaton* <note>The second-lowest note.</note> thinner by a whole tone and sharpening all other notes by a tone—the entire resulting order will be sharper than it was before the tension was applied. Thus, the entire constitution becomes higher, and the **Hypophrygian** mode is created.

Therefore, "Tropes" are not named because they begin in low voices and then "turn" themselves into high voices to return to the end and finish low (as some have chosen to say). On the contrary, there are those who do not start in the low notes but in the high ones, as will be said shortly when we treat each one individually. First, we shall discuss certain general signs for everyone, through which an unknown chant may be recognized—to see if it is corrupted or well-composed—so that we might know how to correctly compose a new one.

It explains how the Hypodorian and Hypophrygian modes are derived through the shifting "tension" or pitch of the celestial order.</summary>

# Third Treatise

->Second part (that is, counterpoint)<-

**Chapter one, in which is placed the knowledge of certain voices and dissonances.**

Thus far we have proceeded in another manner, so as to speak only of voices <note>original: "uocibus"; in this context, "voices" refers to musical pitches or notes</note> brought forth successively; now, however, let it be briefly explained which of those, when two or more are struck or sung together, sound in consonance or dissonance. How indeed consonance or dissonance enters the ear, and whether the opinion of Plato or Nicomachus is true <note>A reference to the debate between Plato’s mathematical idealism and the Pythagorean/Nicomachean views on the perception of sound</note>—since this is a speculative matter—we shall endeavor to declare and define in the second book according to the strength of our intellect. 

But now, because we aim to draw practitioners <note>students of practical music performance rather than abstract theory</note> gradually to this doctrine, let us briefly and as if by way of a corollary explain those things that pertain to practice. It has been said that the whole body of music is one <term>diapason [an octave]</term>, which consists of eight voices. If, therefore, we declare these eight voices by relating them to one another, it will suffice. 

Therefore, by way of instruction, you should know that equal voices <note>unisons</note> agree; the second dissonates with the first; the third consonates with the first; the fourth alone discords with the first. However, the fifth and sixth concord; the seventh disagrees; the eighth sounds together most perfectly. Just as we have done by referring all others to the first, so we do to the second and third and the rest; thus, the third dissonates with the second, but the fourth consonates, and so on for the others in this way: the <unclear>established</unclear> order will show. 

To the end that we may know how to avoid the inharmonious and choose the harmonious, we shall say that the disagreeing voices are three: namely, the second, fourth, and seventh. The second is indeed major or minor, being a whole tone or a semitone. Similarly the fourth, because it is a <term>diatessaron [perfect fourth]</term> or a <term>tritonus [augmented fourth]</term>. The seventh in the same way, because it is a major or minor <term>heptas [seventh]</term>. 

But the concords are the unison, third, fifth, sixth, and eighth. Concerning the unison, however, there is no doubt, because the same thing does not differ from itself; nor for that reason is it counted among the consonances. For a consonance is not a concord of like things, but of unlike things brought back into one, or the blended sound of unlike things falling sweetly upon the ears. Dissonance, however, is a "harsh and unpleasant collision," as <term>Boethius</term> says <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), the most influential musical authority of the Middle Ages</note>, because each sound strives to reach the ear intact, and neither yields to the other—as we shall demonstrate more extensively in the theoretical sections. 

Yet the unison in music is like unity in arithmetic: the beginning of numbers, the fountain and origin of consonances. Therefore, setting aside the unison, we shall say there are four concordant species: namely the third, fifth, sixth, and eighth. Of these, two are perfect—namely the fifth and the eighth—and two are imperfect—the third and the sixth. For they are called "imperfect" because

<summary>This page marks the beginning of a treatise on counterpoint, defining which musical intervals are considered consonant (harmonious) or dissonant (clashing) according to Boethian theory.</summary>
<keywords>counterpoint, consonance, dissonance, Boethius, intervals, unison, octave, diapason, musical theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the classification of musical intervals and how they are perceived by the ear. It focuses on the distinction between perfect and imperfect consonances and introduces the concept of octave equivalence and interval inversion.</meta>

## Second part

They are variable <note>The author is referring to thirds and sixths</note>, because through the addition or subtraction of a semitone <term>semitone [the smallest interval in this system, roughly a half-step]</term> they do not change their nature as a consonance, but always sound well; this refers to the major third <note>original: "tertia ditonalis"</note> or the minor third <note>original: "semiditonalis"</note>. They differ only in this: that one is called major and the other minor. The same must be said of the sixth: a fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note> with a whole tone or with a semitone is either major or minor. 

The octave <note>original: "octaua"</note>, however, accepts neither increase nor decrease without becoming dissonant and discordant, because it must always contain five whole tones and two semitones, neither more nor less. Therefore, it is called the "most perfect" and "equal-sounding" <note>original: "equisona"</note>, because it seems to sound just like the first note, much like a unison. Hence, if a man and a boy sing together, they seem to be in unison, and yet they are at the interval of an octave. 

The fifth <note>original: "quinta"</note>, however, if it receives an increase or decrease of a semitone, either passes into the nature of a sixth or is converted into the harshness and discrepancy of a <term>tritone [an interval of three whole tones, historically called "the devil in music" for its instability]</term>. For this reason, it is indeed perfect, but not in the same way as the octave. 

Other mathematical reasons we shall discuss in our theoretical writings <note>original: "theorias"</note>, which practitioners would not care much for if we placed them here, nor would they even be able to grasp them, since it is necessary for them first to understand proportions and proportionalities. Let them, therefore, agree with the reasons stated here, as they are concerned entirely with practice. Thus, like children eating milky foods <note>A common medieval metaphor for simplified introductory teaching</note>, they shall be led toward harder meats. 

These things being known: if we intend or diminish an octave from any species of consonance, we will without doubt produce that same species. For it has often been said that the whole of harmony is the octave <note>original: "diapason"</note>. Therefore, whatever is said of the first note and its octave will be similar, differing only in this: that one sounds higher <note>original: "acutius"</note> and the other lower <note>original: "grauius"</note>. 

Therefore:
*   the eighth will be like the source, 
*   the ninth like the second, 
*   the tenth like the third, 
*   the eleventh like the fourth, 
*   the twelfth like the fifth, 
*   the thirteenth like the sixth. 

But the fifteenth disagrees like the seventh <note>This likely refers to the interval of a fourteenth, or is a specific pedagogical distinction, as the text immediately clarifies the fifteenth below</note>. The fifteenth is equal-sounding just like the eighth. In the same way, one must calculate from the fifteenth up to the twenty-second. Thus, there are only four different species of consonances, which are frequently replicated through the addition of octaves. The first shall be called "simple," the others "composite," and the third "doubly composite" <note>original: "decomposite"</note>, as is clear in the figure <note>This refers to a diagram that likely appeared in the original manuscript or a related copy</note>. 

And so, if the "parent" species is dissonant, perfect, or imperfect, so too is the "child" species produced from it. This is the production of secondary consonances whenever the species is higher than the <term>plainchant [the base melody, or 'cantus planus']</term> by an octave, or below it when it is diminished. 

But what if it is done conversely? It must be said that if one intends an octave from a lower species, or diminishes it from a higher one: from a third, a sixth is produced; from a sixth, a third; and from a fifth, a fourth. Therefore, the third and the sixth are of the same condition because they are imperfect. But the fifth and fourth especially correspond, concerning which we speak in our theoretical works. But in this...

It explains the principle of octave equivalence—where intervals repeat their character every eight notes—and introduces the concept of interval inversion.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general classification of musical intervals to the practical application of these sounds in "organum"—an early form of polyphonic music where a second voice is added to a plainchant melody. # First Treatise

I promise that I shall speak about this at length in a later volume when we treat the subject of multiple voices. For the present, however, let it be enough to know that as much as the fifth possesses perfection, so much does the fourth approach dissonance <term>among the consonances [the fourth was often considered a "borderline" interval in medieval theory, acting as a dissonance in some contexts and a consonance in others]</term>. This is similar to how a sixth is produced from a third, and vice versa: if the "creator" <note>original: "creans"; referring to the original interval before it is inverted</note> is major, the "created" interval becomes minor, and vice versa. The same <unclear>also</unclear> applies to dissonances, such as those formed from a seventh and vice versa: if the creator is major, the created will be minor, and vice versa.

Now, because we are concerned with organizing <term>organum [the practice of adding a harmony voice to a pre-existing chant melody]</term> over a given song, we will first insert some brief rules of the ancients.

The first rule is: the beginning and the end must be in a perfect species <note>a "perfect" interval, such as an octave or fifth</note> or in a unison.
The second: it is not permitted to make one similar perfect interval after another, nor a <term>unison [two voices singing the exact same pitch]</term>.
The third: two or more imperfect intervals can be placed one after another.
The fourth: if the chant continues on the same pitch for two or more notes, the organum voice should not remain on that same pitch, but should move through different positions.
The fifth rule: a major sixth resolves by joining to an octave, while a minor sixth moves apart to a fifth. Likewise, a major third expands to a fifth, while a minor third leads to a unison.
The sixth: if the tenor <note>the "holding" voice that carries the original melody</note> ascends, the counter-voice <note>original: "Corruptus"; likely a scribal error or local term for the discant or counter-melody</note> should take care to descend.

The first rule is explained thus: when we begin to create organum, we should place the voice at the fifth or the octave, or at some other interval composed of these for the convenience of the singer's range, and also at the unison. When we finish, the same must be done. This is because other consonances are not of such great perfection as these. Therefore, we ought to use the better ones at the beginning and at the end; in the middle, however, it is permitted to intersperse <term>imperfect [intervals like thirds and sixths]</term> ones.

The second rule is understood this way: we should not give—that is, sound—two perfect consonances twice with the tenor ascending or descending in the same perfect species. For then, the progression would seem to be the same voice. For if the tenor moves from D to C and the organum moves from L to K, being at the interval of an octave, it would seem to be the same voice. The same applies to the unison; even if it says H to G, it is prohibited for the same reason—not because it is an absolute similarity, but because the similarity is so great.

However, <term>Tristanus de Silva [a medieval musical authority]</term> says that in the case of the fifth, it is not totally prohibited, since a fifth can follow a fifth, provided that one is a <term>semidiapente [a diminished fifth]</term> and the other is a <term>diapente [a perfect fifth]</term>. We find this in the songs of Johannes Enchiriadis <note>original: "sois enpzantis"; likely a corruption of "Scolis Enchiriadis," a famous 9th-century musical treatise</note> and in other older works. But this is not granted in "integer" notes <note>plain, long notes of equal value</note>, but rather in "broken" notes—that is, in the <term>diminution of notes [the practice of breaking one long note into many fast, decorative notes]</term>, which we will discuss shortly.

Nevertheless, dissimilar perfect intervals can be permitted; that is, an octave after a fifth, or a fifth after an octave. This is done in this manner: if the tenor moves D-E and C-D, and the organum moves L-M and K-H, and so on for others.

The third rule is understood thus: if the tenor ascends E-D-E, the organum can ascend in the same way E-F-G. The same applies in descent and in the same way with the sixth and in other places. However, the one who is organizing should not think...

It also introduces the concept of interval inversion and the use of contrary motion between voices.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for "organum," specifically addressing the "Second Part" of the rules regarding interval progressions. ## Second Part

If the <term>tenor [the pre-existing plainchant melody held by the lower voice]</term> repeats for two or more notes on the same pitch, one can perform two or more imperfect intervals at that same position with the <term>organum [the accompanying harmony voice]</term>. Thus is defined the "straight fourth," although many do not strictly forbid this, especially in the composition of three or four voices, where it is permitted.

The fifth rule is explained as follows: if the tenor descends from D to C, we ascend from B to K <note>original: "b: k"; these letters refer to specific pitch locations in the medieval gamut</note>. This is because if the tenor descends from E to D, or another similar place, it is not appropriate to make the organum intervals K and L, because that would create a minor sixth. If we wish to create that interval, we must raise the pitch of K if we ascend from a lower part, or sustain it if we descend from a higher voice. If this were to be drawn, it should be represented thus: <note>The text indicates a musical symbol or diagram here.</note>. From this follows a noteworthy instruction: if the chant descends from C to D, it is not permitted to perform the organum sequence K-K-L. This is because one of two errors would occur: either the music would move from a minor sixth directly into an octave, or unequal voices would be sung in the same position. In its place, one should perform L-K-L or B-K-L. In the first case, the voice descends only by an <term>implied semitone [semitonium subintellectum; a pitch adjusted by the singer even if not written, a practice known as musica ficta]</term>; in the second, it ascends by a <term>ditone [a major third, literally "two tones"]</term>. If this is drawn, it should be marked as above. One must pay attention to what we said in the first part regarding the implied semitone and consider when a note is raised or lowered from its proper place. One must avoid perfect species <note>perfect intervals like the octave or fifth</note> if they are used incorrectly: for instance, if the tenor sings D-F-G and the F is raised from its proper place, the organum should not create a unison or a perfect interval upon it.

In the same way, if the tenor sings D-C-D, returning immediately to the first pitch, the note C will not sound as a perfect interval. It is different, however, if there is a pause on C or if a <note>cadence or phrase break</note> is made there. Furthermore, the way a minor sixth resolves to a fifth is demonstrated así: if the tenor remains on the same pitch for two or more beats, such as D-D-D, the organum should sing B-B-B. Similarly, if the chant descends by a semitone (either written or implied), and the organum is at the interval of a fifth, it should remain on that same fifth. For example, if the tenor is F-E-F, the organum will be K-K-K.

However, some theorists prohibit wandering through three notes on the same pitch, even if the types of intervals are different. They claim this is because the <term>counterpoint [the added voice]</term> would then seem to be a <term>cantus firmus [a fixed, slow-moving melody]</term>. But this is not a significant obstacle, because both voices can be correctly called "organum" and "tenor" when we place full notes in both. Yet, because we do not wish to depart from common usage, we forbid them from making harmonies in this way: namely, that the organum should not rest on the same pitch for more than two notes. Therefore, in the previous example, one should sing B-K-K.

Furthermore, the movement of a major third to a fifth is exemplified thus: let the tenor be B-G and the organum K-♮-K <note>original uses the "square B" symbol ▢ for B-natural</note>. For this reason, just as with the sixth, care must be taken: if the tenor sings G-E-D, the organum should not perform G-G-H, but rather E-G-B or ♮-G-B. Here, E-G is a ditone and ♮-G is a <term>semiditone [a minor third]</term>, and thus it creates a major third with...

It specifically addresses the use of implied semitones (musica ficta) and the resolution of thirds and sixths into perfect fifths.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of "organum" (polyphonic accompaniment), moving from basic interval rules to specific melodic progressions, the distinction between major and minor thirds, and the introduction of "fuga" or imitative counterpoint.</meta>

# First Treatise

<note>original: "Tractatus primus"</note>

Concerning the <term>tenor [the lower, foundational voice]</term>, since it is raised in its own proper way, if it is to be written down, it should be marked as shown above. Therefore, if the tenor sings the notes **f d c**, the <term>organum [the accompanying harmony voice]</term> should not perform **f f g**; however, it could perform **b f g** or **d f g**. Note that **b** to **f** is a <term>semiditone [a minor third]</term>, while **d** to **f** is a <term>ditone [a major third]</term>. If this is to be written, it must be marked with the symbol **□** <note>This "square" symbol represents "B-quadratum," the ancestor of the modern natural sign, indicating a "hard" or natural B pitch.</note>. Furthermore, a smaller interval may be joined with the tenor; for example, if the tenor sings **f e f**, the notes **b g f** sound most excellent. Likewise, if the tenor is **f g b**, the organum should be **k b b**, and not **k □ b**. For this reason, one must be careful: if the tenor is **f f g**, the organum should not perform **k b a**; or if the tenor is **g g h**, the organum should not perform **l □ b**. However, **l b b** works well. If the tenor should sing in this manner: **g f g**, the organum can perform **g b g** or **□ b g**. This is because that third, even if it appears to be a major third, is understood to be made minor.

Many composers do not observe this distinction between the <term>major third</term> and the <term>minor third</term>. Because of this, although their compositions might delight the listener at first hearing (since the song is new), they become more and more displeasing as they are used more frequently day by day. These singers are ignorant of the causes, both those we have already mentioned and others regarding <term>diminution [the breaking of long notes into shorter, decorative ones]</term> which we shall discuss shortly.

Thus, <term>counterpoint</term> <note>original: "contrapunctus"</note> ought to proceed to the nearest <term>species [types of intervals]</term>: such as from a sixth to an octave or a fifth; or from a third to a <term>unison</term> or a fifth. This same rule applies to composite and re-composite intervals <note>meaning intervals larger than an octave, like a tenth or twelfth</note>. 

But sometimes the organum sings by a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>, a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, or even an <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note>. In those cases, it moves from a third into an octave. For example, if the tenor is **f e**, the organum is **b m**; or if the tenor is **g f**, the organum is **g b**. Or if the tenor is **d d c d d e d**, a good organal part would be **f n m b l k l r**. The same applies in other <term>modes [musical scales]</term>.

However, the best method of organizing music is when the organum imitates the tenor in its ascent or descent, but not at the same time. Instead, after one or more notes, it begins to perform the same melody or a similar one at the <term>fourth</term>, <term>fifth</term>, or even the <term>octave</term>. This can occur in its composite or re-composite forms, either above or below. Practical musicians call this method a <term>fugue</term> <note>original: "fuga"; literally "a flight," where one voice chases another</note>, because one voice follows the other with a similar <term>arsis</term> <note>the "lifting" or upbeat of a melody</note> or <term>thesis</term> <note>the "setting down" or downbeat of a melody</note>. 

For instance, if the tenor sings: **l n m l n m o**, the organum can follow it at the fourth below after the first note and sing **b k □ b k □ l**. Likewise, at the fifth above, keeping the same pause, the organum would sing **p q p q s**. The same logic applies to their composite intervals, or at the <term>unison</term> or <term>octave</term>. If the tenor is **d e f g d c f e d**, the organum can sound the same melody in the octave after two notes: **l m n m o l k n m l**. The same can be done in the unison or in the octaves above and below. 

In these examples, we have placed those final notes in the organum without matching notes in the tenor simply to demonstrate the similarity. We assume that the voices which follow in the tenor will not discord with them. If a fugue begins to sound discordant in its similarity, a "dissimilarity" should be made immediately so that it does not violate the rules mentioned above. The sixth rule is declared as follows: if the chant ascends...

# Second Part

...leads the voice: the <term>discant [the upper voice]</term> should descend to a suitable interval <note>original: "speciem"</note> according to the assigned rules. And if the <term>tenor [the foundational lower voice]</term> descends, the <term>counterpoint [the accompanying voice]</term> should ascend; this is what is most frequently observed in counterpoint, for it is from this contrary motion that the term itself is said to have been taken <note>The word "counterpoint" derives from "punctus contra punctum," or point against point, implying voices moving in opposition.</note>.

**Chapter Two, in which the error of some is rebuked and the truth is demonstrated.**

All the aforementioned rules are understood to apply mostly when the tenor moves upward or downward by step. But what if it moves by leaps and turns? It must be said that when it proceeds in such a way, it is better for the voices of the <term>organum [the polyphonic accompaniment]</term> to unite. For instance, if the tenor sings **o b b**, the organum shall perform **l k l**, or **l m l**, or even **l m n** <note>These letters refer to specific pitches in the medieval gamut, a system of naming notes that predates modern letter names.</note>. The same applies in other modes of the <term>fifth [diapente]</term>. But if there is a <term>fourth [diatessaron]</term> in this manner, such as **d f e**, the organum should be **l k k**; or if the tenor is **c f d**, the organum should be **k k l**.

<term>Ugolino [of Forlì]</term> <note>Ugolino of Forlì (c. 1380–1452) was a famous Italian music theorist whose work "Declaratio musicae disciplinae" codified many rules of counterpoint.</note> set down common rules for certain unrefined musicians <note>original: "barbaris metricis"</note> regarding all intervals, both simple and composite. Some of these rules hold true, while others do not. But so that the truth may shine forth, and falsehood may be put to shame and confounded, I will briefly explain them here, starting first with the ascending <term>unison</term>.

A third should be placed below the unison if the tenor moves up by one step. If it moves by a third or a fourth, it shall hold a fifth below. If it ascends a fifth, it will end only at the <term>octave [diapason]</term>.

**Second rule concerning the descending unison:**
A third should be placed above. If the tenor descends by one step, it remains a unison. If it descends by a third or fourth, it moves to a fifth. If it descends by a fifth or more, it seeks an octave. If it passes through more intervals, the order of reason will teach the way. <margin>Third rule: concerning the ascending third. <insert>ascending</insert></margin> 

A unison should be made if the third moves up by one step. If it moves up by more, a unison should eventually be made. The third moves down if the tenor ascends a third or fourth.

->**Fourth rule concerning the descending third**<-

A fifth will be produced three times if the tenor descends only a third. If there are more steps, a fifth will terminate them. If it oversees a third or fourth octave, a fifth will be made above, if it is joined with an octave. <margin>Fifth rule: concerning the ascending fifth.</margin>

A fifth seeks a third if an ascent of one step is made. You should call it a unison if you intend a third or a fifth. <margin>Sixth rule: concerning the descending fifth.</margin>

The fifth seeks an octave if the tenor descends only one step. An octave will be a sixth if it is associated with another note. After a fifth, an octave will be made if it descends a third below, or if it allows a fourth or fifteenth to be reached. <margin>Seventh rule: concerning the ascending sixth.</margin>

The sixth desires a third if it reaches toward a note above. <margin>Eighth rule: concerning the descending sixth.</margin>

The sixth wants an octave below if the tenor moves down by one step; and more should be made if they precede the octave. A sixteenth wants a third descending below.

->**Ninth rule concerning the ascending octave**<-

After an octave, a fifth follows if the song <note>original: "cantus"</note> moves up by one step; if it sings a fourth or fifth...
