<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on the rules of counterpoint, moving from the definitions of contrary motion to a specific critique of fourteen rules governing harmonic intervals between the tenor and the organum.</meta>

# First Tractate

...the [voice] rightfully demands a third. The tenth rule [pertaining to] descending from an octave: an octave [moves] to a tenth if it only drops by one. If it was a third, then it becomes a twelfth. The eleventh rule [pertaining to] ascending from a tenth: a tenth wants an octave increased by only one; if it exceeds more, then the fifth will take its place. The twelfth rule [pertaining to] descending from a tenth: a descending tenth desires to have a twelfth. The 13th rule [pertaining to] ascending from a twelfth: ascending by one, the twelfth seeks the tenth. The octave, third, fourth, and fifth, and the fifth following the 14th rule descending from a twelfth: if a fifth is joined with a tenth, let a twelfth be made; if a third is joined with a tenth, let it be given; a third with a tenth demands a fifteenth <note>original: "quintaz undecima," likely referring to the quintadecima (fifteenth) and the eleventh</note> and an eleventh.

---

<margin>Note!</margin>
**T**he first rule is refuted in this way: if the <term>tenor [the foundational lower voice]</term> sings **f g**, the <term>organum [the accompanying upper voice]</term> can say **f e** just as **f e** [if it] ascends a third. For he himself says the organum remains better in the third than if it goes to the fifth. Because if it has a fourth, such as [tenor] **g k**, the organum will rightly do **g c**. And if there is a <term>fifth [diapente]</term> such as **f k**, the organum will rightly do **f f** <note>This indicates the voices meeting in a unison on the note 'f'.</note>. The second rule [is also rebuked]: if the tenor descends **f d**, the organum will do just as well with **f f** as with **f h**. The third rule, however, is good enough. The 4th rule: if the tenor sings **f e d c**, the organum will rightly do **h g □ k** <note>The symbol □ refers to *b-quadratum*, or B-natural.</note> just as **b g f g**. Similarly, if the tenor should make **f d**, the organum will do just as well with **b b** as with **b l**. The 5th rule is rejected similarly because if the tenor sings **f b**, the organum does just as well—and better—with **k f** or **k k** than with **k b**. The sixth rule passes well enough; truly if the tenor sings **f c**, the organum [does] well with **k l** as with **l m**. Similarly, if **g e** is the tenor, the organum [does] well with **l l** as with **l m**. The seventh rule is quite true: if the tenor is **e f**, the organum [does] well with **k l** as with **l h**, because from a minor sixth <note>original: "sex.ª miozi"</note> we move well into a fifth. The 8th rule is good; but when the tenor says **f d**, the organum can say **l l** just as **l n**. The ninth is refuted because if the tenor says **e f**, the organum can say **k l** as with **l h**. The tenth [rule] is rebuked because if the tenor sings **e c**, the organum will better do **m m** than **m o**. The eleventh, however, departs greatly from the truth. Because when the tenor makes leaps and turns <note>original: "anfractus," referring to complex or winding melodic movements</note>, the organum ought to consolidate <note>original: "coadunare," to bring the voices closer together or into harmony</note> its voices. For if both [voices] were to run about through leaps and turns, it would be called a contest <note>original: "certamen"</note> rather than harmony. As if the [tenor] says **c d**, the organum will do better with **m b** than **m l**; and then just as the tenor begins to make leaps and turns, the organum should begin to unite. For example, when the tenor is **e e**, the organum is **m □**; when the tenor is **c f**, the organum is **m l**; and when the tenor is **c g**, the organum is **m l**. In this example, if you pay attention, there is the greatest agreement, since the tenor begins to sing gradually from the second up to the fifth, while the organum has united its voices with it from the fifth down to the second.

<summary>This page presents a detailed academic critique of the fourteen rules of counterpoint, arguing for melodic "consolidation" and harmony over complex, conflicting leaps between voices.</summary>
<keywords>organum, tenor, counterpoint, musical intervals, music theory, polyphony, melodic leaps, medieval notation</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the rules of counterpoint, specifically refuting certain "superfluous" rules and transitioning into the Second Part of the tractate which focuses on the psychological effect of harmony and the practical application of modes in polyphony.</meta>

<header>Second part</header>

# Second part

In this way, harmony generates in the minds of listeners a certain innate sweetness that cannot be explained in speech. Truly, our singers consider this very little; rather, they believe that whatever pleases their imagination or fantasy according to an accidental disposition happens to everyone. It is for this reason that the common people are not as readily drawn toward music—or, to speak more truly, toward new harmony—as they used to be in antiquity. However, regarding these matters, we shall speak most clearly in the second book. We have said these things to lead certain singers away from erroneous opinions and to bring them back to a true knowledge of music.

Other rules are sufficiently consistent with the truth, but they should be rejected in this regard because they are superfluous; for beyond the <term>octave [diapason]</term>, there is always a repetition of the first [interval]. And if it is said that we speak because of the fifth or the fourth, for that reason we have given the method of composing a fifth from a fourth and vice versa. And the rest we leave to the diligent investigator; for with these few rules, the whole art of <term>counterpoint [contrapuncti]</term> or of <term>organum [the early practice of polyphony]</term> can be condensed.

Truly, the rest of the things that might happen concerning <term>organization [the practice of adding an accompanying voice]</term> we leave to the judgment of the singers, provided they take care not to do anything against the rules; for even if such things are not strictly approved, they do not depart from the truth. Let them also observe the manner of <term>arsis and thesis [the rising and falling of the melodic line or beat]</term>. That is, from the lower voice to its own higher voice, let there be an ordered motion like a <term>trope [a melodic embellishment]</term>. There should be almost innate pauses when giving <term>perfect species [perfect intervals like the unison, fifth, and octave]</term>.

For instance, if the tone or mode is of the first [the Dorian mode], let them frequent perfect consonances in **d**, **h** <note>original: "b"; in this context, the author likely refers to the "hard b" or B-natural, often written as "h" in Germanic notation</note>, or **l**; sometimes in **f** or **o**, rarely in **g** or **k**. Never, however, in **e** or **b** <note>original: "b"; here referring to the "soft b" or B-flat</note>, which must be understood with a distinction, because elsewhere they can be made anywhere. Let the performer of the <term>organum [the added voice]</term> also preserve the <term>pneumata [melodic phrases or breathings]</term> of the tropes so that phrase corresponds to phrase.

For if the phrases of the <term>tenor [the foundational voice]</term> are of the first mode, those of the organum shall be as well. For example, if the tenor is:
->**f d e d g d c g e f d**<-

The Organum should not do:
->**h □ m l □ □ k □ k : h □**<-
<note>The square symbol likely represents a specific pitch or rhythmic value that cannot be rendered as a standard letter.</note>

But rather:
->**h l m l l n m l m k l**<-

Because the first organization is in the <term>Phrygian mode</term>, but the second is in the <term>Dorian mode</term>. Lest anyone think these small examples are not enough to grasp the whole doctrine, we provide a more subtle way above and below through the whole <term>hand [the Guidonian hand, a mnemonic system for the musical scale]</term> so they may arrange these through lines and spaces. Then, let them try the same thing that was in the Dorian, and place it in the Phrygian, Lydian, and Mixolydian, and let the same be done for their <term>plagal [the secondary, lower-range versions of the modes]</term>.

And when any <term>species [interval type]</term> does not make good consonances, let it be raised or lowered by a sign <note>referring to accidentals such as sharps or flats</note> so that the consonance receives its proper quantity, or discards whatever it has in excess. So that everything that has been said may be more easily remembered, let this example be placed before your eyes:

**Tenor:** **d d e d f e f d c d e d. f g f g h g b b d f e f g e d**
**Organum:** **□ l b k l l m h l m l k l. b □ l l k b □ b k l b g b b □ l l l**

<summary>This page discusses the psychological power of harmony and argues that many traditional rules of counterpoint are superfluous because intervals repeat at the octave. It provides practical instructions on matching the melodic phrases (pneumata) between voices and maintaining modal consistency.</summary>
<keywords>harmony, counterpoint, organum, Dorian mode, Phrygian mode, Guidonian hand, consonances, arsis and thesis, pneumata</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of traditional musical pedagogy, moving from a general discussion of harmony's psychological effects into a technical refutation of Guidonian hexachord theory and a proposal for a more streamlined method of understanding counterpoint and intervals.</meta>

# First Treatise

It is clear from these few preceding examples that the entire art of <term>counterpoint</term> <note>original: "contrapuncti"; the practice of composing a second melody against a plainchant</note> is concluded through the variation of examples across different positions, through both <term>ficta</term> <note>musica ficta, or the use of accidentals outside the standard scale</note> and <term>recta</term> <note>musica recta, the standard medieval musical scale</note> music similarly varied. And thus, through the same example in different <term>tropes</term> <note>modes or melodic formulas</note>, by a small sacred mutation, a vast variety grows. For if a single <term>pneuma</term> <note>original: "pneuma"; a melodic phrase or breath-length of music</note> is varied in so many ways, as was said regarding the tropes, how much more will it be varied with different consonances? Thus, what has been said so far in this section would suffice. 

However, it has been shown that in the first part of his art, <term>Guido</term> <note>Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the most influential music theorist of the Middle Ages</note> attributed so many and such great superfluous, inconvenient, useless, long-winded, and redundant things to music. His followers, limping along under blind leadership and believing themselves to have investigated subtle matters, have remained in worse, more shameful, and more useless conditions. 

There are those who, as they say, when they attempt to perform counterpoint, do not allow the <term>hexachord</term> <note>a six-note scale segment (ut-re-mi-fa-sol-la)</note> to go above or below the plainchant being sung, which they call *gamma-b*. It is also said that their entire <term>Hand</term> <note>The Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic device for finding notes</note> is contained between *Gamma* and *E-la*. Since it agrees with one hexachord assumed as the theme, they create seven "gammas" <note>here meaning seven systems or scale-ranges</note> according to the seven hexachords, and they name them as follows:

1. **Hard B bass** <note>original: "♮ bassum"; the lowest hexachord starting on G</note>
2. **Natural bass** <note>original: "natura bassam"; the hexachord starting on C</note>
3. **Soft B bass** <note>original: "b molle bassum"; the hexachord starting on F, using B-flat</note>
4. **Hard B middle** <note>original: "♮ medianum"</note>
5. **High natural** <note>original: "natura altaz"</note>
6. **High soft B** <note>original: "b molle altum"</note>
7. **High hard B** <note>original: "♮ altum"</note>

We shall demonstrate with the firmest reasons that this is both superfluous and deficient. They establish "gammas" that could be investigated through other means, and they omit others that are necessary, namely <term>conjunctions</term>. We, however, who care to flee obscurities, bring truth into the light, confound falsehoods, and avoid prolixity, will provide a very easy method of finding all the gammas, fulfilling their deficiencies, and avoiding falsehood.

Let us therefore take the first hexachord—namely, the <term>tetrachord</term> <note>a four-note scale segment</note> that ends in **D-sol-re**. We shall speak using their own terms. Let us look through the whole Hand to see which voices accord with it, and thus we shall compose it:

*   **Gamma-ut** from this hexachord will have *re* because it is a unison; *fa* because it is a third; *la* because it is a fifth.
*   **A-re** will have *ut* as a third below; *mi* as a unison; *sol* as a third above.
*   **B-mi**: *re* as a third below; it will lack a unison; *la* as a third above.
*   **C-fa-ut**: *ut, mi, sol*.
*   **D-sol-re**: *ut, re, fa, la*.
*   **E-la-mi**: *re, mi, sol*.
*   **F-fa-ut**: *ut, mi, fa, la*.
*   **G-sol-re-ut**: *re, fa, sol*.
*   **A-la-mi-re**: *mi, fa, la*.
*   **B-flat [B-fa]**: *fa, la*.
*   **B-natural [B-mi]**: *re, la*.

Up to this point, there was a difference in every location. But afterward, **C-sol-fa-ut** will be held just like **C-fa-ut** (as *ut, mi, sol*), and **D-la-sol-re** just like **D-sol-re** (as *ut, re, fa, la*). They do not differ except that if the first ones are simple, these are composite. Regarding the opposition of these notes, we have already spoken above. In some places, we say that if we wish to compose other gammas very easily and without great labor, we shall do so with a prepared method. **C-fa-ut** is in the fifth place from this conjunction, and thus the Gs will respond to each other in the fifth places. We shall therefore argue así: there is as much distance between S and F as between **Gamma-ut** and **D-sol-re**...

<summary>The author critiques the followers of Guido d'Arezzo for their overly complex system of seven hexachords and proposes a simplified method for determining harmonic consonances (unisons, thirds, and fifths) across the musical scale.</summary>
<keywords>counterpoint, hexachord, Guido d'Arezzo, musical gamut, tetrachord, intervals, musical theory, Guidonian Hand</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical deconstruction of the Guidonian hexachord system, specifically focusing on the mathematical and intervallic relationships between various pitches in the gamut (the medieval musical scale).</meta>

## Second Part

Thus <term>E-la-mi</term> <note>The pitch E in the medieval system, which could be sung with the syllables la, mi, or (less commonly) ut.</note> is related to <term>A-re</term> <note>The pitch A.</note> because in both, the <term>solmization</term> <note>The practice of singing music using syllables like ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la.</note> ratio of *mi-sol* is demonstrative; for <term>E-la-mi</term> is as far from its <term>gamma</term> <note>Original: "gama"; here referring to the starting note of a hexachord.</note> as <term>A-re</term> is from that to which it is compared. 

But when we come to <term>F-fa-ut</term> and <term>B-mi</term>, there is not such a distance, because one is shown to be distant by a semitone and the other by a tone. Nor do the <term>signs</term> <note>Original: "signa"; the specific pitch positions or symbols on the musical scale.</note> themselves stand apart from each other by a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"; a perfect fifth interval.</note> in the same way as the <term>hexachords</term> <note>original: "exacorda"; six-note scale segments.</note> or the <term>gamut</term> itself. Wherefore, it does not proceed in the same way; on which account care must be taken both in these and in others when it so happens, lest a perfect dissonance be placed in one of those signs. 

Thus, therefore, <term>B-mi</term> will only have *re* and *la* at the third [interval] below and above. But in the place or sign of <term>B-fa</term> <insert>☐</insert> <note>The "square B" or B-natural symbol.</note> <term>mi</term>, which has a double place, the same must always be done; since the first <term>hexachord</term> possesses a perfect [fifth] with *fa*, but with *mi* it is different, and so also in its octaves. We place the third <term>hexachord</term> in this way: the lower note distant from it comes to <term>Gamma-ut</term> <note>The lowest note of the medieval gamut, equivalent to modern G2.</note>. And just as one <term>hexachord</term> relates to another, so too will the signs located in them differ. 

Hence, just as it is in <term>D-sol-re</term>, so it is in the third in <term>A-re</term> and in others likewise; wherefore <term>F-fa-ut</term> and <term>B-fa-re-sol</term> hold only so much, but <insert>☐</insert> <term>B-mi</term> additionally obtains *mi* as a fifth. Thus when it reaches high <term>E-la-mi</term>, both *mi* and high <term>E-la-mi</term> hold *ut-mi-sol-la*, but <term>B-fa-la</term> will lack a perfect fifth with *mi* because it lacks a perfect octave. 

Hence there is a common saying among practitioners: <term>mi against fa</term> <note>original: "mi contra fa"; the famous prohibition of the tritone, a harsh interval created when singing the syllable 'mi' against 'fa' (such as B-natural against F).</note>; nor should they ever stand in a perfect species, but in imperfect ones they are fine, because they admit major and minor [qualities]. However, they were in this part defective, since that same thing can be said of other syllables, as we shall show shortly. 

When you wish to set forth the fourth <term>hexachord</term> from these, the third <term>fifth</term> extended from <term>D-sol-re</term> falls there. Hence, signs distant by a fifth will have the same properties; for just as <term>A-re</term> is in the third, so is <term>E-la-mi</term> in the fourth, because of *re-fa-la*. But since <term>B-mi</term> and <term>F-fa-ut</term> do not differ by a fifth, <term>F-fa-ut</term> will lack a perfect [fifth], but the fourth in <insert>☐</insert> <term>B-mi</term> is like the third in <term>E-la-mi</term>, because of *ut-re-fa-la*. 

But <term>B-fa</term> will lack perfect [intervals]. If you attempt to discern it, it is distant from that remiss note which <term>A-re</term> denotes; therefore signs which are distant will have the same properties. Hence, just as it is in the fourth in <term>E-la-mi</term>, so it is in the fifth in <term>B-mi</term> and in others distant in the same way. But in <insert>☐</insert> <term>B-mi</term>, the fifth will have what the fourth held in high <term>E-la-mi</term>; yet <term>B-fa</term>, because it is proportioned to none in these two hexachords or scales, is therefore most distant from all others; since it will only have *mi-fa*, a major and minor sixth, and in the high register those composed of them. 

And when you attempt to create the sixth <term>hexachord</term>, you should intend it to be distant from the first, located between <term>A-re</term> and <term>B-mi</term>, a <term>conjuncta</term> <note>original: "coniuncta"; a note outside the standard gamut, used to navigate difficult intervals.</note> where just as <term>Gamma-ut</term> is in the first, so <term>C-fa-ut</term> is in the sixth, and so on for other similarly distant places. But <insert>☐</insert> <term>B-mi</term> will lack perfect [intervals]; and if you now consider completing the seventh <term>hexachord</term>, you should intend a fourth from the sixth, which will fall between <term>E-la-mi</term> and <term>D-sol-re</term>. Therefore the signs which are distant, becoming higher from that distance, will hold themselves just as...

It warns against the 'mi contra fa' dissonance and begins to describe the construction of the sixth and seventh hexachords using conjunctae.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the medieval hexachord system, specifically detailing how to calculate and complete different "gamuts" (scales) by comparing the structures of C-fa-ut, F-fa-ut, and B-mi. It builds on the previous page's focus on intervals and solmization syllables.</meta>

# First Treatise

As <term>C-fa-ut</term> <note>The pitch C in the medieval system.</note> is in the sixth, so <term>F-fa-ut</term> <note>The pitch F.</note> is in the seventh: just as the sixth is in <term>F-fa-ut</term>, so the seventh is in <term>B-fa</term> <note>B-flat.</note> because in both cases the <term>solmization</term> <note>The practice of singing music using syllables like ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la.</note> is *ut-mi-sol*, but in <term>B-mi</term> <note>B-natural.</note> the case for perfect intervals will only hold *mi-sol*. Thus, unless you look closely at these instructions, you will not be able to complete all the <term>gamuts</term> <note>original: "gama"; the full range of notes in the medieval musical system.</note> without great labor. Nor should you believe this has been placed here superfluously, if you consider the teachings of <term>Guido</term> <note>original: "guidonis"; referring to Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the monk who invented the staff and solmization.</note> to be necessary. 

For you who follow him, semitones frequently occur that are implied rather than written, and one must take care with these regarding perfect intervals unless they are supplied through these <term>conjunctions</term> <note>original: "cōiūctas"; the overlapping of hexachords to allow for chromatic changes like B-flat.</note>. Hence, you will see those who propose these scales mark them immediately. But while the gamut can be completed from the upper part in the manner previously described, you might ask: what about the lower part? Let this be your general rule: look to each place to fill its <term>octave</term>, and having established the formation described, you will find that if they are simple intervals—namely the unison, fifth, sixth, and octave—they will correspond in this way: for a sixth, a third; for an octave, a unison. You will always have these in reality. 

But for the <term>fourth</term> and <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note> and beyond, take note: if in the composite figure you have *fa* at the fifth, when proposing the following voice, you should take *sol*, because that *fa* is the fourth voice; and the same must be done for the other voices. For example, in the gamut of F, where <term>C-fa-ut</term> lacks an aspect of E, we will have <term>E-sol-fa-ut</term> as *ut-mi-fa-la*. For the sake of ornament, we will have <term>C-fa-ut</term> as *ut-mi-sol-la* to fill it out. 

But you must know that when *ut* is taken for *mi* and *fa*, it does not seem to be placed there, but that place will lack a fifth; as in the gamut of <term>B-natural</term> <insert>☐</insert>, where *mi-re-mi-sol* is composed, but <term>B-mi</term> alone will obtain *re-sol*, since the *fa* which was to be taken in the place of *mi* is not a fifth. However, <term>A-la-mi-re</term> is *ut-re-fa-la*, while <term>A-re</term> is *ut-mi-fa-la*; and so the gamut is *ut-re-mi-sol*, because it is customary for those performing to obtain *ut-mi-sol*. Thus you have a completed gamut. Just as this gamut is completed, so I leave the others for you to complete. 

It should also be noted that just as the place being proposed lacks a fifth when *fa* is taken for *mi*, so when the proposed case is perfect because *mi* is to be proposed, it will obtain it—as in the <term>B-natural gamut</term> on the fifth degree. Truly <term>C-sol-fa-ut</term> lacked *mi* as a fifth because a third *fa* could not be made; therefore it only obtained *re-sol*; whereas <term>C-fa-ut</term> claims to belong with *ut-re-fa-sol*. But if *la* happens to occupy the fifth place, the note being proposed will lack that same quantity, since beyond *la* no natural voice exists in this established teaching. Therefore, when you wish to propose other gamuts with the signs and prepared elements, it will happen also in the completion thus made, all of which are contained in the following figure.

**P**ater, it is clear from this that neither *fa* nor *ut* can be made against *re*, as in the third gamut and the seventh in <term>A-re</term>. Nor can *re* or *la* be made at the third of *fa*. This is evident from the operation of the third <term>hexachord</term> toward <term>F-fa-ut</term>, and so other voices against others in various hexachords cannot be made; I leave this for the diligent reader to investigate. Take heed, therefore, reader, how many things followed by the followers of Guido come among us. All of this can be defended for those who correctly understand our teaching; but they, having learned the doctrine of only one gamut, think they know many things, not even considering when two gamuts move from one to the other mutually.

It critiques musicians who only understand a single gamut and fail to grasp the complexities of implied semitones and overlapping hexachords.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the specific pitches of the Guidonian gamut to the mathematical foundations of mensural notation. It introduces the hierarchical system of Mode, Time, and Prolation, which governed the duration of musical notes in the late Middle Ages.</meta>

# First Treatise

## Third part, in which harmonic numbers are extensively discussed

We have already clearly shown through geometric demonstration—in the third treatise of the first part, in the chapter on notes—that a **maxima** <term>original: *maxima*; the longest note value in medieval music, literally "the greatest"</term> contains two **longs**, and a **long** <term>original: *longa*</term> contains two **breves** <term>original: *brevis*; literally "short," though it became the standard unit of time</term>. In this part, however, which is entirely concerned with numbers, it remains to be explained how the same note can be worth three or more.

Once the consideration of time is accepted—which is recognized by the beating of a pulse—we must know whether it happens to be doubled, tripled, or quadrupled, or even halved, divided into three parts, or divided into four. For the first consideration is called **Mode** <term>original: *modus*; the relationship determining how many *longs* are in a *maxima*, or *breves* in a *long*</term>. It is not named from "modulating" or "moving," as was said above, but we believe it is named from the "modification" or "computation" of times. 

According to Master Franco <note>Franco of Cologne (active mid-13th century), the theorist who codified "Franconian" notation, which made note shapes indicate specific durations.</note>, it is the conjunction of sound and time measured by long notes. This is nothing other than saying that Mode is the conjunction of the proportion that arises from long and short notes by measuring one measure, namely time itself.

**Prolation** <term>original: *prolatio*; the relationship determining how many *minims* are in a *semibreve*</term> comes from "proffering" <note>original: *proferendo*</note>, because when time is divided into parts, it is better uttered, as happens in the scansion of verses. Egidius de Murino <note>A 14th-century theorist associated with the *Ars Nova*, a style that introduced more complex rhythms.</note> says it is called "prolation" because time is divided into smaller parts so that it may be better uttered; for, as he says, it would be absurd if what can be pronounced could not be written.

Therefore, in this third part on numbers, we have three species: **Mode**, **Time**, and **Prolation**. Just as Mode can be doubled, so Prolation can be divided in the middle. When we join Modes together, we call it **Major Mode**. Conversely, when Prolation is cut, it is called **Major Prolation**.

If we place **Time** as the unit at the tip of the middle finger, **Minor Mode** corresponds on the index finger (resulting from an increase), and **Minor Prolation** is placed on the middle finger (resulting from division). Similarly, **Major Mode** will be correctly placed on the thumb, and **Major Prolation** on the pinky finger <note>This refers to a mnemonic "musical hand" where different joints and fingers represent different rhythmic levels, similar to how the "Guidonian Hand" was used for pitches.</note>.

Since we touched upon the similarity of the matter above, a simple note is called **Time** <term>original: *tempus*; the relationship determining how many *semibreves* are in a *breve*</term>. **Minor Mode** will therefore have the *long*. **Major [Mode]** indeed has the *maxima*, which is called the "double long" by many. **Minor Prolation** has the *semibreve*, which is also called "minor," and **Major [Prolation]** the *minim*. After these, of course, are placed the "points" <note>dots of notation used to add length, divide rhythms, or shift groupings</note> of augmentation, division, or reduction. After these are the diminished notes, namely the *semiminims* <note>original: *scitonia cursee*</note> and the *fusa* <note>original: *minaria fusea*</note>.

We have spoken more extensively about their names and the ways each note is shaped in the chapter on notes. Here, we shall briefly show their knowledge strictly and by way of their correlations. Knowledge of these proceeds from the **breve**, which note...

<summary>This page defines the hierarchical system of medieval mensural notation, explaining the mathematical relationships (Mode, Time, and Prolation) between note values from the Maxima down to the Fusa. It cites authorities like Franco of Cologne and Egidius de Murino while using a finger-counting mnemonic to illustrate these proportions.</summary>
<keywords>mensural notation, Mode, Time, Prolation, Franco of Cologne, Egidius de Murino, maxima, longa, brevis, semibreve, minim, semiminim, fusa, musical hand</keywords>

### Part Three

<meta>catchword from previous page: la</meta>

The **breve** <term>original: *brevis*; a square note value</term> is square like this [symbol]. But if it has a stroke on the right side, pulled either upward or
downward in this manner [symbol], it is made a **long** <term>original: *longa*</term>. If the body of the **long**
is doubled like this [symbol], it is called a **maxima** <term>original: *maxima*; literally "the greatest"</term>. It is so named
by the moderns, but by the ancients it was more rightly called a "double long," for this happens
by the augmentation of the **breve** itself. If it is cut diagonally from corner to corner
in this fashion [symbol], two **semibreves** are produced, which by the
ancients were called "minors." But if the **semibreve** has a stroke pulled upward or
downward [symbol], it is made a **minim** <term>original: *minima*; literally "the smallest"</term>. If the **minim** is
blackened [symbol], it is a **semiminim**. If it is hooked at the head [symbol], it is a **cursea**,
otherwise called **cursuta** or **crocea** <note>These are early names for the crotchet or quarter note.</note>. If it is made like this [symbol], it is a **minaria**. If in this
manner [symbol], it is called a **fusa** <term>original: *fusa*; equivalent to a modern eighth note or quaver</term>. In this way, all the notes are
recognized. Truly, we must distinguish **semibreves**, **breves**, and **longs** from other forms; we will
therefore give the method for recognizing the **long** and the **semibreve**, and thus the **breve** will be
known, which is figured in diverse and various ways in **ligatures** <term>original: *ligatura*; a chain of notes written as a single physical shape, common in medieval and Renaissance notation</term>.
A **ligature** of two or more notes having the first note higher than the one following it, and
lacking a stroke on the left side (whether upward or downward), is called a **long**.
But if any of these conditions are missing, it will not be a **long**. Truly, if it is
not square, whether it be higher or lower, if it lacks any stroke, it is always a **long**.
But if the last note has the contrary conditions, it shall be called a **long**.
Example: in a **ligature** of two or more notes in **arsis** or **thesis** <term>original: *arsis/thesis*; the upward and downward movement of a melody</term>, whether square
or not, or with the first one square and the others not, if it has an ascending stroke on the left side, the first
two notes (even if they are alone) are called **semibreves**. We will therefore
mark **longs** with the letter *l* and **semibreves** with the sign *s*, so that
when we later find similar **ligatures** in songs, we may easily recognize which are
**longs** and which are **semibreves**. The remaining notes, which are neither **semibreves** nor **longs**, are left to be **breves**.
Someone might doubt, and not without reason, why these notes are so
named. We say that because time is brief, the note of brief time is
called a **breve**. Whence it is said: *Since a thousand years in your eyes are as
yesterday’s day which has passed* <note>original: "quoniam mille anni ante oculos tuos tanquaz dies externa que preterijt"; a reference to Psalm 90:4, used here to poetically justify the naming of a "brief" time.</note>. The **long** is so named because it is greater than the **breve**.
The **semibreve** takes its name from the fact that a **breve** is cut into two **semibreves**.
When it is cut into three, they are called "minors."
The **maxima** and **minima** are named by comparison.
For if the **breve** is placed in the positive degree <note>The author is using the grammatical degrees of comparison (positive, comparative, superlative) to explain the hierarchy of note values.</note> with respect to its fractions, it is called "large"; if it is large, then the **long** must be "larger" [comparative],
and thus the **maxima** [superlative] follows as "the largest." But the same
**breve** is called "small" with respect to the **long**, and it is "smaller" [comparative] if it is...

<note>A series of mensural music notation symbols (quadrata, longa, maxima, semibrevis, minima, semiminima, fusa) are integrated into the Latin text to illustrate the definitions. This provides a clear visual pedagogical guide to 16th-century musical notation, showing the specific forms of notes discussed in the treatise.</note>

It also introduces the complex rules for reading ligatures and provides a grammatical/theological justification for the names of notes like the Long and Breve.</summary>

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues the discussion of musical notation and its mathematical foundations, moving from the visual shapes of notes to the numerical logic behind their values and the definition of mathematical perfection in music.</meta>

# First Treatise

is a **semibreve** <term>original: *semibrevis*; a note value half the length of a breve</term> and the **minim** <term>original: *minima*; literally "the smallest," the primary division of the semibreve</term> follows in the superlative. Moreover, the names of the fractions <note>original: *fractionum*; referring to the division of note values into smaller parts</note> were invented by the moderns. Therefore, they do not carry such great authority—for example, when a **semiminim** <term>original: *semiminima*</term> is named, it is because it is half a **minim**. Thus, "excursive" <note>original: *ercursiva*; likely a scribal variant for a fast-moving or "running" note</note> is derived from "running" <note>original: *cursu*</note> in a similar way. Elsewhere, it is called a "minimal area" <note>original: *area minima*</note> because it is smaller in value than those preceding it. Truly, some call the **fusa** <term>original: *fusa*; equivalent to a modern eighth note or quaver</term> such from the likeness of its shape, because it is made in the manner of a spindle <note>original: *fusi*</note>. Others, however, say it is named because singers circle around the song with "fuses" <note>The author suggests the name comes from the rapid, spinning motion of the voice during ornamentation</note>. But enough of these matters; we have spoken of them in the chapter on notes. Now, however, so that we may be able to comprehend the values of these small notes, we must know that there are three numbers which we use in this part: namely, the perfect, the imperfect, and the diminished.

A note is "perfect" when it is of the value of three of the following notes. It is "imperfect" when it contains two. It is "diminished" when it is placed for only one of the subsequent notes. We call the **ternary** <term>the number three</term> perfect, not merely persuasively or by comparison as **Johannes de Muris** <note>A highly influential 14th-century French music theorist</note> says: that the Trinity exists in divine matters and in the intellectual soul. For it is a defect in the disciplines of **mathematics** <term>original: *mathesis*; the study of mental or mathematical sciences</term> to prove anything through some [external] comparison. [He claims] that the third change, the dimension of the body of lines, is somewhat natural; yet we also reject those [arguments]. Because in the same way the **senary** <term>the number six</term> could be said to be prior and more perfect, because it is bounded by six points, and a point is prior in mathematical abstraction.

But the reason which we hand down is mathematical: namely, that the **ternary** is called a perfect number because it is equal to its parts—both the aliquot parts and the quotas—taken together. It therefore exceeds the **senary** in perfection. For a "quota" part is said to be any part contained within the number itself. Thus every **aliquot part** <term>a part that divides a number exactly without a remainder, such as 1 and 2 for the number 3</term> can be called a "quota" part, but not vice versa. Therefore, when something in other sciences must be proven, it is necessary to recur to mathematical demonstrations; because here it is demonstrated, whereas there [in other sciences] comparison suffices. Therefore, those who speak of perfection in music through comparison [to the Trinity] take away its [actual] perfection. So much for the perfect number. 

The **binary** <term>the number two</term> is called the "imperfect" number because it is distant from perfection by one unit. But it is "diminished" because of the **binary**, which should scarcely be called a number except because in this faculty [music] notes are broken [into fractions], and it will hold true in respect to two halves. Otherwise, however, in **arithmetic** <term>original: *arithmetica*</term> a triple classification of number is posited: namely, perfect, superfluous, and diminished, by comparison to their aliquot parts. Whence the **senary** <term>the number six</term>, whose aliquot parts are 1, 2, and 3—which joined together fulfill six and do not exceed it—is called perfect. 

§ 12. Whose parts 1, 2, 3, 4 together...

# Part Three

These are determined by reason: if they exceed their parts, it is considered a **diminished number** <term>original: *diminutus numerus*; in music theory and mathematics, a number whose proper divisors sum to less than the number itself</term>. For example, **10**: because its parts, namely 1, 2, and 5, do not reach the sum of the whole, but rather equal 8. Similarly, for the number **8**, whose parts—1, 2, and 4—total seven and do not exceed it. Therefore, every **inequality** <term>original: *inequalitas*; here referring to the ratio between a whole and its parts</term> is established either in greater or lesser terms. For the former excel in a sort of immoderate fullness of their own body by the numerosity of their parts; the latter, however, are as if oppressed and needy in poverty by a certain scarcity of their own nature, where the sum of the parts is less than they themselves are. But enough of these things for now; now let us gather the differences of the numbers:

## Chapter Two: In which [are described] the figures by which they distinguish numbers

**To** arrive at a true knowledge of these numbers, let me declare certain signs that were invented by the ancients from **geometric figures**. Then we shall add others that the moderns <note>original: *neoterici*; referring to contemporary or recent music theorists of the time</note> have put forward. A rectangle with three strokes was placed in this manner <insert>rectangle with 3 vertical lines</insert> for **perfect mode** <term>original: *modus perfectus*; a rhythmic division where a Large contains three Longs</term>; and with two strokes thus <insert>rectangle with 2 vertical lines</insert> for **imperfect mode**. Or, for **time with prolation** <term>rhythmic levels governing the relationship between the Breve, Semibreve, and Minim</term>, an **O** was placed with three dots in the middle, like this <insert>O with 3 dots</insert>; and for the imperfect, a **C** with two dots in the middle, like this <insert>C with 2 dots</insert>. If it were a **C** with three dots, like this <insert>C with 3 dots</insert>, the time was imperfect and the prolation perfect. But if a $\Phi$ <note>a circle with a vertical stroke</note> is placed with two dots, like this <insert>$\Phi$ with 2 dots</insert>, it was believed to be **perfect time and imperfect prolation**. Others, however, represented them with **Indian figures** <note>original: *indorum figuris*; referring to Hindu-Arabic numerals, which were becoming standard in mathematical and musical treatises</note> in this way:

->3 3 2 2<-
->3 2 3 2<-

The lower number denotes the **time** <term>original: *tempus*</term>, while the upper denotes the **prolation** <term>original: *prolatio*</term>. Now, however, they temper the geometric parts with the Indian figures; for they place one for the **mode** and **time**, and another for the **time** with **prolation**. Each of these is done in four ways. Mode with time is shown thus: **O3**, **C3**, **O2**, **C2**. But time with prolation is shown in this manner: **ʘ**, **O**, **Ꞓ**, **C**. <note>A diagram illustrates mensural notation symbols and vertically aligned numerical proportions used to show musical theory regarding time (tempus) and prolation (prolatio). These signs—circles, semi-circles, and dots—indicate whether a rhythm is divided into three parts (perfect) or two (imperfect).</note> This method of signing was discovered by this reasoning: because the **circle** is a perfect figure, it denotes a perfect species. But when only two parts of the circle are placed <note>referring to the semi-circle or the letter C</note>, they denote two parts of that species, with the third part being lost. Concerning this sign $\Phi$ or this one $\unicode{x1D10A}$ <note>The "cut C" symbol, still used in modern music to indicate "alla breve" or 2/2 time</note>, there is no ambiguity: the first is perfect and the second indeed imperfect. In the first four-part sign, the circle or semicircle shows the **mode**, while the 3 or 2 shows the **time**. Therefore, the subtle reader, through these things we have set down, will be able to find the perfection of either the mode or the time. In the second sign, it designates the time in this way: if a point is in the center...

# First Treatise

...let it be set forth to demonstrate the perfection of prolation <term>original: *prolatio*; the relationship between semibreves and minims</term> or, if not, its imperfection. This applies also to the semicircle, as is clear in the figures.

->Figure of mode with tempus      Figure of tempus with prolation<-

**W**e find these figures thus arranged for this reason: because we have recognized that they maintain the contrary passions of numbers among themselves. For in the first four-part sign <note>likely referring to a diagram showing the four principal mensurations</note>, the upper sign which stays to the left of the reader affirms the perfection of both tempus <term>original: *tempus*; the relationship between breves and semibreves</term> and mode; but in its own contrary, it denies the perfection of mode, which is more universal. The same is found in the subcontraries. The lower sign, however, which stays to the right of the reader, receives entirely contrary passions; and so the lower left is against the upper right. For that which is denied in one is affirmed in another, its contradictory, and conversely; or if both are denied in one, both are affirmed in the other, its contradictory. They can be called subalterns <note>This uses the terminology of the Aristotelian "Square of Opposition" to explain how different musical signs relate to one another logically.</note> because we see that the mode, which is more universal, is the same. The same must be said of the other four-part sign, as was clear in the figures.

**T**ruly, because in this part, whatever is sung through various fractions and different diminutions is reduced to a certain fixed integrity and determined measure <term>original: *mensura*; the steady beat or "tactus" used to coordinate singers</term>, it behooves us to know through the various signs in which notes we ought to keep the whole measures. For a measure, as we have said, is that time without interval comprised between the expansion <term>original: *diastole*</term> and contraction <term>original: *systole*</term> of a healthy <term>original: *eucrato*; from the Greek for "well-tempered" or "balanced"</term> body. From its unequal alteration arise unequal musical proportions, which we shall discuss shortly. Therefore, when a singer desires to sing correctly and in a measured way, let him move his foot, hand, or finger, touching some place while singing, in the likeness of that pulse.

**A**nd when he has sung by the first four-part sign, let him place that measure in the breves. For a long in these three [signs]—a circle, or a circle with a dot ○ ⊙ <note>original: *temporis modulis*; referring to signs of perfect tempus</note>—will be measured by three units of time; but in these—a semicircle or a semicircle with a dot C 𝇌—by two. A double long <term>original: *duplex longa*</term> in these ○ ⊙ would be worth six; but in these C 𝇌, it would be worth four. That same measure in these two ○ ⊙ cuts through the middle into only two, namely breves, and four minims. But in these C 𝇌, it divides equally into three, namely six breves and six minims, unless an unequal comparison is made with the tenor <note>The "tenor" was the foundational voice of a composition; changing the relationship between it and other voices created "proportions."</note>, because then there arises a certain condition of inequality about which we shall speak in the section on proportions. If, however, he has sung by the second four-part sign, let him place the little delay <term>original: *morula*</term> in the semibreves, and then a breve will be worth three measures in these ○, but only two in these C; and just as it was divided in the others...

<summary>The author explains the logical relationship between rhythmic signs using Aristotelian categories and defines the musical "measure" (tactus) as a pulse modeled on the healthy human heartbeat. It provides specific rules for how many beats different notes (Longs, Breves, etc.) receive under various mensuration signs.</summary>

<header>third part</header>

# Third Part

Just as [the breve] is divided into two or three <term>semibreves; the "half-short" note value</term>, so in these, two or three <term>minims; the "smallest" note value</term> are divided, according to whether the sign denotes perfection <note>original: *perfectionem*; a triple division</note> or imperfection <note>original: *imperfectionem*; a duple division</note>. Likewise, they may be divided into four or six <term>semiminims; a further division of the minim</term>. And this is what is most frequently observed. 

Other singers, however, because of the excessive diminution of the song, place the <term>measure; original: *mensura*; the steady beat or tactus</term> which used to be observed in the breve into the semibreve. And if the measure was held in the semibreve, they transfer it to the minim. Thus, for the most part, everyone now holds and writes in composition—for one reason or another—that the beat <note>original: *morula*; literally a "small delay," referring to the unit of time for the tactus</note> of the measure is held as a whole in the minim. 

And if a different sign is placed in the <term>tenor; the foundational voice of a polyphonic composition</term> than in the other voices—for example, if $\mathbb{C}$ <note>The sign for "Imperfect Tempus and Minor Prolation," similar to modern 4/4 time</note> is in the tenor, and in the others [a different sign]—a minim of the tenor is worth as much as the others; that is, it is worth a semibreve because it holds the whole beat. And if this sign 𝇊 <note>The OCR "o2" refers to *Tempus Perfectum Diminutum*, often indicated by a circle with a "2" or a vertical stroke</note> is placed in the others, it is worth as much as a breve. 

This rule is observed by **Ockeghem**, **Busnois**, **Du Fay**, and **Johannes de Monte**, and other famous men in this faculty. **Tinctoris**, however, being ignorant of this truth, sets down certain things that should not be brought to light. Truly, I shall say something about these in the section on proportions, lest the correct order be disturbed by an ignorant opinion. For Busnois and these great men are founded in antiquity; and just as quantity increases on one side by growing, so it decreases on the other by dividing. For if the ancients placed the measure in the breve, the long, and sometimes in the <term>maxima; the largest standard note value</term>, so we place it in the breve, the semibreve, and sometimes in the minim. But enough regarding measures; now I shall speak of the perfection of other species.

### Chapter Three, in which the signs of other species are discussed:

It remains, since we are to divide the numbers in all species from the imperfect in mode and prolation <term>original: *prolatio*; the relationship between semibreves and minims</term>, to declare by which greater figures they are distinguished. This we shall better achieve if we first briefly recall to memory the knowledge of **pauses** <note>original: *pausarum*; musical rests</note> which we set down in the first part. 

The knowledge of these, just as with the notes, begins with the **pause of tempus**, which is figured as a line filling the whole space between the lines [of the staff] in this way: <note>The manuscript would here show a vertical line spanning one space of the staff, representing a breve rest.</note> If it occupies two or three spaces between the lines, it is called a **long pause**. But if it encompasses four spaces in this manner: <note>The manuscript would show a thicker vertical block spanning four spaces.</note> it is a **maxima pause**, beyond which none is called greater. 

There is also the **general pause**, when everyone generally remains quiet when the song has already begun but is not yet finished. It is also called the **final pause** because it is always placed at the end of a song. 

If the breve pause is divided:
* The half which hangs from the upper line is the **semibreve**.
* The part which is raised from the lower line is called the **minim pause**.
* If it is twisted at the head in this way: <note>The manuscript would show a minim rest with a small hook or flag.</note> it is named the **semiminim pause**.

Regarding other fractions...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of mensural notation, specifically focusing on the rules for rests (pausae) and how they indicate the rhythmic "perfection" or "imperfection" of a piece. It references Johannes de Muris, a towering figure in 14th-century music theory mentioned on previous pages.</meta>

# First Treatise

Because of its extreme brevity, a rest is not found <note>The author argues that a note value can be so short that it is impossible for a singer to actually "rest" or take a breath during its duration.</note>. However, a certain Johannes de Muris—our musician and a master to the King of Spain and Castile—established rests for "running notes" <note>original: *cursee*; likely referring to *fusae* or rapid, "running" notes</note> written in this manner: <insert> [music symbol] </insert>. This was based on what occurs with the notes themselves: for if a **minim** <note>the "smallest" standard note value</note> has a hooked head, it becomes a **semiminim**, or "running" note, worth half a minim. If it is double-hooked, it becomes a **minarea** <note>a "diminished" or "smaller" note value</note>, which is half of a running note. Thus, he argued that a rest should be made similarly: if a minim rest is hooked at the top, it becomes a semiminim rest, and if double-hooked, a rest for the running note. However, we do not deny that this *can* be done, as we recognize the logic of the art; but we conclude that it *ought not* to be done. Since that note value is so small that it can barely be grasped in song, how could a singer's breath find rest in it? Therefore, we do not concede that this should be done, but rather propose that it should be avoided.

Once these rests are understood, we easily recognize **perfection** <note>triple division</note> and **imperfection** <note>duple division</note> in other categories. For whenever we see three **long rests** <note>original: *pausas longe*</note> placed together—either one following another, or two together, or all three separated in any song—we understand without a doubt, by the rules of the art, that this indicates the **major mode** <note>the highest level of rhythmic organization</note> and that three **longs** are equal to a **maxima**. If they are placed in pairs of two, we judge it to be imperfect. Furthermore, if a single long rest occupies three spaces [on the staff], we recognize it as **minor perfect**, and thus a **long** is worth three **breves**; but it is imperfect if it occupies only two spaces. Thus, when the minor is imperfect, the major can still be perfected, and vice versa, just as with other signs. 

The **perfection of time** <note>original: *perfectio temporis*; the level of the breve note</note> is also denoted by the rests of **breves**, according to both ourselves and the ancients. As Master Franco <note>referring to Franco of Cologne, the 13th-century father of mensural notation</note> says: if a rest of time <note>a breve rest</note> occupies a whole space, it denotes a whole **perfect time** <note>three units</note>. If it occupies two parts of a space, it demonstrates two parts of time. If it occupies only one part, it indicates a single part, showing one **minor** [unit]. The moderns, however, do not do this; instead, when they wish to suggest two-thirds of a measure, they place two **semibreve rests** side-by-side in this manner: <insert> [symbols] </insert>. Then, a **perfect time** can be correctly recognized, because we understand that the whole rest is worth a **breve** and the smaller ones are included within it. From this, it follows that two-thirds [of a breve] are positioned there, rather than the whole. For when the time is **imperfect**, a rest of time is worth only as much as the rests of two semibreves. Why, then, should two be placed like this <insert> [symbol] </insert> if one were sufficient? It is done in vain by many parts what can be done by fewer. In the same way, regarding **perfect prolation** <note>the division of the semibreve into three minims</note>: if two **minim rests** are found, it denotes perfect prolation, provided nothing else signifies otherwise. For when the prolation is imperfect, a breve rest is worth... <insert> [symbol] </insert> and two minims... thus all time is signified...

<summary>This page discusses the notation of rests, critiquing Johannes de Muris's invention of ultra-short rests and explaining how the physical size and placement of rests on the staff signal the rhythmic "mode" and "time" of a composition.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes de Muris, Franco of Cologne, music theory, mensural notation, rest, perfection, imperfection, semiminim, breve rest, major mode, minor mode, prolation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical notation from the previous page, moving from the rules for rests to the specific division of the smallest note values—the minim and semiminim. The author engages in a scholarly debate with his contemporary, Tristao de Silva, and cites the famous theorist Johannes de Muris to clarify the nature of "major prolation" (triple rhythm).</meta>

### Part Three

<margin>How perfection and imperfection are distinguished in prolation</margin>

How **perfection** <note>triple division</note> or **imperfection** <note>duple division</note> is distinguished in **major prolation** <note><term>major prolation</term>: A rhythmic system where a semibreve is divided into three minims.</note> is not clear to all singers, nor even to certain musicians like our friend Tristao de Silva. He holds the crude opinion of **Johannes de Muris** <note>original: *io. de muris*; a highly influential 14th-century French theorist</note>, affirming that "perfect prolation" is simply "major" and "imperfect" is "minor." We intend to disprove this by the authority of the ancients, the example of modern masters, and by mathematical demonstration.

First, the argument is as follows: **Egidius de Murino** <note>a 14th-century theorist known for his work on motets</note>, when treating the **minim** <note>original: *minima*; the basic short note value of the period</note>, says that it rightly ought to lose a third part <note>i.e., become "imperfect" or duple</note>. A dot, however, because it has nothing beneath it <note>meaning it is the smallest unit</note>, possesses only a half. If, therefore, a minim can lose a third part, I ask: what else is that third part but a **semiminim**? Therefore, three semiminims correctly fill a whole [minim]. It follows then that a minim is capable of being worth three semiminims.

This is evident in the example of **Johannes Urrede** <note>original: *io. olregan*; a Flemish composer active in the Spanish courts, famous for his setting of "L'homme armé"</note> in his *Mass: L'homme armé*. In this work, when he ought to have written two semiminims in place of one minim—as singers and the musician Tristao de Silva would have it—he instead uses **voided minims** <note>hollow note heads</note> with hooked stems in this manner: <note>The author likely points to a musical figure here</note> <unclear>which</unclear> we have said are called **running notes** <note>original: *curseas*; likely referring to *fusae* or eighth-notes</note>. From this, musicians observe that a minim is worth three semiminims. Furthermore, the authority of Egidius and the ancients clearly shows the opposite [of Tristao's view]. When he says: "there are other figures called **imperfect minims**, made in this way... and they have a greater effect than a semiminim because they are 'full' <note>filled-in or black</note>, but a lesser effect than if they had an upright head."

From these words, it is clear that we are doing the opposite—namely, substituting black for white. For when we use two white "running notes" for a minim, as Johannes Urrede himself does, it is because this white note is of greater value than that black one. If two running notes fill a minim, then three semiminims would be necessary [in triple time].

Mathematically, this is proven by what they themselves do: namely, that the **measure** <note>the beat</note> in perfect prolation is placed on the minim. For if a whole unit of time is contained in the minim, and "time" admits both perfection and imperfection, it follows that the minim itself can sometimes be divided into two and sometimes into three equal parts. If this were not so, then neither the **semibreve** nor the **breve** <note>larger note values</note> could be divided for the same reason.

In other ways, those two signs were divided into four parts by certain ancients; for example, combining **mood** <note>division of the long</note> with **time** <note>division of the breve</note> like this: <note>The text shows four arrangements of circles and half-circles: a circle within a circle, a half-circle within a circle, a circle within a half-circle, and a half-circle within a half-circle.</note> **○ ○**, **C ○**, **○ C**, **C C**. This method does not differ from the other, for that which is denoted by the triple part creates an 'O' <note>the circle, symbol of perfection</note> and what is understood by the double part is recognized by a 'C' <note>the half-circle, symbol of imperfection</note>. If these signs are arranged in the present figure, the result will be the same as in the previous one.

Others, however, such as my master **Johannes de Monte**, who was the first to initiate me into music...

The author uses mathematical logic and notation examples to refute the simpler definitions offered by his contemporary, Tristao de Silva.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding musical notation and mensuration. On the previous page, the author was refuting the views of Tristao de Silva and Johannes de Muris concerning the definition of "major" and "minor" prolation. This page focuses on the visual arrangement of signs and the philosophical relationship between musical "art" and "nature."</meta>

## First Treatise

...instilled with the principles [mentioned above], he denied that one sign should be placed at the side as we have arranged, but allowed one to be placed under the other in this manner:
-> o : c . o . c . <- 
so that geometric figures may be mixed with the characters of the "waves" <note>original: *undorum karatheribus*; likely referring to the wavy lines or specific flourishes in the notation</note>:
-> o . o . c : c : o . o . c . o . c . <-
Yet, this is done so that the upper sign holds the place of **3 . 3 . 2 . 2 .** of the former [system], based on the reasoning of other ancients. For if they arrange the "time" <note><term>Tempus</term>: The relationship between the breve and the semibreve.</note> with "prolation" <note><term>Prolation</term>: The relationship between the semibreve and the minim.</note> in this way:
-> **3 3 3 2** <-
Since 3 is nothing other than **o** <note>the circle, representing triple/perfect time</note> and 2 is nothing other than **c** <note>the semicircle, representing duple/imperfect time</note>, it seems reasonable that I should similarly treat "mode" <note><term>Mode</term>: The relationship between the long and the breve.</note> alongside "time."
Regarding the time and prolation that we mentioned should be signed as **o c .**, he said it should be placed thus because then prolation is correctly shown as "enclosed within time." In this way, the perfection or imperfection of each is recognized in a clearer manner. 

However, we told him that the first method was more subtle. For we have proven by geometric demonstration, founded on this principle, that each individual note naturally holds the value of the two notes following it. Therefore, when no contrary sign is found, the song must be sung according to its nature—that is, by the binary number <note>duple meter</note>. But when, through the "way of art" <note>original: *via artis*; referring to the rules of musical science</note>, we make it a ternary number <note>triple meter</note>, we indicate it with some specific sign. Thus, if a note was worth only two by nature, we make it worth three through art.

Therefore, when we display either of these signs: **o** or **c**, prolation is judged to be "imperfect" <note>duple</note> because its specific sign <note>the dot</note> is not there. But when the sign—that is, the dot—is placed in the middle of the circle or semicircle, the "perfection of the circle" is designated, denoting the perfection of prolation. 

**Master Robert the Englishman** <note>original: *Magister Robertus anglicus*; likely referring to a 14th-century theorist such as Robert de Handlo</note>, being ignorant of the properties of notes in geometry, said the opposite. That is, when the sign for "time" was not found, he judged the time to be perfect; he said that almost all songs lacking signs were badly composed. He himself, being ignorant of the doctrine, placed "art" before "nature." The contrary of this is manifest, because art imitates nature as much as it can; however, it is not said that nature imitates art. For we often see art go astray, but nature rarely or never.

<margin>How perfection and imperfection are distinguished through color</margin>

Furthermore, we discern the perfect from the imperfect by other white signs. For instance, if black notes appear among white notes in a song without a close "society" <note>original: *societate*; an alchemical or technical term meaning a grouping or relationship between notes of the same color</note>, it is a sign that the black note is placed so that it loses a whole part <note>i.e., it becomes "imperfect"</note>. Therefore, it should not have a third part unless it is worth three. Thus, when we find black notes without a close society, we say those notes are "perfected" <note>original: *f'rectez*; possibly *perfectas* or *recte*</note>, yet we say they are without a close society.

<summary>This page discusses the geometric and philosophical basis for musical signs, arguing that duple meter is "natural" while triple meter is an "artificial" construction indicated by signs. The author critiques Master Robert the Englishman for misunderstanding these principles and explains how note coloration (black vs. white) signals rhythmic changes.</summary>

<keywords>mensural notation, prolation, Robertus Anglicus, geometry in music, perfection vs. imperfection, art imitates nature, coloration, music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding musical notation and mensuration. On the previous page, the author was refuting the views of Tristao de Silva and Johannes de Muris concerning the definition of "major" and "minor" prolation. This page focuses on the visual arrangement of signs and the historical use of colored and void notes to indicate rhythm.</meta>

<header>Third Part</header>

We say they are "neighboring" notes <note>original: *propinqua*; referring to notes whose values are related or adjacent in the rhythmic hierarchy</note> because if some black notes follow them such that three are placed for two, they are not clearly distinguished; for this can happen in both numbers, namely the perfect and the imperfect. The earlier musicians and singers used to draw black notes where we use white, and red ones where we place whole notes <note>original: *integras*; referring to full, non-voided note heads</note>. They also used to place white notes—that is, void in the middle—as we do now, specifically when they did not have red color ready at hand, according to the opinion of Aegidius de Murino <note>A 14th-century music theorist known for his work on motets and notation</note>. However, Ugolino <note>Ugolino of Forlì (c. 1380–1452), a famous Italian theorist who proposed a complex system of rhythmic notation</note> did not examine these matters well: he placed black, red, and void notes within the same melody. It seemed to him that black notes should be perfect, red ones imperfect, and void ones diminished. But because we do not find this ever done by anyone else, we judge that it should be done by no one.

In another way, according to Master Franco <note>Franco of Cologne, an influential 13th-century theorist who established the system where note shapes signify duration</note>, the ancients discerned the perfect from the imperfect by placing a **b** over binary <note>duple</note> notes and a **3** over ternary <note>triple</note> notes. In this way, the difference in the value of the notes was clearly shown. But since notes of the imperfect mode were sung in perfect time, and notes of the perfect mode in imperfect time, the former were marked with **b** (having found the binary division), while the following were figured with **Ɑ** <note>a reversed C or "broken" circle sign</note>, denoting the opposite of the preceding ones.

Therefore, just as those err by defect who, without any sign, judge perfect species poorly by misforeseeing them, so also they err by excess who, when a perfection can be recognized by one sign, add another. For example, if a rest of a *longa* <note><term>Longa</term>: A long note value, usually twice or three times the length of a breve.</note> occupies three spaces, those who add the sign **o** err. Likewise, if two *semibreve* rests are found in this way, this **o** is superfluously placed, or this **Ɑ** if minimal rests are thus arranged—especially if both are found, since otherwise one might say it was placed to denote what was missing. So much for the figures thus far.

# Chapter Four, in which canons and inscriptions are subtly explained

I do not think it should be passed over in silence if an author wishes to write something underneath the song—an inscription—by which perfection, imperfection, or diminution can be recognized without any other sign. Or, if it has been signed otherwise, he may have issued a contrary rule through a <term>canon</term> <note>In this context, a "canon" is a verbal rule or instruction explaining how to perform a piece, rather than the musical form of a "round."</note> or inscription. It is called a "subscription" because it is always written under the <term>tenor</term> <note>The fundamental voice-part of a polyphonic composition, usually holding the melody in long notes.</note>. A canon, however, is a rule...

<summary>This page discusses historical methods of rhythmic notation, critiquing theorists like Ugolino of Forlì for their use of colored notes and explaining the evolution of signs for perfect (triple) and imperfect (duple) time.</summary>
<keywords>mensural notation, coloration, Aegidius de Murino, Ugolino of Forlì, Franco of Cologne, perfect time, imperfect time, canons, subscriptions, tenor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on musical notation and theoretical complexity. While the previous page dealt with the visual appearance of notes (colors and void shapes), this page shifts focus to the "canon"—not just as a musical form, but as a verbal rule or riddle that a composer provides to explain how the music should be performed. The author, Johannes Tinctoris, cites his own works alongside those of contemporary masters like Antoine Busnoys.</meta>

# First Treatise

...insinuating the will of the composer under a certain obscure ambiguity and in a riddle. This occurs in the mass *Se la face ay pale* <note>original: "si la face appale"; a famous mass by Guillaume Du Fay based on his own secular song</note> where the "sea" <note>original: *pontus*; likely referring to a specific voice part or a cryptic instruction in the tenor</note> increases threefold and twofold, and then remains silent. Sometimes the <term>canon</term> <note>A verbal rule or instruction that explains how a single written part is to be realized into multiple voices or performed in a specific way</note> teaches one to sing by the contrary: those beginning from the end finish at the beginning. This is what Busnoys did <note>Antoine Busnoys (c. 1430–1492), a leading composer of the Burgundian school known for his complex musical puzzles</note> with the instruction: "Where the Alpha is, there let the Omega be, and where the Omega is, let the end be." Indeed, we ourselves have placed something similar in "clandestine words" in a certain song, saying: in the voice which is called the *Contra*, it is sung against the rule. 

By a canon, even the location of notes is changed, such as when Busnoys wrote: "If you do not know, take *cacefaton* as *lichanos hypaton*." <note>The composer is using ancient Greek pitch terminology from the Boethian system to indicate a transposition or a specific pitch class</note> For the first note is written in G, which is the *lichanos meson*, yet the canon instructs to place it in D, which is the location of the *lichanos hypaton*. 

The manner of proceeding according to the canon is also changed, so that a voice is lowered exactly as much as it was supposed to be raised. Busnoys did this with the cryptic instruction: "by <term>antiphrasis</term> <note>a rhetorical device where a word is used in a sense opposite to its proper meaning</note>, let the leader be extinguished and enthroned" <note>original: "sbenorizar ipos dux epitrõzizar"; likely pseudo-Greek jargon used by Busnoys to signal a melodic inversion</note>. The meaning of this is: let that which was to be done above be done below, and vice versa. Similarly, in that passage: "let the scepters be present" <note>original: "reß assint ceptrza"; an obscure or corrupted phrase from a contemporary work</note> where there is <term>arsis</term> <note>the "lifting" or upbeat of a melody</note> and vice versa, where the voice is raised just as much as it seemed it ought to have been lowered.

Sometimes from one written voice, others arise in a <term>fuga</term> <note>A "flight" or canon where one voice follows the other exactly at a specific interval of time and pitch</note>, either in unison, or at the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>interval of a fourth</note>, the <term>diapente</term> <note>interval of a fifth</note>, or even the <term>diapason</term> <note>octave</note>. As we ourselves said in a certain verse of the *Magnificat*: "A fuga of two in unison, with the perfect number preserved." For there is only one written voice, and after six "little delays" <note>rhythmic units or rests</note> in the same sound, another follows it.

We also stated in the Mass that we composed on *L’homme armé* <note>"The Armed Man," the most famous secular tune used as a basis for masses in the Renaissance</note>: "Let Boethius be the leader; as we read in the *Music* <note>Referring to Boethius’s *De institutione musica*, the foundational music theory text of the Middle Ages</note>, let there be a harmonic mean, and let every voice preserve its own number." For the first voice precedes; another follows it in unison after eight rests, and it has no <term>proportio</term> <note>mathematical ratio</note> with any other to which a relation might be made. Another follows after six rests, beginning a fourth below; another, "looking on," begins a fifth below after four; and an octave below the first. And thus four rivers flow from a single source.

But in the motet *Tu lumen* <note>"You Light," a work by Tinctoris</note>, where we placed the music in the "perfection of minims," it is sung through three genera of melodies, which we composed while we were lecturing publicly at Bologna. We suggested that every vowel, denoted by syllables on the lines and spaces, should be worth six measures, just as if this **C** were the sign, since a rest of a *tempus* is placed at the beginning. Therefore, each individual syllable denotes one *tempus*. What these three genera of melodies are, we have stated in the first part of our treatise. For it is sung three times: the first time the second note is raised from the first by a <term>trihemitone</term> <note>a minor third</note>; the second time by a whole tone; and the third time by a semitone. Indeed, we have set forth many other canons in musical terms; for our ancestors were accustomed to do this in their teaching...

<summary>This page discusses the use of "canons" or verbal riddles used by composers like Busnoys and Tinctoris himself to direct singers on how to perform complex polyphony, including inversions, transpositions, and multi-voice fugues from a single written line.</summary>
<keywords>canon, fuga, Busnoys, Boethius, Se la face ay pale, L’homme armé, diatessaron, diapente, diapason, proportionality, musical riddles, Tinctoris</keywords>
