<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical canons and riddles. While the previous page introduced the concept of using cryptic instructions to guide a performer, this page explores how composers use biblical citations and classical references to create these "canons" (musical rules), often using puns on the names of the notes themselves.</meta>

# Third Part

...to demonstrate their intelligence: the unlearned, wishing to imitate them, set down **canons** <term>canon: a rule or instruction, often written as a riddle, that explains how a musical part is to be performed</term> supported only by their own imagination. I will place none of those here, so that they may be forgotten, for they are not steeped in doctrine. Others, however, adapt the style of proceeding from Holy Scripture, such as "let them sink into the deep like a stone." <note>original: "descendant in profundum quasi lapis"; a reference to Exodus 15:5. In music, "the deep" refers to the lowest register.</note> In music, "depth" is found when any voice has its octave below. But it is the opposite when it says, "We hung up our instruments." <note>original: "suspendimus organa nostra"; from Psalm 137:2. In this context, it suggests that the musical parts—the "instruments"—should remain silent or stop playing.</note> We ourselves have also attributed many rules to Holy Scripture, such as in the **canon** "re" <note>the musical note D</note> "rest," or "let them rest from their labors." <note>original: "re quiescant a laboribus suis"; a pun on the note "re" and Revelation 14:13. It instructs the singer to remain silent whenever the note "re" occurs.</note> By this, we insinuate that the note "re" should be silent while the others sing, though the value of the notes must still be computed in the timing of the pauses.

But when it is said, "if you hold with Lord **Agamemnon**, you will lose no hairs from your head," <note>A classic musical riddle. "Hairs" or "heads" of notes refer to their flags or stems; the rule instructs the performer not to diminish or shorten the note values.</note> it indicates that in the **paranete** and **nete** <term>paranete and nete: Greek terms used in medieval theory to describe specific high-pitched strings or notes in the "synemmenon" or conjunct tetrachord</term> of the **synemmenon**, those notes previously dismissed in the canon must be resumed. Thus, a note that was left out in "ut" <note>the note C</note> should follow the note placed in "ut"; and those that rested in "re" should reclaim their place after the labor of the "re" note, so that the words harmonize with the facts. And since we have from the authorities that the **paranete synemmenon** is the pitch "k" and the **nete** is "l," it follows that "their works follow them" <note>original: "opera illorum sequuntur illos"; from Revelation 14:13</note> by reclaiming the notes at the interval of a **fifth**. <term>diapente: the musical interval of a perfect fifth</term>

And so, when the notes in the second part are arranged thus, but "g, h" <note>specific pitch letters</note> ought to be sung as "f, k, g, l, l, h" after the addition of the "works" <note>the resumed notes</note> is made, the value of the preceding notes is fully resumed. Similarly, in the high voice where we said "let 'ut' rest until the desired one comes," we wanted to show that the note which was in "f," which is called "**ut**," should rest—that is, be silent—according to common speech, until the end has been reached.

But when we said in the following part, "and his days are like those of a **hireling**," <note>original: "et sicut mercenarii dies eius"; from Job 7:1</note> we want to show, as above, that what remained unfinished in the first part should be resumed in the second in the **paranete synemmenon**. As we did in the high-voice canon in the manner of a hireling, whose days are computed in the singing to the satisfaction of the others.

Furthermore, when it is said "do not remember," <note>original: "ne recordetis"; a pun on the note "re," meaning "do not sing the note 're'."</note> it is clearly shown that "**re**" is not to be placed on the string; we understand the same for "re" and "**mi**" <note>the notes D and E</note> when we place "neither remember." <note>original: "neque reminiscaris"; a pun on the syllables "re" and "mi."</note> Nevertheless, the moral values of the singing must be computed. When, however, we said at the penultimate point, "let them rest in peace," <note>original: "requiescant in pace"</note> we clearly demonstrate that "re" is to be placed neither in the melody nor in the pause, but having dismissed that beat, we transfer ourselves to another voice. Finally, when it says "**Amen**," we understand it is to be done in the same way. We have seen the canons of many other men, and we ourselves have set down very many. Yet, because specific knowledge of every single detail cannot be had—or if it is, some tiny part always remains confusing—let these things said about canons suffice for the sharpening and refining of wits.

<summary>This page explains the use of cryptic "canons" or verbal rules in musical composition, specifically how scriptural and classical puns (like "re quiescant") tell a singer which notes to skip or resume. The author emphasizes that these riddles serve to sharpen the intellectual skills of the musician.</summary>
<keywords>canon, musical riddles, notation, Agamemnon, pitch terminology, ut re mi, music theory, scriptural puns</keywords>

# Third Treatise

## Chapter One: A Subtle Disputation on the Three Kinds of Proportionality

Just as the various types of proportions are produced from the multiplication of numbers, so too is **proportionality** <term>proportionality: the relationship or comparison between two or more ratios</term> created by the combining of proportions. For a **proportion** <term>proportion: the ratio or relationship between two specific numbers</term> is the habit or relationship of two numbers to each other. Proportionality, however, is the relationship of two proportions to each other. Because we have investigated certain aspects of proportions up to this point, it remains for us to discuss certain matters regarding proportionality that are necessary for practitioners, in order to complete this first volume. 

This proportionality is divided into **continuous** and **separate**. It is **continuous** when the middle number is used twice in comparison to the outer numbers <note>the "extremes"</note>, such as in the numbers 4, 6, and 9. For we say that as four is to six, so six is to nine; because in both cases the ratio is **sesqualtera** <term>sesqualtera: a 3:2 ratio, where the larger number contains the smaller plus half of it again (e.g., 6 is 4 + 2). In music, this creates a perfect fifth.</term> 

When there is not one middle number but two, it is called **separate** or **discontinuous**, as in the numbers 4, 6, 8, and 12. The progression works in this way: as six is to four, so twelve is to eight. If we swap the terms, we conclude thus: therefore, as eight is to four, so twelve is to six. In the first progression, the ratio is **sesqualtera** <note>original: "sesqualtera"; 3:2 ratio</note> on both sides, but in the conclusion, it is **dupla** <note>original: "dupla"; 2:1 ratio, which creates the interval of an octave</note>.

If we wish to argue this way using musical voices, we must take four notes from the **monochord** <term>monochord: a scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used by medieval theorists to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</term> that relate to each other in this manner. Let these be *a, c, d, f*, through which we shall make the progression: as *a* is to *c*, so *d* is to *f*. By swapping the letters, we conclude that as *a* is to *d*, so *c* is to *f*. In the preceding ratio, there is the interval <note>original: "intercapedo"; the distance or space between two notes</note> of a **tritone** <term>tritone: an interval of three whole tones, famously known as the "devil in music" due to its perceived harshness</term>; but in the resulting ratio of both, the interval is a **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term>.

Multiple proportions can also be joined in this disjointed proportionality, as in the numbers 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, and 12. The progression then proceeds as: as 2 is to 3, so 4 is to 6, and 8 is to 12, because the **sesqualtera** ratio is preserved in all of them. By joining the smaller numbers separated from the larger ones, we conclude in this way: therefore, as 2, 3, and 4 relate among themselves, so do 6, 8, and 12 among themselves. 

And in musical voices, if we have the notes *c, d, e, f, g, h*, we argue that as *c* is to *f*, so *d* is to *g*, and *e* is to *h*; for in all of these, there is the consonance of a **diatessaron**. When the first three voices are joined and separated from the higher ones, we conclude: therefore, as *c, d, e* relate among themselves, so do *f, g, h* relate among themselves. In all of these, a **ditone** <note>original: "bis tonus"; literally "two tones," forming a major third</note> creates the difference. This proportionality is varied in many and diverse other ways, which will be discussed more broadly a little later—that is, in the second volume.

<summary>This page defines the mathematical distinction between proportion and proportionality, categorizing them into continuous and discontinuous types. The author demonstrates how these numerical ratios correspond to specific musical intervals like the perfect fourth, the tritone, and the major third.</summary>
<keywords>proportionality, continuous proportionality, discontinuous proportionality, sesqualtera, dupla, monochord, tritone, diatessaron, ditone, music theory, mathematical ratios</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of proportionality from the previous section. Having defined "continuous" and "discontinuous" proportionality, the author now categorizes continuous proportionality into three specific types: arithmetic, geometric, and harmonic. This serves as the concluding theoretical framework for the first volume of this practical treatise on music.</meta>

# Third Part

...Moreover, that which is continuous receives a threefold variation: for either the **excess** <note>the numerical difference or "distance" between numbers</note> of the numbers is considered, or the **proportion** <note>the ratio</note> is observed in both, or the comparison of the excesses and terms is made equal. For the first is called **arithmetic**, the second is **geometric**, and the third truly creates a sweet and delightful **harmony**. Therefore, having discussed these three, we shall bring an end to this first volume for practitioners.

When we find three continuous numbers, or numbers separated by an equal distance, we say that there is **arithmetic proportionality** between them, as in these numbers: **1, 2, 3**. For by the same quantity that the middle number exceeds the smaller, it is itself surpassed by the greater—namely, by one unit. Therefore, it is an arithmetic proportion because equality is observed in the difference <note>original: "excessus"</note> and not in the ratios. Similarly, in these numbers **2, 4, 6**, the number two creates the difference; and in these **3, 6, 9**, the number three; and so on according to this order. For as long as one follows such tracks, no error will lead them away from this likeness.

From this mean <term>mean: the middle value in a sequence of three numbers</term>, however, it is noted that in the smaller terms, greater ratios are found, while in the larger terms, smaller ratios are necessarily found. As in these: **4, 6, 8**. In the smaller terms (**4** and **6**), a **sesqualtera** <term>sesqualtera: a 3:2 ratio</term> is found; in the larger terms (**6** and **8**), a **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: a 4:3 ratio</term> is found.

Let the **geometric mean**, which follows this one, be explained. This alone, or most of all, can truly be called "proportionality," because the investigation focuses on the same ratios between terms, whether the numbers themselves are larger or smaller. In this type, an equal ratio is always maintained while the specific quantity of the numbers is ignored. It is, in fact, the contrary of the arithmetic mean. For example, in the numbers **1, 2, 4**, or in **6, 12, 24**, the ratio is **dupla** <term>dupla: a 2:1 ratio, or a double</term> in both cases. So also in the **triple** ratio: as in **1, 3, 9**, or in **2, 6, 18**; and in the **quadruple** and others similarly. In this proportionality, a property is noted: that in the larger or the smaller terms, the ratios are always equal.

### Chapter Two: In which the harmonic mean is distinguished

The **harmonic mean** is that which is established neither by the same differences nor by equal ratios. Instead, it is that in which the relationship of the largest number to the smallest is the same as the relationship between the difference of the largest and middle numbers to the difference of the middle and smallest numbers. 

For example, consider these terms: **3, 4, 6**. The number six exceeds four by its third part, which is two. Four, however, exceeds three by its fourth part, which is one. Therefore, in these numbers, neither is the same ratio found between the terms, nor are the same differences discovered; yet it is just as...

This section establishes the mathematical foundation for musical harmony, specifically identifying the harmonic mean as the source of "delightful harmony."</summary>

# Third Part

...the greatest term to the smallest. Thus, the difference between the greatest and the middle is compared to the difference between the middle and the last. This is evident because the difference between the middle and the smallest is one unit, and the difference between the middle and the greater makes a difference of two. Therefore, a **double proportion** <note>a 2:1 ratio</note> is found between them, which the greatest (the number six) holds to the smallest (the number three). 

The property of this **mean** <term>mean: the middle value in a sequence of three numbers</term> is the opposite of the arithmetic mean. For in the arithmetic, a greater ratio was found in the smaller terms, and a smaller ratio in the larger ones. In this one, however, it is the reverse: because a greater ratio is found in the larger terms, and a smaller relationship is found in the smaller numbers. 

Furthermore, the **arithmetic mean** is compared to that state <note>original: "republicae," referring to a system of government</note> which is ruled by a few. This is because a greater proportion is maintained in its smaller terms. **Geometric proportionality** is, in a certain way, "popular" <note>referring to a democracy</note>; for it is composed of an equal proportionality for all, whether in the larger or smaller terms. There is a certain equality of the mean among everyone, preserving an equal right in the proportions. 

The **musical mean** <note>also called the harmonic mean</note>, however, is said to belong to the best states <note>referring to an aristocracy or "ideal republic"</note>; this is because a greater proportionality is found in the larger terms. For this reason, these proportionalities are named as follows: one arithmetic, another geometric, and another **harmonic** <note>original: "harmonica"</note>. 

The reason is that the arithmetic arrangement divides quantities only by equal differences. The geometric, truly, joins terms by an equal ratio. But the harmonic relates to something else, for it possesses the observation of proportion not only in the terms, nor only in the differences, but in both together. For you will frequently find the proportions of the musical concords themselves—which they call **symphonies** <term>symphonies: from the Greek "symphonia," meaning sounds that harmonize together</term>—almost exclusively in this mean. 

For the symphony of the **fourth** <term>fourth: a musical interval spanning four notes of a scale; original: "diatessaron"</term> consists of a **4-to-3 ratio** <note>original: "epitrita"</note>, such as 4 to 3. The concord of the **fifth** <term>fifth: a musical interval spanning five notes; original: "diapente"</term> is in a **3-to-2 ratio** <note>original: "hemiolia"</note>, such as 6 to 4. And the concord of all, the **octave** <term>octave: the interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency; original: "diapason"</term>, consists of a **double ratio** <note>a 2:1 ratio</note>, such as 6 to 3. 

Therefore, in this mean, we find these three simple symphonies by comparing the terms. But if a comparison is made to the differences of the terms, we shall produce other symphonies that are not simple: for instance, if a relationship is made from the smallest term to the difference between the smallest and the middle, it will maintain a **triple proportion**, from which the concord of the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: "diapason et diapente"</note> will result. But if I make a relationship of the middle term to the difference between itself and the smallest, we shall find a **quadruple proportion**, which will resound as the **double octave** <note>original: "bis diapason"</note> concord. If these same numbers are multiplied by two...

...should be produced from the numbers 6, 8, 9, and 12, the consonances will remain. But between 8 and 9, another number can be inserted which, when compared to the outer terms, will preserve the same proportions that the number eight held, but in a contrary way. This is because the number nine maintains a **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: a perfect fourth, a 4:3 ratio</term> toward the lower parts and a **diapente** <term>diapente: a perfect fifth, a 3:2 ratio</term> toward the higher parts. The number eight, however, does the opposite. 

Between themselves, 8 and 9 maintain **sesquioctave** <note>original: "sesquioctauas," a 9:8 ratio</note> proportions, from which the species called a **tone** <note>a whole step</note> results. For this species is the "excess" of a fifth over a fourth. Now, if we multiply 9 and 8 by two, we will have 18 and 16, which we recognize as being in a **sesquioctave** proportion. Between these, as **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the primary authority on music theory in the Middle Ages</note> says, a middle number is placed—namely 17. When 17 is compared to the larger number (18), it produces a **minor semitone**, but compared to the smallest (16), it produces a **major semitone**. For the proportion of 17:16 is greater than the comparison of 18:17. 

However, since we are writing for **new singers** <note>student cantors or beginners</note>, it is permitted to discuss more clearly how we understand musical harmonies to arise from these proportions.

## Chapter Three, in which the primary divisions of the monochord are applied to numerical ratios:

<note>Small guide letter 'n' in the margin for an intended initial capital 'I' or 'N'. The text likely begins 'In'.</note>
**In** the first division of our regular **monochord** <term>monochord: a scientific and musical instrument with a single string and a movable bridge</term>, we said that Boethius subtly divided his regular monochord using numbers and measurements. However, for the sake of beginners, we have divided our own using continuous quantities and **common fractions** <note>original: "uulgaribus fractionibus," everyday measurements like halves or thirds</note>. We did this so that it would not be necessary for the student to have first mastered both arithmetic and geometry; for to do otherwise would be to fall into the error we have previously forbidden. 

We have said that none of those advanced sciences are necessary for grasping our teaching, provided the student is educated in the first rudiments. Because of this, we said the string should be divided in the middle, or the quantity should be doubled, tripled, or cut into three parts. These terms are very well known to common people. 

But now, because we have determined certain things regarding **discrete quantity**—that is, regarding numbers and the proportions of numbers which we recognized as being more necessary for singers—we shall demonstrate how those same common divisions we applied to the string consist of numerical ratios. It works in this way: 

Imagine, for example, a string that is twenty-four "fingers" in its total length, bounded by points **a** and **d**. Therefore, when we cut it in the middle and mark the section with the letter **b**, the segment **a-b** (twelve fingers) corresponds to the segment **a-d** (twenty-four fingers) in a **double proportion** <note>a 2:1 ratio, which creates the octave</note>. If, therefore, the string in its total length...

...you will strike [the string], and once the sound is carefully weighed, by placing your finger at point **b** and striking the string **b-q**, you will find that it resonates with the **diapason** <term>diapason: a perfect octave, a 2:1 ratio</term> consonance. Thus, the diapason is said to consist of a "double" property. 

When we divide the distance **b-a** in the middle, marking the letter **d** at the midpoint, the string **d-q** is established as being 18 <note>OCR reads "13", but 18 is required for the 4:3 ratio described here.</note> fingers long. When compared to the whole, this produces a **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: a 4:3 ratio, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-third of it</term> proportion. From this, it follows that the distance **a-d** is a **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: a perfect fourth</term> harmony. 

However, when we bisect the quantity **b-q** in the middle, marking the letter **p** at the center of the division, it is established that **p-q** is only 6 fingers; a number which, when multiplied by four, completes the total of 24. Therefore, its relationship is necessarily a **quadruple** <note>a 4:1 ratio</note> proportion. From this, it follows that **p**, in relation to the whole, resonates the melody of a **double diapason** <note>two octaves</note>.

And when the segment **b-p** is divided in the middle and the section is marked with the letter **l**, we clearly see that **l-q** is 9 fingers in length. If we compare this to the whole [24], we find a **duple-superbipartient** <term>duple-superbipartient: a 24:9 or 8:3 ratio</term> relationship, which preserves the consonance of the **diapason and diatessaron** <note>an octave plus a perfect fourth</note>; which, as **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), a philosopher whose works on music remained the standard textbook for over a thousand years</note> says, only **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), a Greco-Roman mathematician and music theorist</note> admits among the consonances. Therefore, when dividing the whole string into three parts and coming from letter **a** (or more truly, starting from **a**), we place the letter **m** at the mark; we clearly show that **m-q** is 8 fingers, which, when taken three times, measures the 24 in its entirety. Thus, maintaining a **triple** <note>3:1</note> proportion, it resonates the **diapente and diapason** <note>an octave plus a perfect fifth</note> in relation to the whole string.

# Third Part

...we find the **superbipartient** <term>superbipartient: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus two equal parts of that smaller number (e.g., 5:3 or 15:9)</term> proportion among them; for the number nine is exceeded by the number fifteen by six, which is exactly composed of two parts of nine <note>6 is two-thirds of 9</note>. This relationship creates **major sixths** <term>major sixths: also called "hexads," an interval spanning six scale degrees</term>. Thus, we have made all our divisions—both the common and the complex fractions—extremely easy.

**Guido** <term>Guido d'Arezzo: an 11th-century monk who revolutionized music notation and theory</term>, however, teaches how to divide his **monochord** <term>monochord: a one-stringed instrument used to measure the mathematical intervals of the scale</term> through nine steps. It is clearly evident to those observing it that this is laborious and tedious. This is because, as we have said, that genus consists of a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctave: a 9:8 ratio, which defines a whole tone</term> proportion. It is more difficult for someone to find an eighth part repeatedly than it is to take a half or a third part. Yet, through our division, just as through his, the **tone** <note>a whole step</note> is effectively found—such as between **d** and **e**, which the numbers 18 and 16 fulfill, or **l** and **m**, which are bounded by the span of the numbers 9 and 8. But enough of these matters for now; let us now discuss which **semitones** <term>semitones: the "half-steps" of the scale</term> of the monochord are to be sung, and which should be avoided, since we find one is larger and the other smaller.

### Chapter Nine: Which semitones should be sung or avoided

Since it has been said that a tone cannot be divided into two equal **semitones**, and I have shown that all the tonal spaces of the "perfect instrument" are divided into two semitones, it remains to be said which of them is to be sung and which avoided; and likewise for the other species that are varied by semitones. In this way, we shall orderly complete the supplement of this first volume, primarily refined for practical musicians...

<meta>catchword: ...ciamus.</meta>

In the first "imperfect" art—that is, in the first division of the monochord—there is only one semitone that must be avoided: that which is known to have been called the **apotome** <term>apotome: the larger "leftover" semitone in Pythagorean tuning (roughly 114 cents), which sounds slightly larger than a standard half-step</term> by Plato. Therefore, singers or instrumentalists should never make a transition from the note or string **b** <note>b-flat</note> to **♮** <note>b-natural</note>, nor vice-versa. For that semitone is not used in **symphony** <note>consonance or harmony</note>, as it does not fit in the **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: a perfect fourth</term>, nor in the **diapente** <term>diapente: a perfect fifth</term>, nor in the **diapason** <term>diapason: an octave</term>, nor in other imperfect species, whether they be dissonant or consonant in succession. It is never used. 

On the "perfect" monochord, however, there are many places where a transition in singing must be avoided. Therefore, by way of instruction, we shall assign these to the practical musicians. To the theorists, however, we shall demonstrate the truth with the firmest reasons in the following volume. Now, regarding the measured figure which was placed in the first part, second treatise, fifth chapter...

<summary>The author critiques Guido d'Arezzo's complex monochord division, proposing a simpler method for calculating intervals like the major sixth. He also introduces a practical rule for singers: avoid the "apotome" (the chromatic semitone between B-flat and B-natural) because it does not align with standard harmonic intervals.</summary>
<keywords>monochord division, Guido d'Arezzo, major sixth, sesquioctave, semitones, apotome, b-flat, b-natural, diatessaron, diapente, diapason</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical guide on the monochord's division, moving from the mathematical ratios of the major sixth to a practical application of which intervals and transitions between notes are considered musically "good" or "bad" for performance.</meta>

<note>Editorial Note: Significant water staining is visible on the bottom edge of this page. While the text remains legible, it suggests the manuscript suffered from dampness at some point in its history.</note>

# Third Treatise

...let us return to where we were. For the first note, or string, is **a**. The second is the first **b**—that is, the small **b mollis** <term>b mollis: literally "soft B" (♭), known today as B-flat</term> joined to it. This transition is good because it is seen to be separated by a **semitone** <term>semitone: the smallest interval in traditional music, or a half-step</term>, which is well-suited to harmonies <note>original: "symphonijs"</note>.

But a transition from the first **b** to the [natural] **b** does not occur, because that semitone—which is called an **apotome** <term>apotome: in Pythagorean tuning, the "leftover" semitone that is slightly larger than a standard musical semitone; it was considered un-singable and dissonant</term>—is not sung. From that [flat] **b** to **c** is a good semitone, but from **c** to the first **♮** <note>the "square" B, representing B-natural</note> is bad. From that note to **d** is good, and from **d** to the second **b** [flat] is likewise good; from that to **e** is bad. But from **e** to **f** is good, while from **f** to the second **♮** [natural] is bad. Yet from that note to **g** is good, and from **g** to the third **b** [flat] is likewise good. From the third **b** [flat] to [natural] **b** is bad; from [natural] **b** to **i** <note>likely a marker for a higher degree in the scale</note> or to **b** is good. From [natural] **b** to the degree **♮**, and so on in this manner throughout their octaves: the "bad" intervals correspond to bad ones, and the "good" ones to good ones.

The transition of good and bad **tones** <term>tonus: a whole step, consisting of two semitones</term>—which does not occur on the very next note, but always skips one in the middle of this instrument—is explained thus: from **a** to [natural] **b** is a good tone because it is composed of a major and minor semitone. Likewise, from the first joined **b mollis** [flat] to **c**. In the same way, from the letter **b** to the first **♮** [natural], from which the move to the second **b** [flat] is bad. The same applies from the second **♮** [natural] to the third **b** [flat], and similarly in their octaves.

As for other transitions, the tones that skip one note are always individually good. **Minor thirds** <term>triemtonia: an interval of three semitones</term>, which skip two notes, are everywhere good, unless an accidental order is mixed with another accidental—such as from the first **b** [flat] to the first **♮** [natural] and from the second **b** [flat] to the second **♮** [natural]; for this reason, the third **b** [flat] is not good with the **♮** [natural].

The **major third** <term>ditonus: an interval of two whole tones</term>, which is formed by skipping four notes, is everywhere good except from the letter **b** to the second **b ♮** [natural], or from the first **♮** [natural] to **f**, nor from **e** to the third **b** [flat], nor from the second **♮** [natural] to **b** or to **i**; and it is the same in their octaves.

The **fourth** <term>diatessaron: a musical interval spanning four scale degrees</term>, which on this instrument occurs at the sixth note, is always good in every voice except from the third **b** [flat] to the third **♮** [natural] and in its octaves.

The **fifth** <term>diapente: a musical interval spanning five scale degrees</term>, which occurs at the eighth note, is everywhere good except from the first **b quadrum** <term>b quadrum: the "square" B (♮), or B-natural</term> to the third **b** [flat], because a perfect fifth occurs at the fourth **b**.

Regarding the **minor sixth** <term>exade minori: a hexad or interval of six degrees</term>, which always occurs at the ninth string, we feel the same as we do about the minor third. For a string that was lacking a minor third <note>original: "triētonio cassa"</note> will also lack a minor sixth. The same applies to the major sixth and major third; for a string that lacked a major third <note>original: "ditono caruit"</note> is deprived of the major sixth. So too with the major or minor **seventh** <term>septima: an interval of seven degrees</term>, just as with the tone and semitone. Nor do the major or minor sevenths relate to their octaves in the same way as these do to their source.

Our contemporaries arrange these strings, or rather the "pulls" by which the strings are struck—which are commonly called **keys** <term>teclae: the Latinized form of 'tasti,' the keys of a keyboard instrument</term>—on the monochord in such a way that the keys of the natural order proceed in a straight line from the placed...

<summary>This page provides a detailed classification of musical intervals (semitones, thirds, fourths, fifths, and sixths) as either harmonious or dissonant based on their placement on the monochord. It concludes by describing the physical arrangement of the "teclae" or keyboard keys used to play these notes.</summary>
<keywords>monochord, music theory, semitone, apotome, b-flat, b-natural, keyboard keys, diatessaron, diapente, hexad, medieval music notation</keywords>

Part Three

# Part Three

...[sy]nemenon <term>synemenon: a group of four notes (tetrachord) used to include B-flat in the medieval scale</term>, as we showed measured in the first figure. However, the keys of the synemenon and the accidental rows are placed slightly raised above these, painted in a different color as is evident in the figure. But it must be noted—and noted most carefully—concerning that string placed between **b** and **g**: for some practitioners, providing for it less well, arrange it so that with these [notes] there is a good semitone, but with **g** it is a bad one. And so they make a fifth <term>diapente: the interval of a perfect fifth</term> resonate with the first square **b** <note>original: "b quadra", our B-natural</note>; this fifth is useless because it is harsh <note>original: "rasor"</note> and, to speak more truly, should never be played. 

Indeed, if someone says it is placed there so that when the tenor <term>tenor: the fundamental voice part in medieval polyphony</term> descends to **a** through **b**, the discant <term>discant: a melody sung above a plainchant</term> may have a major sixth in it tending toward the octave <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term> **b**, we reply that a variation must then be made in the tenor. That is, it should descend through the first soft **b** <note>original: "b mollis", our B-flat</note> which is joined to it, which will be a major sixth relative to **g**, and thus the transition will be not only good, but better, sweeter, and more pleasant. And if a middle voice is interposed, it has a major third in **b**, from which it will come to the fifth—namely **e**—preserving the rule set down above; it does not have this if it descends in another way, for the second **b** is not joined to this for the reason indicated.

And if anyone wished to say that the nature and conditions which **b** held ought to be reborn there and obtained by [the other] **b**, and since **d** is shown to have a semitone below it and above it, in the same way should **b**, we would respond saying the argument does not hold. For **g** possessed that quality, claiming for itself a total similarity above and below in the synemenon tetrachord <term>tetrachord: a group of four notes spanning a fourth</term>; but **b** does not, because it has two whole tones below it. Even if the first fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note> is extended in the disjunct [tetrachord], so too is the fourth <term>diatessaron: the interval of a perfect fourth</term>. 

Nevertheless, it does not have a fourth above the first fifth, but the second. Therefore, that string in the conjunct [tetrachord] is the *deuterus* <term>deuterus: the second of the four medieval modal categories, encompassing the Phrygian and Hypophrygian modes starting on E</term>, both authentic and plagal <term>authentic and plagal: the two forms of a musical mode, depending on whether the range stays above or moves below the final note</term>. Other practitioners say that if this were done, the fifth from **e** to square **b** would not have a middle third that is major to the lower note and minor to the upper, as we said in the second part, in the third treatise on composition. 

But this is no obstacle, because since that mode is of the stirring Phrygian <term>Phrygian: a musical mode often associated in the Renaissance with intensity, agitation, or excitement</term>, it does not matter if it lacks a middle third, or if the major third is placed toward the higher note and the minor toward the lower. Certain people, wishing to satisfy both sides, inserted another string between the third **b** and the [other] **b**, which they make to be distant from the third **b** by the space of a comma <term>comma: a very small interval resulting from the difference between two slightly different tunings of the same note</term>. This, however, is not praised, because it would then be another mixed genus and not a simple diatonic <term>diatonic: the standard seven-note scale system</term>. Tristan de Silva, our friend, used to say that another string should be interposed between **f** and the second **e**; and so by numbers he found...

<summary>This page critiques the placement of strings on the monochord, arguing against certain "harsh" intervals and explaining how to use B-flat (b mollis) to create sweeter harmonic transitions. It also discusses the Phrygian mode and mentions a contemporary theorist, Tristan de Silva.</summary>
<keywords>monochord, b mollis, b quadra, diapente, Phrygian mode, Tristan de Silva, tuning, semitones, musical theory</keywords>

## Part Three

...he testified; however, the early authors understood better. We shall explain the truth of this matter in the following volume using the firmest logic of numbers. Now, however, by summarizing the aforementioned things, let us bring an end to this work.

## Epilogue

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'V' begins this section, featuring intricate line-work and scroll motifs characteristic of late 15th-century printing.</note>

<margin>a</margin> **S**weep away <note>The Latin 'Verre' or 'Absterge' (suggested by the context of clouds) encourages the reader to clear their mind.</note> therefore, most pleasant reader, the sluggish clouds of former ignorance from your soul, and—irradiated by the clear brightness of this our little work—expel the pitch-black darkness from your eyes. Observe and look upon all this equipment of our music; cast your eyes around. Survey everything. Examine all the places. The more clearly you apply your mind, the more you will agree with us. And when, with God leading, you arrive at the knowledge of this most beautiful truth which we have taken care to bring forward for the common good: you will give thanks to God and preserve the memory of me. You will look with favor upon this little work and my labor. Indeed, it is necessary that you do so if you wish to escape the most shameful crime of ingratitude. If, however, you find anything discordant with reason and not in agreement with the truth, I leave room for correction and submit myself to examination. 

But I pray one thing: that you understand before you disapprove, and do not go headlong into judging. I do not fear a battle, if it is fought with reason present and acting as judge. Observe, I pray, with how much sweat we have composed this little book: refuting the most trivial opinions of certain musicians and singers, while approving those of others whom truth favored more; and by directing by the plumb-line <note>original: "ad perpendiculum," a metaphor for extreme precision, as used by a mason or architect</note> those things which seemed to have been handed down by others in an undigested and confused manner. In this work, if you do not see that flower of eloquence which is found in Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), the Roman senator whose works on music were the standard academic authorities for over a thousand years</note>, you will grant me pardon. For I have always been more studious of truth than of eloquence. And for us, for the most part in this little work, our discourse is directed toward the others who are proven to be mostly unskilled and unrefined. And sometimes it was necessary to interweave into the work their awkward sayings, woven with barbaric words, so that they might be rejected. Therefore, so that I may first collect a brief epitome <term>epitome: a summary or condensed version of a larger work</term> of all these sayings, by discussing the sounds brought forth successively and in order up to the whole harmony...

<summary>The author concludes the third part of his treatise and provides an epilogue, urging the reader to value his rigorous, reason-based approach to music theory over unrefined traditional practices. He acknowledges his preference for plain truth over the "eloquence" of classical authors like Boethius.</summary>
<keywords>Epilogue, musical logic, Boethius, pedagogical method, musical pedagogy, Franchinus Gaffurius, music theory correction</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page serves as the conclusion to Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja’s "Musica practica." It transitions from the technical mathematical proofs regarding the monochord into a final exhortation to the reader, followed by the formal publication details (colophon).</meta>

...the unique qualities of these modes, passing through world-music and human-music <note>Ramos refers here to the Boethian categories of *musica mundana* (the harmony of the universe and planets) and *musica humana* (the harmony between the human soul and body).</note>, we have shown to be both wondrous and diverse. And by running through two other types of melodies, which were scrutinized with great subtlety and purpose by the ancients, we have arrived at the definition of the very "symphonies" <note>In this context, *symphonia* refers to musical consonances or harmonious intervals.</note> used by both the ancients and the moderns <note>original: "neotericorum," referring to contemporary 15th-century composers.</note>. 

Then, having entered through the properties of numbers and having set aside the frivolous opinions of singers who are hesitant regarding mathematical proportions, we have approached the proportionalities upon which these harmonies lean as if upon foundations. We have subtly demonstrated that our <term>monochord</term> <note>A one-stringed instrument used by theorists to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note> is correctly divided through the use of numbers. 

But for anyone who desires to grasp the true and very easy path of this discipline—without the obscurity of complex arguments or the long, winding paths of proofs and disproofs—let them seek out our smaller book of music, which we have titled the *Introductory* or the *Isagogicon* <note>original: "isagogicon," a Greek-derived term for an introductory handbook.</note>. There, you will find the sum of this matter abundantly, briefly, and clearly. And when you wish to confirm with logic the things you see there, or to look more deeply into them, you shall return to this present work, which serves as the citadel and the rampart for that smaller book. From this work, you can select the appropriate tools to declare and defend those principles. 

Therefore, examine diligently the things thought out by others and those things recently discovered by me. For you will receive no small amount of utility and pleasure in reading these pages. And you shall give thanks to the immortal God, the giver of all good things, who—as I have said before—established all the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>The traditional curriculum of higher learning, of which music was a core component alongside arithmetic, geometry, and astronomy.</note> for the perfection and delight of humankind. To Him be glory through infinite ages of ages. Amen.

### Here ends the *Musica Practica* of Bartolomé Ramos de Pareja, a Spaniard
### from the province of Baetica and the City of Baeza in the diocese or suffragan
### of Jaén. Printed in the nourishing city of Bologna while he was lecturing there publicly.
### In the year of our Lord fourteen hundred and eighty-two,
### on the fourth day before the Ides of May. <note>This date, "quarto idus Maij," corresponds to May 12, 1482.</note>
